[trialAccount t18420613-1859]
1859. MARY DWYER was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. ELLIS and ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE BRISTOW . I keep the King's Head, Crown-street, Soho. On the 31st of May, the prisoner came with another woman at a quarter before twelve o'clock at night—I served her with a pint of beer, which came 2 d.—she gave me a bad shilling—I put it by itself, under the ledge, close by the machine—I gave her 10 d., and they left—on the 2nd of June the prisoner came again, about eight in the evening, and had a glass of gin—she gave me a bad shilling—I bent it—I went round to take hold of her—she said she did not know it was bad, that a gentleman gave it her is the square close by.
GEORGE MAUNDER ( police-constable F 57) I took the prisoner, and have two shillings which Bristow gave me.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. These shillings are both counterfeit, and both cast in one mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I had never been in his house before—my child died, and a woman pressed me to go, and have a pint of beer—I did not have gin—it was not me.
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1860]
1860. CATHERINE O'HARA was indicted for a misdemeanor.
RACHAEL WHITESIDE . I am the wife of John Whiteside, who keeps the Coopers' Arms, West-street, Smithfield. On the 4th of May, the prisoner came to my house with a woman named Davis—they had a pint of half-and-half, and paid for it in halfpence—then Davis called for some gin, and gave me half-a-crown—I gave her 2 s. 4 d. in change—I put the half-crown in the till—there were only a shilling and two sixpences in it—no other half-crown—in twenty minutes the prisoner and the other returned—they asked for some half-and-half, and a quartern of gin from the bottle—they gave me another half-crown—I took it, and said it would not do—I gave the prisoner in charge—as we were going to the station, I saw the prisoner put her hand into her pocket, and take something out—I seized her hand, and took from it four bad shillings, wrapped up—she said the had found them.
JOSEPH MARTIN . I am an inspector of the City police. On the 4th of May; I was at the station when the prisoner was brought in—I received four bad shillings from Whiteside—the prisoner had two good shillin ✗ four sixpences, and 9 1/4 d. in copper—I found one of the sixpences was bad.
STEPHEN SMITH ( City police-constable, NO. 254.) I took the prisoner, and received from Martin the four shillings he took from Whiteside.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These four shillings are all counterfeit, and this sixpence is also bad—the half-crowns were produced at the trial of Davis, the day before yesterday—they were both counterfeits.
Prisoner's Defence. I met this other person; she took me to the Cooper' s Arms; she asked me whether I would take some half-and-half; she gave a half-crown, and received the change; we came out; I walked as far as Smithfield; she asked me to come back, and get some gin; I said no, but she persuaded me; I went back; she called for a quartern of the best gin, and gave the landlady another half-crown; she said it was bad, and returned it to Davis; she found the first was a bad one, and
gave her into custody; then Davis put these things into ray hand; I did not know what they were.
STEPHEN SMITH re-examined. The prisoner did not say any thing when I took her—she did not say the other woman had put them into her hand.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1861]
1861. JOHN EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 1 half-crown, the money of John Hawkins and another, his masters.
JOHN HAWKINS . I am a partner in the firm of Hawkins and Payne, grocers, in Bethnal-green. The prisoner was our shopman for about three weeks—having lost some money, I marked three half-crowns, and one shilling—I gave them to Livermore—on Tuesday, the 24th of May, I called the prisoner in, and charged him with robbing me—he said "No, I have not"—an officer was there—we asked him if he would have any objection to our searching his boxes—he said no—we went into his room, and the officer asked him what money he had in his box—he said he did not know, he thought 30 s.—in searching his box I found ten half-crowns, and some other silver and copper—I picked out one half-crown that I had marked the night before, and given to Mr. Livermore—the other two half-crowns I had marked and given to Mr. Livermore, were in the till—the prisoner and my partner had access to the till.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you find the prisoner's box open? A. It was shut, but not locked; any one could have gone and seen anything that was in the box, and have had access to his bed-room—no one but the prisoner had had access to my till—I had a porter in my employ—he had not access to the till—the porter is not here—I have a housekeeper—she is not in the habit of going to the prisoner's box—I know that by her testimony—she is not here—I was not in the shop when Livermore came in—I do not know that the prisoner came from the country—I have inquired, and found he came from the country—I had a respectable character with him from Messrs. Booth and Ingleby,—the last situation he filled was in town—I never said the housekeeper was in the habit of going to the prisoner's box—that is my deliberate. answer—I never said anything to that effect, or that she used to go and look into his box—I have not seen the marked shilling since I gave it into Mr. Livermore's hand—my partner went to the till to see the shilling—he is not here.
COURT. Q. Were you present when the policeman took the prisoner? A. Yes—I heard the policeman speak to him on the subject of this half-crown—the prisoner said, "It is not your half-crown, Mr. Hawkins, I had it in my pocket on Sunday."
Cross-examined. Q. He used the words "in my pocket?" A. Yes,—I told the Magistrate that—my evidence was read over to me, and I signed it—I could not swear whether that part was read over to me—I deposed to that about the Sunday—I did not mention it to-day because I was not asked the question.
WILLIAM CHARLES LIVERMORE . I live in Paradise-row, Bethnal-green. The prosecutor gave me three marked half-crowns and a shilling—
I took them to his shop, in Bethnal Green-road, about eight o'clock on Tuesday morning, and bought something—I gave them to the prisoner—this is one of the half-crowns.
CHARLES SMITH ( police-sergeant K 34.) I went to the shop, and the prosecutor searched the prisoner's box—I found ten half-crowns, two shillings, two sixpences, and some halfpence—Mr. Hawkins said the half-crown was his—the prisoner said, "It is not yours, Mr. Hawkins, it is mine, I had it on Sunday."
MR. PHILLIPS called
JAMES EDWARDS . I am the prisoner's brother; he has come from Brighton. On hearing my brother was taken up I saw Mr. Hawkins—he told me the housekeeper used to go and look into my brother's box, and witnessed an increase of money—I swear that—he told me that the day after my brother was remanded from Worship-street while he was in Clerkenwell—I went to Mr. Hawkins's house, in Whitechapel—I questioned him as to what led to his suspicion, because I had reason to infer, from my brother going home on Sunday morning, with a suit of clothes which I had purchased, that that might be the reason of their suspicions and taking some undue advantage of him—I asked the prosecutor what reason he had for suspecting my brother—he said he was a man of very gay habits—I denied it—he said he was out all day on Sunday, and had a new suit of clothes on—I told him I had purchased it, and that seemed to relieve his mind, he had accused my brother of paying for these clothes out of the till—I said I could not see what inducement my brother had to do it; he had money of his own, a home to come to, and his clothes purchased.
COURT. Q. This was after he was remanded? A. Yes—he would have to come before the Magistrate again—the 'prosecutor would know that—the solicitor was not engaged then—I did not speak; the Magistrste seemed so fully satisfied, that my brother was committed without my making the statement—I was not examined, because I did not think it necessary.
COURT to CHARLES SMITH. Q. Did you draw the prisoner's attention to the marked half-crown? A. I had given him back the remainder of the half-crowns—I had this one in my hand—I said, "Is this your half-crown, Mr. Hawkins?"—he said, "Yes"—the prisoner said, "It is not yours, it is mine, I had it on Sunday"—I did not put the half-crown into his hand.
NOT GUILTY .
First Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18420613-1862]
1862. GEORGE OAKLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of April, 1 ham, value 6 s., the goods of Samuel Rogers.
HENRY ROGERS . I live with my brother Samuel, in Kingsland-road, Shoreditch. At half-past four o'clock on the 29th of April, my brother lost this ham—I had seen it safe about ten minutes past four—it was brought back about half-past four.
CHARLES SHOREY . I am shop-boy to Mr. Hemmings, in Kingsland-road. I saw the prisoner coming along the road, at past four that day, in a direction from the prosecutor's, with a ham under his apron—I informed a man that was passing, and he ran after the prisoner—he is not here.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Do you know Mr. Rogers's shop? A. Yes—I first saw the prisoner about two hundred yards from there—he was nearly opposite my master's shop.
JAMES WALL . On the 29th I was on duty near Shoreditch church, about half-past four o'clock in the afternoon, and saw the prisoner running with something in a black apron—he threw it away when he got about ten yards farther—it contained a ham—I took it back to Mr. Rogers's, and he claimed it.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you close by Shoreditch church? A. Yes—the prisoner was about four yards from me when I first saw him—I could not see the prosecutor's shop from where I was—when I was before the Magistrate on the Saturday I did not stop to be examined—I was examined on the Monday—the policeman did not come to me between the Saturday and the Monday—I went of my own accord—I was not threatened to be taken into custody about this ham.
GUILTY.* Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-1863]
1863. MARY ANN BROOKS and MARY ANN ELLIS were indieted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 9 yards of printed cotton, value 4 s. 6 l.; and 1 pair of stockings, value 9 d.; the goods of James Clover Tayers.
ELIZA TAYERS . I am the wife of James Clover Tayers, a draper, in High-street, Bromley. At half-past five o'clock, on the 19th of May, I was called by Dykes, and missed this cotton—I saw Brooks about three yards from my shop—I caught her, and asked if she had come into my shop, and taken a gown-piece—she said she did not—I gave her in charge, and went after Ellis, who had got about twelve yards—I took her back—I found Dykes had taken it back—it is mine—Brooks said, "I did not take it"—these stockings were taken from the window.
ELIZABETH DYKES . I am the prosecutor's servant. I went into the shop, and missed the cotton—I had not seen any one in the shop—Brooks was brought back by my mistress—before she returned I found this cotton just outside the door—I had seen it safe about ten minutes before—I had seen the two prisoners and another together, a little while before, sitting on the steps next door.
ELIZA WILES . I was coming down the street about half-past five o'clock—I saw Ellis take a pair of black stockings out of her bosom, and put them into a horse-trough, opposite Mrs. Reeves—I saw Mrs. Tayers come after her—Mrs. Reeves's little girl took the stockings out of the trough.
NOT GUILTY .

HANNAH HAWKINS . The prisoner was in my employ—I have not received any money from him, as from Miss Atkinson, since the 24th of February—he ought to have paid me every farthing,

[trialAccount t18420613-1865]
1865. GEORGE ALGAR, JOHN GWYNN , and ELLEN SMITH , were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 3 shillings and 2 sixpences, the monies of Benjamin Anning, from the person of Eliza Anning.
ELIZA ANNING . I am the wife of Benjamin Anning. On Thursday, the 19th of May, I landed from a vessel, about two o'clock, and went to Tower-hill—there were five men about there—I noticed one of them—Marshall came and told me something—I missed three shillings and two sixpences from my right hand'—I had seen them safe about five minutes before—I observed Gwynn looked at me very hard.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Were the guns firing? A. No—they had been firing.
JOHN ADAMS . I was on Tower-hill—I saw Algar and Gwynn together on Tower-hill—they joined two more, who have escaped—I saw the prosecutrix—the men followed her—they went up to her, and lifted up her gown—they saw me, and drew back—they went to her again, lifted her gown, took the money with their right hand; and one, who has escaped, took the money, and passed it to Gwynn—he and Algar ran off—I pursued, and they were taken—Smith and some other persons came up, and attempted to rescue Algar from the policeman—he was thrown down, and much hurt.
Cross-examined. Q. It was a man that is not here that lifted the pocket, and took something out? A. Yes—Algar was with Gwynn, and talking to him, and the other two crossed over to where these two men were—the others covered while the man picked the pocket—my attention was directed to the pocket.
Gwynn. Q. Where were you when you saw the money given to me? A. Close by.
WILLIAM MARSHALL . I am a smith. I was going across Tower-hill—I saw Algar and Gwynn, and two other men, ga behind the prosecutrix—one, who is not here, lifted the pocket, and turned it inside out, and then ran through the Nag's Head public-house—Smith was not by then—she was behind me, and I was close to them—Smith was about eight yards from the prosecutrix when the money was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not Algar talking to one of the other men? A. I did not see him—I did not observe them all along—my attention was fixed on the man who lifted the pocket.
Gwynn. Q. Did not you say that the map that lifted the pocket, put his bands into his breeches' pockets, and walked through the Nag's-head? A. I did not see him.
THOMAS ARNOLD . I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw Gwynn running—I took him—Smith immediately came up, and said, "That is not the, man, let him go"—she got in between us several times, and tried to rescue him—Gwynn kicked me several times very violently, and threw me down—he attempted to pass something to Smith, and I heard silver drop—I did not see it.
Cross-examined. Q. Algar gave you no trouble? A. No. Gwynn. Q. Did you not say it was not me threw you down? A. No.
WILLIAM LEECH ( police-constable H 18.) I heard the cry of, "Stop thief," and took Algar—two sixpences and one shilling were picked up—Smith said it was her money, and endeavoured all she could to prevent our taking the prisoners—I said if she would go to the station, if it was hers she should have it.
RICHARD ROBERTS . I saw Smith come up, and some money placed by
Gwynn in her hand—she attempted to put it to her bosom—the policeman gave her a push, and the money fell on the ground.
ALGAR*— GUILTY . Aged 19.
GWYNN*— GUILTY . Aged 21.
SMITH* *— GUILTY . Aged 19.
✗ Transported for Ten Years
[trialAccount t18420613-1866]
1866. MARIA WHELAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 3 yards of lace, value 8 d.; 1 seal, value 4 d.; 4 sovereigns, 2 half-crowns, 6 shillings, and 1 sixpence; the property of Henry Wylde, her master.
MARTHA LUCY WYLDE . I am the wife of Henry Wylde—the prisoner was in my service. In consequence of circumstances, I parted with her on the 11th of May; but before that I gave my daughter four sovereigns and 11 s. 6 d. in silver to give to the prisoner to pay the butcher's bill—she did not say any thing to me about the money—she gave me warning to leave at three o'clock that day—I did not ask her whether she had paid the money—she ought to have paid it about ten in the morning, when the butcher called.
LOUISA WYLDE . I received some money from my mother on the 11th of May—it was wrapped in a piece of paper—I gave it to the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST Q. Where was she when you gave it to her? A. In the kitchen—there was another servant in the the house, who was not in the kitchen—I told the prisoner it was the money to give the butcher—I saw her till about three in the afternoon—I do not remember' the butcher calling—I gave her the money about ten in the morning—I am sure I gave it her—I did not count it myself—I received from my mamma a piece of paper, which I presume contained the money, and took it to the prisoner directly.
BRYAN WARD . I am in the service of the butcher who supplied Mr. Wylde with meat—I had a bill against Mr. Wylde for meat—I do not know the amount of it—I delivered the bill—I did not receive any money for it from the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not call at the house at all for it? A. I do not know—I missed calling one day in that week—I do not know whether I called that day.
GEORGE STRINGFIELD . I am a butcher. I sent Mr. Wylde a bill of 4 l. 11 s. 6 d.—I did not receive the money.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you receive your money by the man? A. Yes—I live in Duke-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner never was in my shop.
MARY ANN SANDERS . I am servant to Mr. Wylde. Between eleven and twelve o'clock that day, the prisoner went out—she asked me if the butcher had been—I said yes—she said she was sorry for it, because she she had 4 l. 11 s. 6 d. for him—he had called while she was out.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) I took the prisoner—I told her it was for taking 4 l. 11 s. 6 d.—she said she had not received the money from her mistress—this is Mr. Comb's handwriting to this deposition—I do not recollect any thing about this statement—I was present the whole time—I do not recollect that the prisoner said any thing more than denying the charge—to the best of my recollection, she said she
bad not received the money of her mistress—( read)—The prisoner says, "I received no money to pay the gentleman."
Cross-examined. Q. When did you take her? A. On the 16th, Whit Monday, and in her box I found this seal and lace.
ELIZABETH WALLER . The prisoner came to our house that day, and said," I have five sovereigns that a friend of mine has given me."
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18420613-1867]
1867. DANIEL BRYANT and HENRY HUDSON were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 2 loaves of bread, value 1 s. 6 d. the goods of Thomas Bartlett Young; and that Bryant had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS BARTLETT YOUNG . I live in Drummond-street, Euston-square.
These two loaves of bread are mine—I was not at home at the time they were taken.
CHARLES GRINHAM ( police-constable S 221.) At half-past nine o'clock on the 26th of May, I was in Seymour-street—I saw the prisoners and another—I followed them to Drummond-street—they went to the prosecutor's—Bryant went in on his hands and knees, and brought out two loaves—the other two were outside—they then went to another street, and parted—I took Bryant—I am sure the prisoners are two of them.
ROBERT KNIGHT ( police-constable S 221.) I produce a certificate of Bryant's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( ready)—the prisoner is the person.

[trialAccount t18420613-1868]
1868. JAMES CROUCH was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of May, 9 oz✗. weight of bread, value 1 d., the goods of John Williams.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I am a labourer in the West India Docks, and live in St. George's-in-the-East At half-past seven o'clock, on the 31st of May, I had some bread and butter, in an old red handkerchief—I missed it at half-past ten—I have seen it since in the officer's hand—it is mine.
JOHN PASSMORE . I took the prisoner—he told me he had been working, the day before—I went with him at Mr. Bayley's, who, he said, had employed him—he said he had not been working there, and he knew nothing of him.
THOMAS PHIPPS . I found this bread and butter on the prisoner—I saw him take it off the check-box—he told me it belonged to himself—I do not think he had any of his own—it was in a red handkerchief—there was nine ounces of bread.
Prisoner's Defence. It was in a handkerchief, and I thought it was mine. I went into the Docks that morning, and had been at work there.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined One Month.

1869. GEORGE BECK was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 2 pairs of shoes, value 4 d.; 1 shoe, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 boot, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 last, value 6 d.; the goods of George Purden.
GEORGE PURDEN . I am a shoemaker, living in Stafford-street, Lisson-grove. When I went home, on the 19th of May, I found my window broken, and two pairs of shoes gone—these are them.
WILLIAM WARNER . I am a shoemaker, in Bell-street, Lisson-grove. About seven o'clock that evening, the prisoner came and asked if I would buy two pairs of shoes, that a lady had given him at Maida-hill—I said, "What do you want for them?"—he said, "They are worth 2 d. or 3 d." I gave him 2 d. for them.
Prisoner's Defence. Two other boys came to me, and said, "There is an old pair of shoes down this area, go and get them." I said, "I don't like;" they said they would give me a good hiding if I did not. I took them from outside the window-ledge in the area. I do not know anything about breaking the window.
GUILTY .— Confined Six Days, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18420613-1870]
1870. FRANCIS JACKSON was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of May, 1 pair of boots, value 2 s., the goods of Charles Edwin Kendall.
GEORGE IRVING . I am shopman to Charles Edwin Kendall. Between nine and ten o'clock, on the night of the 20th of May, the prisoner came in with two others—he sat himself down by the side of a customer, to try on some half-boots—during that time the customer laid a pair of half-boots down by the side of him, and while he was trying on another pair, the prisoner took these boots out—he went out up the hill—I followed him—some person called him—he came back, took the boots from under his arm, and put them into his hat—when he got opposite our shop I took him in—I took the hat off his head, and he took the boots out of his hat himself.
HENRY MARSH ( police-constable G 117.) I took the prisoner, and took the boots from his hand—I had a hard struggle to get them.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1871]
1871. MARGARET MAHONY was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; 4 1/2 yards of ribbon, value 6 d.; 1 pinafore, value 3 d.; and 1 collar value 3 d.; the goods of Joseph Moses, her master.
PRISCILLA MOSES . I am the wife of Joseph Moses, of Brill-row, Somers-town, the prisoner was my servant. On the 26th of May I missed these things—I saw them again with the policeman—they were found in her box.
ELIZABETH DISCOMB . The prisoner came to my house on Tuesday evening—I let her sleep at my place—she brought her box on the Friday morning—I saw the pinafore, the ribbon, and the collar in it—the prosecutrix identified them.
WILLIAM ELMER ( police-constable S 187.) I took the prisoner, and have the property.
Prisoner's Defence. I left on Thursday evening, and on the Sunday Moses came and broke my box open, in presence of a policeman; these things are my own; I bought the collar at a shop in Judd-street; the pinafore is my sister's. I took a shawl out of pawn, and this pinafore was round it; the petticoat I had made two years ago.
PRISCILLA MOSES re-examined. I made this pinafore myself—here is my name marked on it, and I have the fellow pinafore to it—the petticoat is mine, but she has put new strings to it.
ELIZABETH DISCOMB re-examined. I went with the prisoner to get the shall out of pledge the night before—there was a pinafore round it.
NOT GUILTY .

1872. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of May, 1 truck, value 3 l., the goods of Joseph Price.
THOMAS WHITE . I am porter to Joseph Price, and live in Long-lane, Bermondsey—this is my master's truck—it was safe on the 27th of May, and on the 30th it was gone—it had been placed in Mr. Crowder's yard—I saw it at the station on the 2nd of June.
WILLIAM HORSNELL ( police-constable G 172.) On the 28th of May, I saw the prisoner coming up Old-street with this truck—I asked how he one by it—he said a man in Covent-garden had employed him with it, to carry some greens to Liverpool-road—I asked where he was going to take it—he said to a man named Chapman—I took him to the station.
Prisoner's Defence. I was hired by a man in Covent-garden to take some greens to Liverpool-road, and in going back I was taken.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1873]
1873. THOMAS SHARP and GEORGE MASON were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 3 garden hand-glasses, value 12 s., the goods of Michael Martin.
JOHN FULLER MARTIN . I live on Thames Bank, Pimlico, with my father, Michael Martin, a gardener. On the morning of the 14th of May I missed three garden hand-glasses—this is the lead of them—they are my father's—I know it by the rings—each of the prisoners could carry one—there was 81bs. weight of lead—this is not half of it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When were these things safe? A. At seven o'clock on Friday, the 13th—I know this ring at the top. by its being our own make—they are not common to such frames—I swear to its being our lead.
HANNAH CORBETT . I am the wife of William Corbett, and live in Dorset-street, Vauxhall. About half-past eight o'clock in the evening of the 14th of May, the prisoners brought the lead to our shop—Sharp had it—I gave him 6 d. for it—Mason was within the door with him.
WILLIAM THOMPSON ( police-sergeant B 7.) The frames were broken in the lane, about one hundred yards from where they were taken—I there found the glass of the frame.
Sharp's Defence. We were coming by, and saw some bits of lead, and we sold them for 6 d.
SHARP*— GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Nine Months.
MASON*— GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Six Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

ELIZABETH DURR . I am the wife of John Durr—we live in St. Martin's-lane. The prisoner occupied a room in our house on the 18th of May—she left on the 19th, and the blanket was gone—I found her on the Saturday, at Westminster.
ANN JONES . The prisoner took me home to sleep with her on the 17th of May, and I remained with her on the Tuesday—on the Wednesday morning, about eight o'clock, she got up, and took the blanket off the bed, and promised to return, but she did not—I remained there on the Wednesday, and on Thursday I left the lodging—I am a dress-maker.

[trialAccount t18420613-1876]
1876. JOHN PRATT was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February, 1 coat, value 15 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 9 s.; 1 pair of gloves, value 1 s.; and 1 half-sovereign; the property of Robert Grant: 2 coats, value 2 l.; and 2 handkerchiefs, value 6 d.; the goods of Henry Thomas Brind.
ROBERT GRANT . I am a porter, and lodge in Castle-court, Finsbury—the prisoner lodged there. I had a box there containing the articles stated as my property—I saw it safe on the 10th of February, and on the 16th I found the box was broken open, and the things were gone—when I got up in the morning, on the 16th, I left the prisoner in bed, and Brind was there dressing.
HENRY THOMAS BRIND . I locked up my box, and left it there—I went out with Grant on the morning of the 16th, to go to work—I left two coats and two handkerchiefs in the box—I came back at nine o'clock, and went into the kitchen to breakfast—I had not been there long before I heard the prisoner come down, go out, and slam the door after him—I went up stairs, and found my box broken open, and these things gone, and Grant's too—when I went out in the morning I left the prisoner in bed, and my little brother dressing.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me come down? A. No, but there was nobody but you in the house.
JAMES RICHARD BRIND . I left the prisoner in bed when I was dressing that morning—I went out a few minutes after six o'clock—I left the room all right—I did not see the prisoner again that morning.
SARAH BRIND . The prisoner lodged in my house—no one lodged in that room but him and Grant and my sons—I heard the prisoner run down that morning—it must have been him, as there was no one else in the house—the door shuts and fastens inside—no one could have got in but by my letting them in—the prisoner had not paid for his lodging, and he gave no notice.
ELIZABETH TRUST . I live opposite Mrs. Brind. About nine o'clock that morning I saw a man come out of her house with a large bundle—the prisoner resembles him in appearance—I could not see his face—he walked on to Windmill-street, and then ran.
Prisoner's Defence, I lodged with her two years ago, and she could not say anything against my character. I went oat that morning to try to get work, and could not; I went into the country, and returned on the 11th of April; I got a month in the City Bridewell, and then I was taken on this charge, which I know nothing of.
GUILTY.* Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-1877]
1877. CHARLES TOOMBS was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 2 trowels, value 2 s., the goods of Benjamin Hawkins.
BENJAMIN HAWKINS . I keep a beer-shop in Pierpoint-row, Islington. On the 4th of May the prisoner was there with another man—I had two trowels in my ground—I missed them—these now produced are them—I can swear to one of them particularly, and I believe the other to be mine.
JOHN WHITHEY . I am a pawnbroker. The two trowels were pawned with me—I cannot tell by whom—these are the duplicates I gave for them.
HENRY CORNISH . I bought these two duplicates of the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the duplicates of a young man for four-pence; I was going down Islington for some rosewood about a fortnight afterwards; the prosecutor met me, and asked me about the trowels; I told him where the man lived that I bought the duplicates of, but he was gone away.
BENJAMIN HAWKINS re-examined. He took me to an empty house.
GUILTY.** Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420613-1878]
1878. THOMAS NEATE was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 1 pewter pot, value 4 d.; the goods of James Maskell.
JAMES BUCK . I am pot-boy, to James Maskell, who keeps the Adam and Eve, John-street, Clerkenwell. Between six and seven o'clock, in the evening, the prisoner was in the tap-room—he had a red handkerchief which looked as if something was in it—he first placed it on the table at which he was sitting, and had a pot of beer—there was a little disturbance in the parlour, but not in the tap-room—somebody present spoke to me—I went and sat opposite to the prisoner—I saw the handkerchief on the table—I took it up, and found a pint pot in it—I asked if it belonged to him, and if that was the trick he had been up to—he made no reply—he got up and went towards the door—as he got up I saw a half pint pot on the stool, which I had seen half an hour before on the table, opposite to where he was sitting—I took it up, and gave it to my mistress—the pot on the stool was open—the pot in the handkerchief was wrapped in paper, and then in the handkerchief—nobody was sitting by the prisoner—House detained him—the pint pot belonged to Mr. Knight, the landlord of a public-house—the other pot is James Maskell's
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. HOW many persons were in the house that night? A. I cannot say—there were two men fighting—they went into the tap-room after the fight was over—I have known House about three months—I have drank with him once or twice a week—I have heard of a Licensed Victuallers' Society—I have heard there is a reward for convicting pot-stealers—I have heard it is a guinea—I do not expect to get any thing for the conviction of the prisoner—I would not take it—I live by cleaning pots—this is the first time I ever was a witness—I and House have never talked about the reward given for pot-stealing—I swear that.
ROBERT HOUSE . I am a floor-cloth painter, living in Wynyatt-place. On Wednesday evening, the 18th of May, I was at the Adam and Eve with Buck—I saw the prisoner come into the tap-room—he seated himself at my right hand—he had something in a handkerchief—he put it on the table—there was nothing on it before he sat down—there was nothing on the stool then—he got up again, and there was a half-pint pot on the stool—I spoke to the potman about him—I saw Buck tike hold of the handkerchief, and open it—I saw a pint pot in it, covered with brown paper—the prisoner walked towards the door—I stopped and asked him if he recollected me—he made a reply, but I do not know what—I asked if he recollected Mr. Williams's of the Sir John Falstaff—the potman took the pot from the settle, and gave it to the landlady—the other pot was taken by the policeman—no one sat on the settle with the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not see to whom either of the pots were given? A. No—I swear that—I never swore one was given to me—I do not know that I swore it before the Magistrate—I swore to the truth—I mentioned Mr. Williams's name to the Magistrate—I have been in the public-house about six times—I do not know Buck any more than seeing him there—I never drank with him, to my knowledge—I never got a reward for giving evidence—I have heard of the Licensed Victuallers' Society—I believe they give a reward of a guinea—I have seen it posted up in several public-houses—there is not one in the house Buck lived in, to my knowledge—they would not give me a reward for getting a man acquitted—I have only seen Buck once since we were before the Magistrate—I have not been talking to him respecting the prisoner—I would not believe Buck when he swore he would not take a guinea—a witness gets 3 s. 6 d. a day here—I have never been a witness here before—there were about eight men in the tap-room—there was no row while I was there—the prisoner denied that the handkerchief was his, or the half-pint pot with i—he did it in presence of Buck.
COURT. Q. Have you had any promise of any reward if you convict!? A. No.
STEPHEN KNIGHT . I keep the Ironmongers' Arms, in St. Luke's. This pint pot is mine—I do not know when I lost it.
JAMES CHURCHYARD ( police-constable G 174.) I was called to the tap-room at' the Adam and Eve—I found the prisoner detained there by House—I have a pint pot which was given me by Mrs. Maskell, and the paper and the handkerchief—I took the small pot from the hand of House, I believe, but there were two or three struggling at the bar—the prisoner denied all knowledge of the pot or handkerchief—I found 2 s. 10 d. on him, and two or three medals.
JAMES MASKELL . I keep the Adam and Eve. This half-pint pot is mine.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1879]

No evidence. NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1880]
1880. WILLIAM KEYLER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of May, 1 half-crown, 12 shillings, and 11 sixpences, the monies of John Hanson, his master.
SARAH HANSON . I am single, and live with my brother, John Hanson, of the Coach and Horses, in High-street, Kensington. The prisoner was in his service—on the evening of the 12th of May I saw him at the bar—he said Mrs. Camp had sent him for change for a sovereign—she is a customer—I gave him the full change, in half-crowns, eleven sixpences, and the remainder in shillings—I did not get the sovereign, or see him after till he was in custody—he had no wages due to him.
MARTHA CAMP . I am the wife of John Camp, and live in Palace-place, Kensington. I deal with the prosecutor—I did not send the prisoner on the 12th of May to his master or mistress for change—I got no change for a sovereign.
RICHARD HILL ( police-constable T 113.) I apprehended the prisoner at Twickenham on the 27th of May—I was in plain clothes, but he knew me perfectly well—he saw me coming, and ran away—I overtook him—I told him what I took him for, and that all he said would be used in evidence against him—he said it was a drunken job, but he had been pressed to do it some time, and he was sorry for it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was rather intoxicated, and asked my mistress to give me change; I went to my brother's, and heard my master was going to have me taken up; I was afraid to go back.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1881]
1881. THQMAS HERBERT and JOHN GARRATT were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, 1 coat, value 1 l. 17 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; and 1 handkerchief, value 3 s.; the goods of Charles Thomas Edwards; to which
HERBERT pleaded GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
CHARLES THOMAS EDWARDS . I live in West-street, Somers-town, and work at a greengrocer's. Garrett is my landlord's son, and lived in the same house with me—on Wednesday night, the 25th of May, I was going to bed, about hall-past eleven o'clock—I observed the window of the room about three inches open, and the curtain broken down—I had a box under the bed in that room, in which I had a suit of clothes—I found it broken open, and missed from it a coat, a black silk handkerchief, and a pair of black cloth trowsers—I had seen them safe the day before, and I had locked the box—the duplicate of these things was sent to my landlord, in a public-house, by a little boy—it was shown to me that night, and the next morning the landlady gave it me—I gave it to the policeman, who went to the pawnbroker's—the articles produced are mine, and are what I had seen safe in my box the evening before.
WILLIAM HUNT ( police-constable E 67.) The prisoner Herbert was given into my custody, and I received this duplicate from the prosecutor.
THOMAS FRYER ( police-sergeant P 15.) I took Garrett into custody in Fizroy-market—I told him it was for felony—he said, "You go and take the other one"—I asked what he meant—he said, "I will go and show you where he lives"—I said, "If you make any communication to me, I shall have to state it"—as he got opposite No. 21. Warren-street, where Herbert lived,. he pointed down the area and said, "There"—he afterwards said, "I did not take the property, though I was present at the time the box was broken open by Herbert," and he said they got in at the back. THOMAS BLACKBURN. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Skinner-street,
Somer's-town. This coat, trowsers, and handkerchief were pawned with me by Herbert.
GARRATT— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1882]
1882. JOHN ROUSE was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of May, 20 feet of lead pipe, value l., the goods of Islipp Odell; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ISLIPP ODELL . I live at Kentish-town. I had a pump at Hackney, and about twenty feet of pipe attached to it—I went to the premises where it was, and missed the suction-pipe, on the 9th of May—I had seen it a day or two previously—I have seen some pipe produced very similar to mine.
THOMAS LARTER ( police-constable N 116.) I know the yard belonging to Mr. Odell, at Hackney—it is on a part of my beat—I know the pump there—I saw it in the yard before the pipe was missed from it—here is a piece of the pipe, with a wooden plug in it—I can speak to this, as I had noticed it in the yard for eight weeks previously—I had taken notice of it every time I went round my beat—the premises are open to the road—here is a rivet to keep the wood in, which I had noticed—on Monday, the 9th of May, I missed this pipe, and in the evening of the next day I was at the station, at Kingsland, when the prisoner was brought in by another officer, who had the piece of pipe which had the piece of wood in it, and I knew it directly.
Prisoner. I do not see how he can swear to this, when every pump has got the same. Witness. But this is missing from the pump, and I know it by the rivet that is through it.
JAMES BRENNAN ( police-constable N 69.) On Tuesday evening, the 10th of May, I received information, and went to Queen's-row, Dalston, near to where it had been said the pipe was concealed—I got there about half-past eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner and another man in company—I watched them for half an hour, and saw them go over the palings into 8 field in Queen's-row—they went into a pit in the field, and stopped a few minutes—they then went in the direction of the canal—I followed and overtook them—I took hold of one in each hand by their collars—I said I was a policeman, and they might consider themselves in my custody—the prisoner put his hand into his right pocket, and drew out something and struck me on the head and shoulders; it was a piece of lead, I suppose; I felt it very heavy, and was obliged to let the other man go; but I succeeded in throwing the prisoner down, and while he was down I took from him these five pieces of lead pipe, and this one piece I wrenched out of his band—his companion then took this other piece from his pocket and struck me on the back with it—I called "Police" and "Murder"—some people came to my assistance, and I got the prisoner to the station—directly I got there Larter knew this piece of lead as belonging to the pump at Hackney——they tore my clothes, broke my hat, and I have Jumps on my head now.
JOHN HUGH HENDIE . I am a plumber. I put up the pump for Mr. Odell—I have examined these pieces of pipe, and, to the best of my belief they are pieces of the pipe I put to that pump, but I could not swear to them—I saw the prisoner on Monday morning, the 9th, loitering about the public-house near where the pump was.
RICHARD HAWKES ( police-sergeant N 6.) On Tuesday morning, the
10th of May, I found three pieces of this pipe in a dry ditch, about half a mile from the Middlesex Arms—I have compared them with the other process produced, and they all correspond.
Primer's Defence. I was coming across the fields with another man, and saw this in the fields; we took a little of it; the policeman came and struck me with a stick; I took one piece out and did strike him; he got me down, took off my handkerchief and tied my legs; he took off my braces and tied my hands..
THOMAS HATTON ( police-constable K 272.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—(read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-1883]
1883. MARIA SNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May, 1 cape, value 1 l.; 1 collar, value 15 s.; 3 yards of satinet, value 15 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 9 d.; 8 yards of ribbon, value 3 s. 6 d.; 10 artificial flowers, value 1 l. 7 s.; and 1 yard of silk, value 3 s.; the goods of Jane Hains, her mistress.
JANE HAINS . I am single. I am a milliner, and live in New Bond-street—the prisoner was in my employ—I sent her away—I received information, and gave her into custody on the last day of May—I afterwards went to the station and saw her—she told me if I would go with her to her house she would give me my property—I went with her and the inspector to Hastings's-street—she produced several boxes, and showed me a muslin cape, a lace collar, nine or ten flowers, and some other things, which are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you searched her boxes at her mother's before that? A. Yes—on the 5th of April, I think it was—I know Mrs. Chafford, a milliner, who lives near me—I believe the prisoner went from my service to live with her, but I know nothing of that lady—the prisoner left me because I suspected her and sent her away.
I am a police-inspector. I took the prisoner.
Cross—examined. Q. Did you say you were sorry for this girl? A. Yes—I do not recollect that I told her it would be better to tell the truth—I told her she was my prisoner on suspicion of stealing some silk—she laid, "I stole nothing"—at the station she sent for me, and said if I would go with her she would give up the things—I did not tell her it would be better for her.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined One Week.
[trialAccount t18420613-1884]
1884. ALFRED TYRRELL was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 23 account-books, value 16 s.; and 9 unfinished account-books, value 1l.; the goods of Edward Haskin Wills, his master.
EDWARD HASKIN WILLS . I am a book-binder, and live in Brook-street, Ratcliffe—the prisoner was my apprentice. On the 26th of May I vent out about nine o'clock in the morning, leaving him in the house—I came back about twelve, and he was absent—I had suspicion, went to my work-shop, and missed two dozen long pocket memorandum books, which were finished—I gave information at the station, and saw the prisoner the same night in custody—these thirty-two books now produced,
which are finished, are my property—he had been working at them—I missed these that are unfinished, on the 2nd of June, after the prisoner was committed—I can swear to them.
FREDERICK ESQUILAUT . On the morning of the 26th of May, the prisoner came to my house in Oxford-street, and brought these finished books to me—he said, "Is this Mr. Esquilaut's?"—I said, "Yes"—he said he had been recommended by Mr. Hunter, in Holborn, to know if I would buy them—I hesitated, as they were not so good as I was accustomed to buy, but I bought them of him.
GEORGE JOSEPH BROWN ( police-constable K 164.) The prisoner was given into my charge on the 26th of May—I received these books from Esquilaut on the 27th.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.

[trialAccount t18420613-1890]
1890. WILLIAM SHOREY was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 54lbs. weight of lead-pipe, value 2 s. 6 d.; 2 bill-hooks, value 2 s.; 1 hammer, value 6 s.; 2 bags, value 6 s.; and 1/2 a pint of peas, value 6 d.; the goods of John Copeland.
THOMAS HICKMAN ( police-constable D 204.) Between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning, on the 28th of March, I was on duty at Maida-hill, Padding ton—I saw the prisoner with another man, each carrying a sack, which appeared to be weighty—they were making towards Lisson-grove—they saw me, and put the sacks down on a resting-place, and sat on them—I passed them on the opposite side—I saw each of them take up their sacks and go on the other side—I turned back and watched them for five minutes—I gave an alarm, and they stopped at the corner of the building—they then ran away with their sacks—several persons pursued with me—they went to a hedge—they had their bags when they went to the hedge—when they came back from it, they had not got them—the bags were brought from the hedge before I got up to them, by the parties who pursued—they contained the leaden-pipe, a hammer, and a bill-hook—neither of them were taken at the time—both made their escape—the prisoner was taken by another officer—I am sure he is one of the men—I knew him before.
EDWARD FORREST . I am bailiff to Mr. John Copeland, of Sudbury, near Harrow. On Sunday morning, the 27th of March, I went to the tool-house, and missed two bill-hooks, a hammer, two sacks, a quantity of lead, and some peas—I had put the bill-hooks and the hammer in there on the Saturday morning—I had put this piece of lead in the tool-house three days previous—it had a tap in it—I took the tap out, and have it here—this hammer and the bill-hooks are my master's—his dwelling-house was attempted to be broken open the same night.
GEORGE LACEY ( police-constable D 283.) On Saturday night, the 26th of March, I was on duty in the Harrow-road, about two miles from Sudbury—I saw the prisoner and another man—I stopped them, and asked where they were going—they said, to a brother in Harrow—they went by me—I was returning about three o'clock in the morning of the 27th, and heard some talking—I stopped and listened—I saw the prisoner and the other man—I went up to them—the other man, who was sitting down. said, "It is a very cold night"—I said, "Yes, it is; have you been sitting here long?"—he said, "No, only just sat down to tie my shoe up"—he got up, and took up a canvas bag that he had with him—it contained peas—I allowed them to go on.
Prisoner. I know nothing about it; I was at work on the Eastern Counties' Railway when this was lost.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.

1891. JOHN KNOWLES was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 handkerchief, value 7 d., the goods of Henry George Mumbray, from his person.
HENRY GEORGE MUMBRAY . I am a bookbinder, and live in Brunswick-place, City-road. About four o'clock in the afternoon of Wednesday, the 1st of June, I was in St. James's-park—the policeman spoke to me—I felt in my coat-pocket, and missed my handkerchief—I told the officer the handkerchief was gone—he left me, and returned in about a minute,
with the prisoner—I saw the policeman take my handkerchief from the prisoner's breast—this is it—I had had it in my possession just before.
JOHN PHILLIPS ( police-constable D 132.) I was in the Park on the 1st of June—I saw some persons standing round the Palace gates, and the prisoner also—I saw the prisoner put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, take out his handkerchief, and put it into his breast—he went away—I spoke to the prosecutor, went after the prisoner, and took the handkerchief from him.

1894. JAMES WEST was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May 3 trusses of hay, value 6 s;. the goods of John Hargood, his master; and WILLIAM ALLEN , for receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
JOHN HARGOOD . I am a tailor, and live at Stanmore. On the 24th of May I directed West to bind three quarters of a load of hay—two cuts of it were to go to a customer, and the rest to go to my lawn—it was a little field of three acres, which I buy the crop of—he bound three quarters and three trusses—he accounted for three quarters only—the three trusses were concealed in a loft just by—Allen lodged in Mr. Dorey's loft—I had not sold any hay to Dorey—West only charged for binding the three quarters, not for the three trusses.
GEORGE SHORT . I am in Mr. Hargood's service. On the 24th of May I was sent to a Rick belonging to him, to take half a load down to Mr. Law ford—West sent me with three trusses to Mr. Dorey's, to the shed, and he told me to tell Allen to take them in, and put them into the loft—I was not aware that it was wrong at that time—I saw Allen put one into the loft, and when I went back again the other two were gone—I took half a load to Mrs. Law ford, six trusses of hay to my master's stable, and three trusses of straw—my master gave West 1 s. for binding the hay—West put the hay on my back to take to the shed—he said he should send a truss or two to Mr. Dorey's; he should like to make a good half-day's work; he did not want to go on the common again.
West. Q. What did you tell me when you first came to me with the
pot of beer? A. I told you my master said I mast carry tome hay to the shed if it rained, but it did not rain.
COURT. Q. Did you carry it in consequence of your master saying it was to go there if it rained, or of West sending it to Dorey's on your back? A. His putting it on my back.
WILLIAM DOREY . I am a coachman, living at Great Stan more—I occupy a stable and shed there—Allen was my stable-man. On the 24th of May I got information that there were some trusses of hay in my loft—I had not ordered it—I asked Allen next morning where he got the three trasses of hay he had in the loft—he said he put them from the shed into the loft—I said it was my belief it was Mr. Hargood's, as I understood he had some bound the previous day, and I had sent for him—I ordered Allen to take it back to Mr. Hargood's rick—I had not bought any of Mr. Hargood, or desired Allen to purchase any for me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you understand from Mrs. Dorey that the hay was there? A. I did—it was before any inquiry was
made.
WILLIAM HARWOOD . On Wednesday, the 25th of May, I went to my father's rick-yard, and found three trusses lying there—from the quality of the hay I believe it to be my father's—I was sent for by Mr. Dorey.
GEORGE SHORT re-examined. He gave me the order to take it to Dorey's almost before he began to cut any.
West. I did up three quarters and three trusses of straw; I asked Short if he had carried it all right, and he said "Yes;" I said, "Go and
ask for the money," and he went and brought me 1 s.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1895]
1895. THOMAS DANIELS was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 3rd of March, of an evil-disposed person, 19 printed books, value 3 l.; the goods of William Valentine, clerk: well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution,
THE REV. WILLIAM VALENTINE . I am a clergyman ot the Church of England. I was many years chaplain, and house-governor of the London Hospital, and am now incumbent of the new church of St. John's, Stepney, I had books in my library, which cost me upwards of 2000 l.—I transmitted about half of them to Mr. Evans, to be sold by auction—among them were the books that have since been discovered and produced—amongst others I sent Ritson's poems (which is a very valuable work—perhaps worth a guinea) Butler's Hudibras, and Lovelace's Lucasta, Littleton's Poems, and a translation of Caraoens—the books I have missed are worth 30 l.-in about a fortnight after, Mr. Evans sent me a catalogue—I saw there were many books missing, and I sent for him—I did not hear of any being found till the 14th of May—I have known the prisoner for several years—he must have known me well—I have sent books to him, and purchased books of him—he has sent them home—he keeps a book stall nearly opposite the hospital gate—I went to prisoner's stall on the evening of the 14th of May (in consequence of a communication I had from a person named Lewis, and after having been at Mr. Gladdin's) I saw nine volumes at the stall, belonging to me—among them Lovelace's Lucasta—I put my hand on eight of the books, and said, "This is my
property"—the prisoner did not make any reply—I repeated that, and he said he could take me to the party of whom he bought the books in North-street, which is not far from there, and that his name was Jamieson—I asked whether he knew the man well, or whether he was sure he could take me there—he then said' that the man gave him his direction—I sent to the police-officer whom I had seen at Gladdin's-he spoke to the prisoner, and he went with the constable—on the same evening, I found a volume of Camoens belonging to me, at the same stall—I had the works of Lord Byron, and in the Childe Harold was an autograph of Lord Byron—I received a volume of Hudibras from Mr. Chevely.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I take it for granted, yon are in the habit of buying books? A. I am—I bought the Ritson, I believe at Gladdin's—it is worth a guinea and a half—I think I got it for 16 s.—it might be 14 s.—my books have been sold by auction—the value of the books I sent was 1000 l.—I sent these books to Evans, the first week in March—I left the hospital the night before the books were sent away-I went to the hospital two or three times after I left—I had dealt with the prisoner perhaps about ten times—I found him fair and honest—they were cheap—they were books of trifling value—I got them as cheap as I could—the most of them were second-hand school books—the Campbell has not been found—the Sharp's edition of the poems, were duodecimosize—I did not leave the stall before I gave the prisoner into custody—I sent for the policeman.
MR. CLARKSON. A.. What was the value of the Campbell? A. Not much—it was bound in Russia, and my name being in it, I must have lent it some one to read—I always put my name in it, that they may know where to return it—I have very frequently found a book, and not known who it belonged to.
MICHAEL GORMAN . I am porter to Mr. Evans, in Pall-mall. I was employed to remove the prosecutor's books from the London Hospital to my master's premises—the prosecutor unlocked the bookcase, and pointed out these books to me—there were several bundles tied up, before I got there—I put them in bags, and the bags into the cart, and took them to Mr. Evans—it was stated ion the newspaper after that one of these bags was stolen from the cart, but I am positive it was not from the time they were put into the cart, till I got to the end of Aldgate, as I walked on one side, or behind the cart, and from there, to the best of my recollection, I got into the cart—I should think there was no opportunity for any one to steal them while I was in the library, unless they were stolen after I bagged them, and went out to get some refreshment—to the best of my belief there were none untied from the time I went out—I deposited the books in my master's premises, locked the door, and gave the key to Mr. Evans.
Cross-examined. Q. If I understand, you are ready to swear that none of these books were stolen? A. Yes—not between the London Hospital and Mr. Evans's—we had a pot of beer on the road—I left the cart outside for about two minutes—the carman went in first—I followed him—I am in the h bit of attending Evan's auction rooms—they sell books cheap there sometimes.
THOMAS EVANS . I am one of the partners in the house in Pall-mall.
I sorted the whole of the books, which were brought by Gorman—there were a great many valuable works—after that was done, I prepared a catalogue, and sent it to the prosecutor—I know Ritson's Scotch Poems very well—the two volumes would produce 14 s. or 15 s. in a sale—the Byron, in the condition this is, would be worth about 30 s.—it is six volumes, bound in green morocco; and Butler would be worth about 5 s.
Cross-examined. Q I suppose the value of this early edition of Byron is the binding? A. Yes—because it has been superseded by a late edition.
COURT. Q. Occasionally a book of value is to be picked up at a stall, at a small price? A. Yes—a stall keeper would be more likely to judge from the binding, than the value of the book.
RICHARD LEWIS . I am porter to Mr. Golden, in White chapel-road. I know the prisoner as keeping a stall there—on Saturday the 14th of May, I saw all these books except the Byron, at the prisoner's stall—I purchased the Ritson in two volumes, and Butler in two volumes of him for 5 s.—I afterwards saw a volume of Campbell's Pleasures of Hope, there, with the prosecutor's name in it—I held it up to the prisoner, and remarked that the Rev. Mr. Valentine's name was in it—I knew Mr. Valentine—he merely nodded—seeing the prosecutor's name in it, I went and mentioned it to my master—I went back to the stall in about ten minutes, and asked the prisoner for Campbell's Pleasures of Hope—be said it was sold—it was a 12mo. book, in good condition—I went back to Mr. Gladdin, and in consequence of what he said I went and told Mr. Valentine—when I came back, I told the prisoner he was not to sell any more books till Mr. Valentine came down.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he say, "Very wail?" A. Yes, and then I left him—I have bought books of him before—I attend sales, and am porter to Mr. Gladdin who lives about a minute's walk from the prisoner's house—Mr. Gladdin's is a shop—he sells all kinds of books.
REV. WILLIAM VALENTINE re-examined. Some of these books bear my name—the autograph of Lord Byron was in this volume, where this bit of wafer is—I received information of this about eleven o'clock in the morning—I was exceedingly unwell, and did not go about till about half-past five.
HENRY TONGMAN . I bought five volumes of Byron's Works of the prisoner, about the middle of April—I gave him 6 s. for them.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he first asked you 9 s. for them? A. Yes, which I refused—I went three days afterwards, and got them for 6 s.—I found them openly lying on the stall.
COURT. Q. Is it common for persons in the book trade to sell books for less than they give for them? A. They frequently sell very cheap—the marking of books varies.
THOMAS CHEVELEY . I live in Jubilee-place, Stepney. On the 14th of May, I saw a little volume of Hudibras on the prisoner's stall—I bought that, and another book of poems of small value for two shillings—I saw Littleton's Works there—I had frequently seen the prisoner's stall before, but I had not noticed books of the value of these there—he had generally books of an inferior description—I asked if he had any other books of a similar description—he said he thought he had, and looked about—I said I was in the habit of passing, and would look in again.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go again? A. Yes, on my return-I am rather a judge of books—an illiterate person might think this book of small value.
JAMES EVES ( police-sergeant H 14.) I remember on the evening of the 14th of May being at Mr. Gladdin's shop, and I saw Mr. Valentine-I followed him to the prisoner's stall—he found there some of his books, which had been stolen—I told the prisoner he had better come to the station, and explain how he got them—he said he bought them of a man named Jamieson, who lived at No. 12, North-street, White chapel—in going along, he said, "I had the address of the man, but I have lost it"—he said he gave 12 s. for them—I was present when he was examined before the Magistrate—he said on the first occasion he did not wish to say any thing—I went to two Nos. 12, in North-street, but could hear of no person of the name of Jamieson.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1896]
1896. GEORGE COLEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of June, 9 ivory balls, value 8 s., the goods of John Hawkins.
JOHN HAWKINS . I keep the Crown and Anchor in Farringdon-street On the night of the 10th of June the prisoner walked into my house—when he came down stairs a person said something to me, and I went after the prisoner to Shoe-lane—I met him, and said I wanted to speak to him—he ran away—I followed him to Crown-court—I caught hold of him, and the policeman came up—I lost nine ivory balls—they were safe in the house two minutes before the prisoner came in.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. There was another man in the house, was there not? A. Yes—I saw the prisoner come in, and open the parlour door—I was in the bar serving—I can see every body go up stairs—I have not seen the other man since—I had been into the bagatelle-room ten minutes before, and the balls were all right.
RICHARD JACKSON . I saw the prisoner come into the prosecutor's—there was not another young man with him then—he opened the parlour door, and called his friend—they went up stairs, and came down—they were not in the house more than a minute and a half—the bagatelles-balls are generally kept up stairs—when the prisoner came down he looked in at the parlour door—his friend went out, and he went and called " jack "—I mentioned it to Mr. Hawkins—the prisoner had a blouse on—I could not see whether he had any thing under it.
ANN LOFTING . I live in Crown-court, Crown-place, Temple-bar. I heard a cry of "Stop thief" on the evening of the 10th of June—I swa the prisoner, with a blouse on, run up a court, and persons following him, calling "Stop thief"—he threw one of these balls away—a little farther on he threw away five more—I picked them up—they came into my place, and marked them—I only know the prisoner by his dress.
JOHN HAWKINS re-examined. J was in the court—I had hold of him, directly afterwards—he is the same person.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in Crown-court at the time Jackson speaks of? A. Yes—I ran after the prisoner—I did not see the other man—I am certain the prisoner wore that blouse.
ISAAC TOMLINSON . I was going up Fleet-street, and met the prosecutor—he told me what had taken place—I saw the prisoner throw the balls out of his pocket into this court—he had a blouse on.
WILLIAM WHITE ( police-constable F 88.) I found the prisoner in the prosecutor's custody, in Shire-lane—I picked up one ball next morning, a little after day-light, in the court.
( Property produced and sworn to.) GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18420613-1897]
1897. GEORGE RICHARDSON was indicted for embezzlement.
JAMES FRITH . I am a wine and spirit-merchant, in Bishops gate-street. The prisoner was in my service about the middle of last March—I took him as a commercial traveler—I afterwards employed him to receive money on my account—I received this letter from him on the 29th of May—I know it is his handwriting—he sent it on Sunday—I gave directions to have him apprehended the next day—there was an account due to me from Farr and from Furnaval—the prisoner has never accounted to me for the sums due to me from them.
Prisoner. You agreed to give me 2 l. a week as salary, from my saying that 15 s. a week was as little as I could do to travel on, and 1 l. 5 s. to live upon; you did this when I had been a fortnight or three weeks in your service, in presence of your wife; you applied to me to apply to the Guarantee Society for security for 100 l., but my two referees being out of town, I declined it, but I offered to procure you security for 100 l. from friends; your answer was, "Never mind, there is no occasion for it." Witness. I wanted security, and they would not be security for him after making inquiries—I only agreed to give him 1 l. 5 s. a week, and said in a month or two I would raise him 5 s. a week, till it came to three guineas.
( Letter read)—" Dolphin, Long-alley, 29th May. Sir, with feelings of true repentance, I make another appeal to you. I, on the other tide, give a faithful account of what I am due to you, from a consciousness that I have acted wrong. I hope you will this time forgive me. I have a friend who will be my security for the amount, if it be repaid, by instalments; if you will give me another trial, all shall be right for the future. I shall not absent myself, for the sake of my family. I hope this time you will be lenient. I shall be at the warehouse to-morrow morning, at nine o'clock. With all humility, and hopes of forgiveness, I remain, &c. Garrett, 5 l. 8 s;. Gale, 7 s;. Rout, 19 s;. Farr, 19s.) Bun yard, 1 l. 1 s.; Furnival, 19 s.; Payne, 1 l. 12 s.—total, 11 l. 15 s."
Prisoner. You were about discharging your man Thomas, and you said to me, "Has he said any thing to you about going away?"—I said he had, and you said, "Don't you think you bad better get into conversation with him, and say you have a salary of two guineas and a half a week." Witness. Nothing of the kind.
CHARLES FURNIVAL . I keep the Dolphin, in Long-alley, Moor fields, the prisoner lodged with me for about a fortnight—I bought a keg of spruce of him, as Mr. Frith's traveler—I asked if he was authorized to receive the money—he said he was—I paid him, and have the receipt—( read.)
NATHANIEL FABR . I am a licensed victualler. On the 16th of May the prisoner came, and said he called to collect the money, if it was convenient for me to pay him—the bill was 1 l., which I paid; he wrote the receipt before my face to the bill of parcels—( read.)
WILLIAM WOOD ( City police-constable, No. 667.) I took the prisoner.
Prisoner. I have got him nearly forty customers; and when it is considered the small amount of salary proposed to be given, I think there is
not a man of business who would say it was enough—I expected the prosecutor would have paid me the two weeks salary due to me to the 1st of June; but he has never paid me any wages for the last fortnight.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 42.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1898]
1898. THOMAS CHAPMAN the younger was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 1 mortgage deed, being a security for the payment to William Green, of 185 l., and of the value of 185 l., the property of the said William Green.—Three other counts, varying the description of the instrument.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM GREEN . I farm about 160 acres of land, and carry on the business of a cow-keeper and dairyman, in Pancras-street, Tottenham-court-road. I know the prisoner—he was indebted to me 185 l. last year—that was the balance of a larger sum which he had owed me—he gave me, as a security for that sum, a bill of 185 l., payable six months after date, and dated 8th November, 1841, and a mortgage-deed—this is the bill, and the draft of the mortgage-deed—( looking at them)—my attorney brought it to me, and I examined it—the bill became due on the 11th of May in this year—it was presented in a regular way—I did not receive any money—the prisoner called on me in the afternoon after the bill was presented—he said, "Have you got the leases and mortgage-deed with you?"—I said, "Yes, I have"—he said, "Will you come with me up to my father's house; and I think we shall come to some arrangement"—I said I could not go very well then, as I was just going out with my milk—he said, "Pooh, pooh, never mind that"—I went up stairs, put them into my pocket, and went with him to his father's house, in Tottenham-court-road—(the prisoner lives in Cheyne-street)—when we got to his father's house, I found his father, Mrs. Chapman, and her brother—the prisoner's father has the same Christian name as he has—I said, "Do you want to see me, Mr. Chapman?" (addressing myself to the elder Mr. Chapman)—he said, "Not particularly"—he said to his son, " Tom, why don't you settle with Mr. Green?"—and turning to me at the same time, he said to me, "Have you got the leases with you?" "I have, sir," said I, and I took the lease and mortgage-deed out of my pocket, and handed them to the elder Mr. Chapman—he took them in his hand, opened them, looked at them about a minute, and laid them down on the table—the prisoner then said, "I want to look at these things"—he took them off the table, and opened them and read them, I should think for about a minute—he then got up, and he and his uncle went out of the room together—the uncle was out of the room for it might be a minute, or a minute and a half—he then came in by himself—I said, "Where is he gone with the leases?"—the mother said, "I don't know; father, you had better go and see"—the father went to see, and while he was absent, the prisoner came in, sat down in the chair that he went from, and said, "There shall be no more bother about these things; I'm d—d if I have not burned them!"—I have never seen the deed since—I said I would not believe it, and asked him what he called himself, whether he called himself a gentleman, or what he called himself—I do not know what he said, but his mother made answer, "What a shameful rascal you must be to serve Mr. Green in this kind of way!"—his mother then went
out of the room, and brought up some pieces of paper, which I observed by the writing, was part of the mortgage-deed, and I saw it was burnt—the deed was on paper—there was some altercation took place, and they so arranged, that I was to have some horses and carts in lieu of the money—a parcel of old things—I said, "I won't have any thing to do with it, tell them yourselves, and pay me over the money; that is all I want"—I vent and consulted my attorney, and directed the prisoner to be apprehended—the deed I produced at the prisoner's father's was the one I had received from my attorney, Mr. Burt.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What was the original sum? A. I lent the prisoner 285 l. at first, and for that he and his brother together gave me a bill—I got 100 l. on that bill when it became doe—I believe the prisoner gave me about 20 l. bonus—it was their own proposal, I did not go after them—I cannot say exactly what it was—it was either under or over 20 l.—it was under, I know—I will not swear that—it may have been 20 l.—the bill became due, and I got 100 l. and took another bill for 185 l.—I got a bonus of 10 l. for that—he owed me a bill of 4 l. for dung, and he give me 14 l.—I got 30 l. for the two bills—I was not in the habit of discounting bills—whatever I have done was for this prisoner—I discounted two bills, I believe, before—the first, I think, was for 80 l.—I got 4 l. bonus on that, and there was another I got 4 l. on—I did not get interest on either bill.
Q. At the time of the destruction of this mortgage deed, did you know the prisoner was a bankrupt? A. No, Sir, I did not—I heard that they were going to strike the docket that very night, after he destroyed the deed—this was on the 11th of May—I met him at the office afterwards—I do sot believe I saw him any where else before I Was there—I saw him the same night, as he was to sell nine cows, and I was to have part of the money, but, to the best of my knowledge, I did not see him afterwards—I saw him that night in George-street.
Q. Were you in no house with him after the burning of the deed? A. To the best of my knowledge, I was not—I mean to swear I did not drink with him—I was in no premises with him that night, to the best of my knowledge—I met him in the street—I was in no house in George-street with him, till I went with the policeman to take him up.
Q. Then you never drank ginger-beer and half-and-half in the counting-house with him? A. That was when I went there to receive my money for the nine cows—I never asked him, after that night, for a wagon and two carts and two horses—it was an arrangement of theirs, I would not have it—I met him the same night at the gate of the premises in George-street, and asked him if he had got the money—he said he had got no money—I said I knew he had, and asked him what had become of the money for the nine cows—"I have not been paid," says he—I said, "I won't believe you; you would not sell nine cows without being paid."
Q. Did he not say he should be very glad if he could pay you, but he could not put you in a better situation than his other creditors? A. Not to my knowledge—I wanted to be better off than his other creditors, certainly; he had destroyed my deed—I heard there was a docket the night that the prisoner had the deed—I did not know that I could not receive this money without a fraud on the other creditors—they told me they were going to compound it—I understood, when they were going
to Mr. Williams's, they were going to compound—I did not know these was a docket struck—I understood they were going to issue circulars to pay so much in the pound—I wanted my money at any time—he sold the cows that night—he was before the Magistrate after that, he was bailed, and has surrendered here—I have bad a great deal of dealings with his father in my lifetime—he advanced me money on one occasion—he had the lease of my premises, and that I paid him—the prisoner never told me that the lease of the premises I had was his father's—I thought they were the prisoner's.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was the first bill destroyed when you received the other? A. The first bill was backed by George Chapman, and accepted by Thomas—the day before that bill became due the prisoner came and said to me, "I shall not be able to take up that bill, I am afraid; I have been laying out a great deal of money in building and one thing or other; I don't think I shall be able to do it"—"Well," says I, if you can't pay it, your brother must"—he said, "He will call here presentry," and he did—I did not present the bill on its becoming due, as the prisoner called and said to me, "Don't you present that bill, I shall be out of town, and the people at our house know nothing about it," and I did not present it—I waited till ten o'clock at night for both parties to come, as I supposed, to settle—I did not demand the money of George—I went to my attorney, and found that by not presenting it I had taken off the responsibility from George—George is a good man; I should have taken a bill on him much better than this—I should not have taken this bill at all without the backing of George—I have never been able to get the money since.
THOMAS JOHN DUBOIS . I am clerk to Mr. Boy dell, a solicitor. I served a notice on a woman, who stated she was the prisoner's wife, to produce to the Court and Jury a certain indenture or mortgage deed, dated the 8th of November, 1841, which was a security from Thomas Chapman to William Green for the sum of 185 l.—I also served a copy of the same notice on Mr. Williams, the prisoner's attorney—( notice read.) HENRY BURT. I am solicitor to the Southampton estate, and acted as attorney to Mr. Green, on the preparation of the mortgage deed—this is a draft of it—I cannot recollect whether Mr. Green or Mr. Chapman brought the lease for preparing this deed—I believe it was Chapman—our clerk prepared it—I examined it—it was engrossed on paper, and executed at our office by the prisoner—our clerk's name is Charlton—I cannot say whether he or myself was present on the occasion of its execution—either he or myself was the attesting witnesses—no one else was in the room—Mr. Charlton was not there the whole of the time.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. You cannot swear whether you attested it, or your clerk? A. No, I saw it executed.
CHARLES HENRY CHARLTON . I am clerk to Mr. Burt—I saw the deed executed.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Were you the attesting witness to this deed? A. I cannot swear that I was.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you clerk to Mr. Burt? A. Yes, I was articled to Mr. Jacob Burt, his partner—I remember the execution of this deed—Mr. Burt and myself, nobody else, were in the room where this was executed, that I am aware of—I cannot swear that I attested this deed—I might have done it—I cannot swear positively whether
it was attested—no doubt it was—I do not swear that I saw it signed by Mr. Chapman.
GEORGE WALKER . I am one of Mr. Burt's clerks. I examined this draft with the original deed before it was executed—I believe I read the engrossment—this is a true draft of the engrossment—the deed was duly stamped with a 2 l. stamp after it was executed, the next day.
JOHN CHAMPNEYS ( police-sergeant S 7.) On the 15th of May the prisoner was brought to the station in Albany-street, at ten o'clock in the evening—he produced his protection as a bankrupt on that occasion, and I let him go—he said, "You are not going to lock me up"—the prosecutor said, "I don't want to lock you up, all I want is my money," and there was an agreement that they should meet the next day at Clerk en well.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What was it you said about the settlement? A. Green asked Chapman to come to some settlement, and Chapman said, "I have put you in the schedule; I can do no more; here it is; I can't come to a settlement; I have pat you as a creditor to the full amount;" after the production of the protection, Mr. Green said he would be very glad if he could settle with him—I said, "You had better. meet to-morrow"—it was proposed that they should meet at Clerkenwall police-court—I said, "You can give him into custody then if you like."
(Joseph Shackell, police-inspector; Dr. Francis Boote, Gower-street; Thomas Charles Jackson, Oxford-street; William Henry Akerwright, 47. Dorrington-square; John Hans Heel; John Hellyer, silversmith, 211, Tottenham-court-road; and Thomas Hunt, goldsmith and jeweler, 151, Tottenham-court-road; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy. — Judgment Respited.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Buttock, Esq.
