[trialAccount t18420613-1900]
1900. THOMAS CHATTERDON and WILLIAM DAVIS were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 1 decanter, value 5 s.; and 1 pint of wine, value 2 s;. the goods of Noah Bond ; to which CHATTERDON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 28.
THOMAS MAPPLETON. I am bar-man to Mr. Noah Bond, who keeps the Nag's Head, in James-street, Covent-garden. On the 14th of May, I was on service in the house—about ten minutes before five o'clock in the morning the prisoners came in at the frotit-door with a female—they had a quartern of gin, and staid but a short time—they then all went out together—the prisoners returned in about twenty minutes, and asked if I had seen the female—I said I had not, she went out with them—they then went away—Chatter don afterwards came back alone in better than half-an-hour—he went through a door into a private part of the bar, which is only
made public in the day-time—he asked for a glass of gin, which I served him with—in a short time Davis came, and asked if I had seen hit friend—I was engaged at the time, and he saw Chatter don—Davis staid but a short time—after Chatter don had gone, I was told something, and I missed a decanter of wine which had been standing on a cabinet close to where Chatter don had been—I had seen it safe an hour and a half before.
WILLIAM LEWIS KETCHER . I lived in Acorn-street, Bishops gate. I was at the Nag's Head on the 14th of May, about five minutes before five in the morning—I saw Chatter don there—he went into a part called the " bottle department"—Davis came in two or three minutes afterwards—they had some gin to drink—Davis was at the bar playing with some girls—Chatter don was in the bottle department, and I saw him put his left arm across the bar, and with his left hand take the decanter of wine—Davis was at that time playing with the girls, attracting the bar-man's attention to the right-hand part of the bar—when Chatter don took the decanter, Davis immediately went to him, and closed the door of the bottle department—Davis left first, and Chatter don afterwards—as he left I saw part of the decanter protruding from his pocket—I pursued him down Hart-street—he looked round, saw me, and ran off.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was Chatter don tipsy? A. I should think he was rather the worse for liquor—he was not decidedly drunk—he reeled into the place.
JOHN MAN WOOD ( police-constable F 65.) At a quarter past five o'clock that morning I saw Chatter don, and pursued him—J went into a brothel in Phoenix-alley—I saw both the prisoners there, and Davis had got the decanter in his band—as soon as he saw me he shoved it under the bed, and said, "What is all this about?"—I said Chatter don was charged with stealing a decanter of wine—Davis said no more, but made his escape out of a side door—I took Chatter don—he said he knew nothing about it.
DAV1S- NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1901]
1901. THOMAS CHATTERDON was again indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 watch, value 5 l;. 2 seals, value 10 s.; and 1 watch-guard, value 11 s.; the goods of Benjamin Bellringer.
SARAH BELLRINGER . I am the wife of Benjamin Bellringer; we live in Crown-court, Russell-street, Covent-garden. The prisoner had a ready. furnished room in our house from the 31st of March till the 8th of April—on that morning he brought two more persons to breakfast with him, and they all left—I had a watch hanging in the kitchen, which had a silver guard, seals, and a key to it—I missed it when they were gone—the prisoner never returned—this is the watch, guard, seals, and key.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I took the apartment? A. No, another took it, but you came as two brothers, in the name of Chatterdon—I was told Mr. Adams called, but I did not see him—he did not give me any money for the rent.
COURT. Q. Are you sure the prisoner was one who was in the house that morning? A. Yes—he went away, and did not come back.
GEORGE ATTENBOROUGH . I am in the service of Richard Attenborough, a pawnbroker. I produce a silver watch, a guard, and seals, which I took in of the prisoner on the morning of the 8th of April—I am sure he is the person.
Prisoner's Defence, A person named Adams took the apartment; he
asked me if I would take part of a lodging with him, as his cousin was going into the country; and he said, when he left I should go and stop with him; we all had breakfast there that morning, and all came out together; the party who took the apartment would have been here, but I understand he is confined in Tot hill-fields for an assault.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years. (There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18420613-1902]
1902. JOHN LINEHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 8lbs. 6oz. weight of nails, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Thomas Cubitt.
JOSEPH MARKQUICK . I am in the employ of Lewis Cubitt, who lives at Chelsea. About twenty minutes before six o'clock in the evening of the 4th of June I saw the prisoner get over a wall and building belonging to Thomas Cubitt, which is opposite where I live—the workmen had left about a quarter of an hour—I saw the prisoner come back with a cap in his hand, which he had on his head when he went over—I followed, he saw me, and ran away—I took him, and found in his cap about 1000 nails—he said he picked them up among the rubbish—I said, "What, new nails?"—he said "Yes"—I gave him to a policeman, and went to the building, and saw the nails had been shifted—I found some nails in the premises similar to those found on him—part of them were scattered on the floor—I compared those in his cap with what I found, and they corresponded—they were Mr. Thomas Cubitt's.
JOHN DENMAN ( police-constable B 69.) The prisoner was given in charge—be said he took them to sell for a halfpenny a pound, as he had done before—he told me he took them out of the building.
Prisoner's Defence. Mr. Cubitt's foreman said if I told the truth he would let me go; they were nails that were lying on the floor.
GUILTY . Aged 11.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1903]
1903. THOMAS POTTER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Philip Hemery, from his person.
THOMAS HARE ( police-constable A 103.) I was on duty at Westminster on the morning of the 7th of June—I saw the prosecutor listening to the band, and the prisoner close behind him—I watched, and as the soldiers were coming out of the archway I saw the prisoner get closer to the prosecutor, and turn from him with this handkerchief in his right hand—I took him—he dropped the handkerchief—the prosecutor turned round, and owned it—the prisoner said he had not had it.
PHILIP HEMERY . I live in St. James's-place, Hampstead-road. I was in the Colour-yard, at St. James's, about eleven o'clock in the morning of the 7th of June—I put my hand to my pocket, for my handkerchief, and missed it—I turned, and saw it in the policeman's hand—he was in the act of seizing the prisoner—it was safe in my pocket two or three minutes before—this is it.
Prisoners Defence. I was listening to the band, and as I was coming out the policeman seized me, and said, "What do you mean by picking that man's pocket?" there were two or three men between me and the
prosecutor; he said he saw it in my hand, and the prosecutor said he did not.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1904]
1904. ELLEN LYON was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 2 pairs of shoes, value 5 s., the goods of James Francis Thompson.
JONATHAN POLLARD ( police-constable D 125.) In consequence of information, I went in search of the prisoner on the 6th of June, and found her walking down Marylebone-lane, about twenty minutes before four o'clock in the afternoon, with this gaiter in her hand, and found this pair of shoes in her apron—I asked where she got them—she said she picked them up—I took her to the station, and she was searched by Eckett—who gave me another pair of shoes—the prisoner was the worse for liquor.
MARY ECKETT . I am the wife of William Eckett, a policeman. I live at the station at Marylebone-lane—I asked the prisoner if she had any thing in her pocket—she said she had no pocket, but I found her pocket, and this pair of shoes in it—she said, "For the honour of God, don't produce these shoes; there is plenty besides this against me, keep them for yourself, and say nothing about them."
FRANCIS WILLIAMS . I am salesman to James Francis Thompson. These shoes are my employer's—they were at a stall outside his shop—I bad seen them safe about half-past one o'clock—I had not missed then till the policeman brought them at half-past six that afternoon.
Prisoner's Defence. I met a person; she treated me; she bad these shoes in her basket; she gave them to me, and told me to wait at the corner, which I did, and the policeman and another passed by me; I saw this gaiter on the pavement, and was following a young man, as I thought it might be his.
GUILTY . Aged 24— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1905]
1905. GEORGE ALLEN and RICHARD BROOKS were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 1 pair of boots, value 3 s. 8 d.; the goods of William Buer; and that Allen had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS SPARKS . I am a stable-keeper, at Ealing. On the 4th of June, I was in Frederick-street, Regent's-park, about half-past two o'clock in the afternoon, and saw the two prisoners—they had just joined together when I met them, and were about 150 yards from the prosecutor's—Allen pulled a pair of boots out of his pocket, and showed them to Brooks—he then put them into his pocket again, and passed by me—I watched them till they turned into Albany-street—I went on the way I was going—I went to Mr. Buer's shop, and went with his lad after the prisoner—I saw these boots fall from Brooks's apron.
EDWARD EDWARDS . I am foreman to Mr. William Buer, a pawnbroker. Mr. Sparks came to our shop, while I was looking for this pair of boots, which I had missed from a board outside—I went after the prisoners, and saw them in Albany-street—they turned down a court, and when they came out of the court, I laid hold of both of them—Brooks escaped, Mr. Buer was coming up the street, and took him—I did not see Brooks drop any thing, but I saw this pair picked up, which are what I missed.
CHARLES CLOKE ( police-constable S 85.) I was at the station when the prisoners and the boots were brought.
Allen's Defence. I was going up Albany-street, the man took me, and struck me several times; I had nothing about me.
Brook's Defence. He searched me, and tore my waistcoat open; I had nothing, and he let me go; I went on, Mr. Buer met me, and took me back to where they were scuffling.
HENRY FOWLER ( police-constable, E 111.) I produce a certificate of Allen's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office, ( read) the prisoner is the person.
ALLEN— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.
BROOKS— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1906]
1906. SARAH BARROW was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 20 sovereigns, value 20 l., the monies of Henrich Jager, from his person; and SARAH EVANS , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—3rd COUNT, for feloniously harbouring and maintaining the said Sarah Barrow.
HENRICH JAGER . I am a sugar baker, and live at Limehouse. On the night of the 17th of May, I saw Barrow in the Commercial-road—I went with her to where she lived in Dorset-place—she did not go to bed, but I fell on the bed, and went to sleep—I had been down to the Nore that day, and was tired—Barrow afterwards awoke me, and told me I must get up, as her husband was coming—I got up, and went out—I then put my hand into my pocket, and missed twenty sovereigns—I had 2 l. odd in silver, loose, and twenty sovereigns in a purse—when I went into the house I had my hand in my pocket, and knew the money was there then—when I got out the twenty sovereigns were gone out of the purse, and about half the silver was put into it—I went back to the house with a policeman, who knocked several times—they would not open the door—he was obliged to break it open—the policeman asked the landlord for a light—he went up stairs, and left me to mind the door—I had treated Barrow—I had paid for what she bad with sixpence in silver.
JURY. Q. Had you been drinking? A. I had had something, but was not intoxicated.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you go to this house merely to sleep? A. I went with her, and went to sleep.
GEORGE HAILES ( police-constable K 54.) The prosecutor came to me about two o'clock in the morning, on the 18th of May, in the Commercial-road; I went with him to No. 3, Dorset-place—I saw a light, and heard a talking up stairs—I knocked at the door for I suppose ten minutes—I then heard the back-door open, and it appeared as if they were clambering over a wall—I heard several bricks fall into the water tub, which we found afterwards, and the light was put out—I got the door open, and told the prosecutor to mind the door while I got a fresh light, and went up stairs—I found Barrow and another young woman there in bed—the prosecutor then came up, and said, in Barrow's presence, that he had been robbed of twenty sovereigns, and he pointed out Barrow as the one who had robbed him—I told her to get up, and dress—the other female who was in bed said, "If there has been any thing stolen, you may search my things before I put then on; I know nothing about it"—I searched the room, but found nothing—I went with Barrow to the station—I then went
back to the house, and found Evans—I had seen her down stairs when I first went to the house, and she gave me a light—the prosecutor said " That is the woman who was in company with Barrow"—she denied it, and said she had not seen the man—I searched the lower room—I found a watch, but no money.
Barrow's Defence, I met the prosecutor at the Star and Garter; I took him home, he fell on the bed, and went to sleep; I went down, and staid with my landlady; in half-an-hour I went to call him up; as I wanted to go to bed; the people below asked him to pay for the room; he said he would not; we had had the bricklayers in the house, which might be the reason of the bricks falling into the water-tub.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1907]
1907. MARY DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of June, 2 yards of calico, value 2 s.; and 1 towel, value 1 s;. the goods of William Palmer.
MARY ANN PALMER . I am the wife of William Palmer; we live in Frederick-street, Hampstead-road. On Saturday last the prisoner came to clean a room for a lodger, and I asked her to clean two rooms for me—I left her in my back kitchen, while I went up stairs—my daughter came to me, and in consequence of what she said, I went down stairs, and from a drawer in my front kitchen I missed a parcel which I had put there my. self, he night before—it contained an unmade calico chemise—I went to the prisoner, and spoke to her—she said she had not been to the drawer at all—I was standing in the back kitchen, speaking to her, and I saw a drawer there, into which I had put some linen which had come borne the night before, and it did not shut quite close—I had put a towel over it, and I observed that the towel was gone—I said, "You have opened that drawer, and taken the towel"—she said she had not, and she was willing to take her oath that she had not—there is a bed in that back kitchen—I saw the bottom of it was tumbled, and said, "You have been doing something to that bed"—she said she had not—I turned it up at the foot, and the parcel I had missed and the towel fell out on the floor—I said I did not wish to injure her, if she would own to taking the things, as she knew she had done so, she might go; but she was so very abusive, I was forced to send for an officer,
FRANCES PALMER . I am the witness' daughter. I had been in the hospital, and had come out—I was sitting in a chair, and leaning my head against a sofa-bed in the front kitchen—my eyes were shut, but I was awake I saw the prisoner come into the front kitchen, she opened the bottom drawer, and there was nothing there—she then opened the next, and took a white parcel out of the corner, and went out of the kitchen—I went and told my mother, and she came down.
JOHN CARTER ( police-constable S 88.) I was sent for to take the prisoner—I received this parcel and towel from the prosecutor—the prisoner said she had never seen the calico, or the towel, or the drawer at all).
Prisoners Defence. She ordered me out of the house; I said if she would send for an officer and search me, I would go, but I had not seen the things; she kept me sitting an hour and a half before she sent for the officer; the daughter never saw me in the front kitchen; I never saw the drawer, nor the calico.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1908]
1908. HENRY BODMAN was indicted for embezzlement.
MARY SPARROW . I am single. I keep a china and glass shop in Paddington-street—the prisoner was my errand-boy—he came into my service on the 2nd of May—he received some monies for me which he brought to me—on the 3rd of May I sent him out with some china in a basket, to No, 6 York-buildings, New-road—he was to receive 22 s.—he did not return to bring the china or the money—I saw him again on the 2nd of June, in charge.
EMMA WELLS . I live with Mrs. Easy, at No. 6, York-buildings. I bought a set of china at Mrs. Sparrow's, and the prisoner went with me to carry it in a basket—I took it out of the basket, and gave him the basket, with a sovereign, and 2 s. in silver.
Prisoner. This witness would not pay me, because I had not got a receipt. Witness. I gave him the money in the passage.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON ( police-sergeant D 4.) On the 2nd of June the prisoner was given to me by his father, who told me what he was charged with—I found 1 s. in one of the legs of his trowsers, and 2 s. in the other—as we were going to the office I said, "What have you done with the basket?"—he said, "I threw it away in a court in Fleet-street."
Prisoner's Defence. She would not pay me because I had not a receipt. I was coming back, a young man met me, and took me to the Surrey Theatre. I threw away the basket in a court in Fleet-street. The young man kept me about a week, and then gave me 5 s.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.

1909. EMILY WYATT was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of April, 1 pair of spectacles, value 20 s., the goods of William Hexter.
CHARLOTTE HEXTER . I am the wife of William Hexter, of Adam-street East, Manchester-square—the prisoner called on the 14th of April, under the pretence of telling me of a place for my son—(I had seen her once before)—I had a pair of silver spectacles on the table—they were safe about two hours before she came—I missed them next morning—these now produced are them.
RICHARD KING TAYLOR . I live in St. Martin's-lane, and am a tailor. The prisoner lodged in my house from the 10th to the 16th of April—on the 14th of April I saw her wearing a pair of silver spectacles about the house—they were like these—I never saw her wear spectacles before or after.
HENRY HUTCHINS . I am apprentice to Mr. Clark, a pawnbroker, in Long-acre. These spectacles were pawned by Miller.
MARY ANN MILLER . I live in Parker-street, Drury-lane. T was servant to the prisoner—in April last I remember her bringing home a pair of silver spectacles, which she said she had found—she gave them to me to pawn, which I did at Mr. Clark's, for 3 s. 6 d.—I gave her the money.
Prisoner's Defence. I did take them and pawn them, but I meant to take them back. The prosecutor told me, if I would tell where they were she would not come against me: I did tell, and then she took me. I am a foreigner, and did not know the law was so strict.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18420613-1910]
1910. THOMAS NEALE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 shirt, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of George Groombridge.
GEORGE SEARLE . I am in the service of Mr. George Groombridge, a linen-draper, in Ratcliff-highway. On the 1st of June the prisoner came in for a shirt—I took down one—he then said, "Never mind," and walked out of the shop—as he was going out my master spoke to roe—I went after the prisoner—he turned up the next turning, and I lost sight of him—I spoke to a policeman, and soon after the policeman brought him back—I found this new shirt on him, which I had missed from outside our house—when he got to the station he said he had bought it of a man in the Back road for 1 s. 6 d., and had had it three weeks—it is perfectly new, and had never been put on before.
GEORGE HAILES . I am a policeman. On the evening of the 1st of June, about seven o'clock, I met the prisoner going from the shop, with something under his jacket, which appeared like a shirt or a handkerchief—when I got to the prosecutor's shop I was told something—I went after the prisoner, and met him coming out of a public-house, which I had seen him go into—he then had this new shirt on his back, and his own white one under his arm—I took him to the prosecutor, and asked him where be got the check-shirt from—be said he had had it four or five months—Searle identified it by the make and by the buttons—when the prisoner got to the station he said he bought it in the Back-road, of a sailor, who had several red ones and a blue one.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been in the prosecutor's shop, and they wanted more money than I had got; I went down Back-lane, and met a man who had shirts to sell; he wanted 2 s. for one; I had no more than 1 s. 6 d., and he took that; I went to a public-house, and took my old one off, and put it on; I was coming out, and the policeman took me.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
NEW COURT.— Monday, June 20 th, 1842.

1915. HENRY SPINKS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 2 shillings, the monies of Charles Gurney and another, his masters.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES GURNEY . I have a partner, and keep the Crosby Head, in Pit field-street, Hoxton. The prisoner was my barman—I marked eight half-crowns on the Saturday, and on the Monday four half-crowns, eight shillings, and four sixpences—Inspector Shackell saw me mark them—on Tuesday night, the 17th of May, the prisoner went to his supper—I placed the four half-crowns, eight shillings, and four sixpences, in the till, clearing the whole of the money that was in the till previously—he finished his supper a few minutes after nine o'clock, and went into the shop—at ten o'clock I cleared the whole money from the till, with the exception of the nine shillings—I examined it, and found a deficiency—I called Inspector Shackell and Brannan, and gave the prisoner in charge—he was searched, and some money found on him—amongst it was two shillings which I had placed in the till on that Monday night—I had marked no shilling on Saturday—Brannan asked where he got it from—he said he had taken these two shillings in change for half-a-crown last week, and then he hesitated, and said on Saturday.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you owe him any thing for wages? A. I believe a quarter's wages, about 6 l. 5 s., but he never asked for them—he was taken a few minutes after ten o'clock, and about a quarter of an hour after he was taken, his attention was called to the shillings—it was within an hour—he was searched immediately after he was taken—he was not spoken to about the shillings directly he was taken—I did not count the money I left in the till—there was not more than 12 s.—I will swear there was not 15 s.—there are five tills—I never leave more than 2 s. in—all the barmen have access to them—there are three besides the prisoner—a great many persons come in the course of an hour.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you sure he said he had these two shillings on the Saturday? A. Yes.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-sergeant G 20.) About ten o'clock, on the 17th of May, I was called to the prosecutor's—Shackell was with me—the prisoner was brought to the counting-house—I told him I was a sergeant of police, that he was suspected of robbing his master's till, and asked how much he had about him—he said 30 s.—I asked what change he had given that evening over the counter—he said no change—I searched him, and found a purse, containing a sovereign, a sixpence, three four penny pieces, and also in the same pocket 3 s. 6 d.—three of the loose shillings were marked—I also found some coppers—the prosecutor looked at the marked shillings, and said they were the marked money he had put into the till that evening, about nine o'clock—I asked the prisoner where he got the marked money from—he said, "I got it in change for a half-crown last week"—he then said, "On Saturday."
Cross-examined. Q. All this matter was gone into as one case before the Magistrate, was it not? A. Yes—the policeman had sent people there to
make purchases—Mrs. Kershaw was one of them—I examined the shillings, and put them down on the desk, and showed them to the prisoner when I asked him about them—it was not a minute from the time of my taking him and taking up the shillings.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How close was he? A. Quite close—these are the shillings—they are marked over the crown.
JOSEPH SHACKELL . I am a police-inspector. I saw the prosecutor mark the shillings on the 17th of May—I was called about ten o'clock—I saw the money found on the prisoner.
ELIZABETH KERSHAW . I am the wife of John Kershaw, a policeman. Between nine and ten o'clock at night, on the 17th of May, I gave half-a-crown to the prisoner, and got a glass of gin-and-peppermint—the prisoner served me—he gave me 2 s. 4 d. out—he took the 2 s. out of the till.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1916]
1916. HENRY SPINKS was again indicted, with JAMES BECKWITH , for stealing, on the 17th of May, 1 half-crown, the money of Charles Gurney and another, the masters of Spinks.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES GURNEY . Spinks was in my employ as bar-man—Beckwith had been in a great many times during the day in question—on the 17th of May, among other things, I marked this half-crown, which Mrs. Kershaw got, and the officer marked it also—here are two black marks on the top of the crown—it is marked differently from the others that were put into the till—Brannan brought Beckwith into the counting-house, and this half-crow was taken out of his pocket, and some halfpence—I do not recollect anything else—the officer said to him, "Do you call these halfpence?"—Spinks then said, "I gave him change for a 5 s. piece, for half-a-pint of stout"—they were both sober—there was more silver on him than the change for a 5 s. piece.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. When was it you began this system of marking money? A. On the 14th of May—I marked twelve half-crowns all together, and put the marked money into the till on the Sunday and Monday, and four half-crowns on the Tuesday—I found a 5 s. piece in one of the tills—I cannot say which till it was in—I took it out—I took another customer into custody—he was searched, and nothing but half-pence found on him, and we let him go.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What wages were due to Spinks? A. quarter's wages—I cannot say how much more—there may have been more—I paid him his wages at the end of every quarter, if he wanted it—we made no particular agreement—I paid him every quarter—if our servants want the money they ask for it.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did he ever ask you for any wages? A. No, I should have paid him if he had—I commenced this system of marking, be. cause I suspected I was being robbed.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-sergeant G 20.) I took Beckwith into custody about ten minutes after I had taken Spinks—I asked Beckwith what he had about him, and told him I took him for being concerned with the prosecutor's bar-man in robbing the till—he said, "I have done nothing, I have got nothing; I have only got a few halfpence"—on coming out of
the public-house door, be endeavoured to thrust his hand into his right-hand dowsers' pocket—I prevented him, and took him round into the counting-house where Spinks and Shackell were—I found a half-crown, three shillings, and four sixpences in his right-hand trowsers' pocket—I put it on the desk, and said, "Do you call these halfpence?"—he did not say anything, but Spinks nodded to him, and said, "I gave him change for a 5 s. piece"—I searched his coat pocket, and found 11 d. in halfpence
in it.
JOSEPH SHACKELL . After Spinks was brought into the counting-house, I saw Beckwith brought in—Brannan said, "Is this what you call half-pence?" and threw them down—there was 7 s. 6 d.—I immediately said, "This is one of the half-crowns I marked," and which, about three-quarters of an hour before, I had given to Mrs. Kershaw, with three others—Beckwith said nothing—Spinks said, "Oh yes, I gave him change for-a 5 s. piece, for half-a-pint of stout"—I asked Mr. Gurney what half-a-pint of stout would come to—he said, 1 1/4 d., but there was no farthing found on him.
ELIZABETH KERSHAW . I got this half-crown, with three others, from Mr. Shackell, on Tuesday night, the 17th of May—I got a glass of gin-and-peppermint with it, and gave it to Spinks—he put it into the till—I looked at it the moment before I gave it, and saw these marks over the crown—I am sure this is the half-crown I gave to Spinks.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you pass other half-crowns of the marked money? A. Yes, at Gurney's.
HENRY REDMAN ( police-constable G 224.) I took Spinks to the Police-court the day after he was taken up, and after Beckwith had been taken up, he asked if I had been to his uncle to let him know—I said I had not, I did not know whether Mr. Gurney had—he said, "I know nothing of the other prisoner, I never saw him before."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1917]
1917. JOHN DOWSETT, alias Frederick Edwards, was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 spoon, value 10 s., the goods of William Puncher, his master.
WILLIAM PUNCHER. I keep the Red Lion, in Parliament-street, Westminster—the prisoner was my pot-boy. In March, 1841, I lost this spoon.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. This occurred fifteen or sixteen months ago, at the time the prisoner left you? A. Yes—he was taken a few days ago—I have no desire to press against him.
CHARLES MUNDAY . I was in the service of the prosecutor. I missed this spoon directly after the prisoner had left—I took him the day after he left, and found the spoon at his mother's house, but he ran from me—I do not know where he has been since.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1918]
1918. JOHN HOLMES , WILLIAM SHEEHAN , and RICHARD SULLIVAN were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 14 feet of lead pipe, value 1 l. 8 s., the goods of, and belonging to, James Ponsford; being fixed to a building.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN POLEY . I am a coachman, living at Elms-lane, Bays water. I live
opposite No. 14, Gloucester-row. In the evening of the 6th of June, I saw all the prisoners there—one was in the building—the other two throwing stones into a ditch—about half-an-hour after, I saw Holmes at No. 14,—he opened the garret-window, and went into the next house, came out again, and went to No. 14—he then began knocking some pipe, which was inside the third-floor—he wrenched it up and down, then dropped part of it down—I went round, and gave information—I then went into my room, and saw all three of them in the building—Sullivan was in the window, looking on, and Sheehan and Holmes were wrenching the pipe up and down—Sullivan was not ten yards from the others—he was at the window and was looking on.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How did they get into these houses? A. The back way—they are unfinished houses, and hare no doors.
GEORGE MASON . I am a carpenter, living at Elm's-lane. Poley gave me information in the evening of the 8th of June—I went into his house, and watched the prisoners—I saw them all three in No. 14—Holmes and Sheehan were wrenching the pipe up and down, and Sullivan standing by the window, looking on—he could not have avoided seeing them—be was about three yards from them—I went with Mr. William Ponsford into the building, and took Sullivan in the first-floor, went up stairs, and found some lead broken off—we then found Holmes in one corner of the top room.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not Sullivan stand by the side of the window? A. He did, he was looking out of the window.
THOMAS WHITEHAIRS . I live in Kensington-garden Terrace. I went to this building at half-past six o'clock, and took Holmes in the corner of the room—I asked him whether there were any more—he said there were—I had seen the three prisoners about half-past five the same evening—I knew them before.
LIONEL PONSFORD . These buildings belong to my uncle, James Ponsford—this leaden pipe is his—I know of its haying been placed there—I have seen the spot where it was broken off.
Cross-examined. Q. You have a share, have you not, in these houses? A. I have not, they are entirely his. (The prisoners received good characters.)
HOLMES— GUILTY . Aged 16.
SHEEHAN— GUILTY . Aged 17.) Confined Three Months.
SULLIVAN— GUILTY . Aged 17.
[trialAccount t18420613-1919]
1919. JEREMIAH THOMAS SEATHEARD was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 mangle, value 10 l.; and 2 wooden rollers, value 4 s.; the goods of Nicholas White.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
NICHOLAS WHITE . I am an auctioneer, living in Bridge-House-place, Newington-causeway. I let the prisoner the front and back parlours, on the left-hand side of a house in Three-Cup-alley, Shoreditch, about twenty months since—the two rooms on the right-hand side I reserved for myself, and in the front parlour on the right-hand I deposited a mangle—I did hot give the prisoner authority to take it out or sell it, or to go into that room—in consequence of information, I went into that room on Friday,
the 13th of May, with the policeman—I found the prisoner, his wife, and another woman, in the same room where the mangle had been—they were apparently living there—I did not see the mangle—I pointed out the prisoner to the policeman—the policeman said he was come to take him into custody for stealing the mangle—the prisoner said it was as much his as mine—I charged him with having removed it—he admitted it—he did not say where he had taken it—it was worth 10 l.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long was it since you had seen the prisoner before you went to take him? A. I had not seen him since I let him the rooms—he paid me no rent—I did not apply to him for it—the house was in a dilapidated state—there were no other tenants in the house, of my letting, but I heard there were other persons there—I did not know who was in the house or out of it—I went into these rooms when I let them to the prisoner—I locked the mangle up in the front parlour—I cannot tell whether I went into the room that I had let to the prisoner when I went with the policeman—I went into the room where the mangle was in, directly, because the prisoner was to be secured—there are eight small rooms in the house—to my knowledge people have not gone in and out, paying no rent, but lodging as they had a mind—it is my house—the mangle had been in the house two or three months before the prisoner went there—it had been in the next house; and having let the next house, I caused it to be removed into this room—the other house was in a dilapidated condition—I had let it, and not applied for the rent—the house in' which the prisoner lived was left me by will, by Mr. Hutton—the glass was all out before the prisoner came into it, and I put the glass all in—after the prisoner was in custody I got some glass from a shop—the prisoner's wife told me there was some glass that she had left there for 5 s.—I have not received any communication about glass from anybody since the prisoner has been there—I never said anything to anybody about not wishing to hurt the prisoner—the solicitor said, "I suppose you have no vindictive feelings?"—I said, "No, but I must put an end to stealing property in this way"—I have distained on the prisoner's wife lately, after first offering her goods if she would leave the place—there was a weekly rent to have been paid by the prisoner—it was not an understanding with the prisoner that I should not be paid any rent because the house was dilapidated—very recently there was a quarrel with the prisoner's wife and a bad character, and they broke sixteen squares of glass—I believe the prisoner is a cutter-up of beef and ham at fairs—the rent was 3 s. 3 d. a week—I did not make a memorandum of it in a book—after he was in custody I told his wife she appeared not to be able to pay so much as the rent of two rooms; if she chose to go into a back room up stairs, she might—I have seen the mangle at Dawson's—I know it from a crack in the iron-work of the box, and from its general appearance.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Have you any doubt about its being yours? A. Not the slightest.
JANE WILLIAMS . I am the wife of Edward Williams, and have lived in the same house where the prisoner did, in Three-Cup-alley, about nine months; the mangle was in the room on the left-band side—on the 28th of April, Mr. Dawson's men came with a cart—the prisoner was at the door, and Mr. Dawson's men took away the mangle—I did not hear any one give directions—I saw it put into the cart—I followed the cart to Mr. Dawson's house, in Old-street Road—when I got there I told the
men it was not the prisoner's property, but it was Mr. White's—the mangle was then taken out of the cart, and taken into Mr. Dawson's—I after. wards saw it at Worship-street—I am sure it is the same mangle that was taken out of the house where I live, and the one taken to Dawson's and the one that Mr. White identified—it had been mended on the lid, and I knew it by some flat-headed nails driven in on the lid—I have no doubt it is the same I had seen removed.
Cross-examined. Q. Yon were not on terms of intimacy with the prisoner? A. I was not—I lived at the top part of the house—I had never been in the rooms below—I never saw the mangle till I saw it taken out in the cart—I was at the top window, and looked down on the top of it—I saw the nails in it at Mr. Dawson's door—there was a large patch of black in the middle of the mangle—I did not pay any rent at that time—I have been living there nine months—I have paid rent to Mr. White since the 28th of April—I had only one room, and paid 1 s. 9 d. a week.
ELIZABETH HORROD . I am a widow, living in Three-Cup-alley. I know there was a mangle on those premises—I do not know myself whose
it was—I went there with the intention of purchasing it—I have since seen it at the police-office, and it is the one I saw on those premises.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen it before? A. Yes, about a year and nine months ago—I did not buy it, because it was too dear——there was no crack in the iron-work—there was something the matter with the spindle, and the handle would not keep on—I did not offer 1 l. and my own mangle for it—I have paid about a quarter of a year's rent—I took the place of Mrs. Fossett, not of the prosecutor.
JOHN DAWSON . I am a furniture-broker, and live in Old-street-road. The prisoner called on my wife, and said he had got a mangle to sell, I sent two of my men for it, but I went to the premises first, and treated with the prisoner for the purchase of it—I was to give him 4 l. 15 s. for it—he asked 6 l. or 7 l.—the mangle came to my shop, and I paid for it—there was a mangle claimed by Mr. White, and he removed one—but I mean to swear he has removed a wrong mangle.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the one removed by Mr. White the one you got from the prisoner? A. It was not, it was one I purchased of a man named Webb, who is here—the one I bought of the prisoner has the name of Moore croft on it, and the one they swore to was one of Baker's make—the mangle identified by Mr. White has one of the ears in two pieces—I was not present when the prosecutor identified the mangle, I was in bed—I did not object to their coming on my premises.
COURT. Q. I suppose you have lost the best mangle? A. It is, a great deal.
HENRY WILLIAM DUBOIS ( police-constable N 14.) I took the prisoner, and told him what he was charged with—I found this mangle in Dawsou's cellar, and took it to the station—I said to the prisoner, "Is this the mangle?"—he said, "I believe it is." MR. HORRY called
SAMUEL WEBB . I am a launderer, living in Ebury-farm-row—I have seen the mangle which is identified by the prosecutor—I sold it to Mr. Dawson, about six or seven weeks ago—I know it by two particular marks on the top—one is some nails which I had put in inside, where the panel slips in, and one of the ears is in two pieces, when it is off—I had these three nails put into the top, and there is a very black patch
on it that I scraped with a knife, and put putty and oil in, to stop the worm-holes, and the pencil-marks on the crosspieces, and where the balance is put in, one of the pieces is shaken off.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. How long have you been a landerer? A. The last eight years—I never knew the prisoner before I saw him at Worship-street—this mangle came from Tatters ell's, at Hyde-park-corner—I bought it in the yard about a fortnight before I sold it to Dawson, for 2 l. 7 s, 6 d.—a man named Loosely was present—I hired a cart of Hamar, a green-grocer—I gave him 2 s., and the man half-a-crown, for helping me, and telling me it was to be sold—it was removed at four o'clock in the afternoon—there was no balance in it then—I sold it to Davson for 4 l. 7 s.—I never bought and sold but two before—I never saw Dawson till I had the mangle to sell—I put a ticket in the window, "Baker's patent mangle for sale"—I had a great many applications, but could not get the price—Dawson came to me afterwards, and asked me if I should know it again, I said, "Yes, from a hundred"—I have been with Dawson several times—I came here with him—we have had very little together—on Thursday I brought my own dinner—on Friday we had a little repast, which Dawson paid for, and on Saturday—there were some nails in the mangle I sold—one was a tin tack—the black patch is on the crosspiece which the panel goes in.
MR. HORRY. Q. Was it Mr. Dawson came to you about the mangle for the prisoner's friends? A. Mr. Dawson—I bought it to sell again.
JOIIPH LOOSRLBY . I am a carpenter, living in Queen-street, Pimlico—I was present when Webb bought a mangle—I marked it with my pencil on the cross pieces—one of the ears was in two pieces—I knew the mangle for four or five years at Mr. Tatters all's, at Hyde Park-corner.
MR. CHAENOCK. Q. How long have you known Webb? A. Between four and five years—the marks were put on when I took the mangle to pieces, that I might put it up again.
THOMAS EDWARDS . I am a carman, living in Queen-street, Hoxton-market—I took this mangle from Mr. Webb to Dawson's—I know it by taking it from Mr. Webb to Mr. Dawson's—I put it up in the cellar, and there it remained, till Mr. White came and took it away—it had an oak bed—Mr. Dawson applied to me to come here—he sent me to Webb's for it.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. How many mangles has Mr. Dawson? A. Twenty or thirty—there is a great similarity between mangles—these are all put in the lower part of Mr. Dawson's premises for safe custody—I am the man that fetched a mangle from Three Cups-alley, on or about the 28th of April—I was not told that it was not the property of the man that sold it, to my recollection—it is often the case, where I fetch mangles and goods from, there is wrangling and jangling amongst the people—there are often disputes about the property ray master purchases—it makes no impression on me—I was not told it was stolen, and would be claimed by the owners—Mr. White's name was not mentioned—I fetched it about seven o'clock in the morning.
HARRIET BURLEY . I am the wife of a plasterer, living in Cock-lane, Church-lane, Shore ditch. I know the house in Three Cups-alley—I have been several times in the room of that house, where the mangle was, and used it—the one now produced is not it—I know it because one of our
little children put the candle on the end of the mangle, it fell down, and burnt a part as big as a sixpence.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. When were you applied to come up? A. When the prisoner was taken up—I have known his wife twenty-two years, and she said to me, "Dear me, father is taken up for selling a mangle, and they have brought the wrong mangle"—I was sitting with his wife when he was taken, and they took me—I had two hours imprisonment—I used to have the mangle to mangle my things, and pay so much a dozen to Mrs. Seat herd—I remember no other mangle there but this—I had not been at the premises before the prisoner came—I am sure there was only one mangle. MR. CHARNOCK called
PETER HILLS . I know the mangle that was on Mr. White's premise, in Three Cups-alley—I had known it nine or ten years—I hare pot it up ten or twelve times—it belonged originally to Mr. York Haltom, who is deceased—I went to Dawson's and saw it there, and identified it among many others—I have no doubt of it—I know it, because I repaired those places in it
Cross-examined. Q. When did you put it together last? A. About two years ago—I have not put up many mangles since that—I was foreman to Mr. York Halton—I have put it up in Three Cups-alley, in Kent-street, and Thames-street, City—it had been much used, but was standing by for years—the bed of it was oak—a person who works for Mr. White, asked me if I should know it—I said, yes—I met him and the policeman and went and identified it—when it was put up the last time, it was for Mr. Halton.
ANN TOSSETT . I was present on the 28th of April, at seven o'clock in the morning, when the cart was at the door—there were other peoun there—I said to the men who were removing the mangle, that they were removing the mangle belonging to Mr. White of Newington-causeway—I followed the cart to Mr. Dawson's, and told them the same as I did at the house, that they were taking away stolen property.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you live in Three Cups-alley? A. Yes—I pay no rent—I have been there two years, and have paid no rent during that time—I was at my own door when I spoke to the men—I cannot tell whether they gave any answer to me—they paid no attention to what I said.
GUILTY . Aged 63. Confined Six Months.

1921. ELLEN SIMS was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 2 shillings, the monies of William Joliife, from his person.
WILLIAM JOLIFFE . I live in Mitchell-street, St. Luke's, and an doll-maker. On the 22nd of May, about three o'clock in the morning, I was in a coffee-shop taking a cup of coffee—the prisoner was there, and a young fellow insulted her very grossly indeed, and I took it up—we staid there about half an hour—the prisoner went out, and the man also—they got into the street, and were going to fight—I went out in about twenty minutes—I saw the prisoner, and she took me to a little house in George-yard,
where they take spirits at night—I treated her with a quartern of gin—she then wanted another—I said, "I shall not give you any more here, but if you will come out I don't mind"—we then got into the passage—she put her arm round my neck—she put her hand in my left-hand waistcoat-pocket, and robbed me of 21 s.—she then went out—I went out, saw her running down the court, and accused her of robbing me of 2 s.—she said it was more than she knew, she had 2 s. that she got from a gentleman, and 4 s. that she got from a gentleman in Old-street; and if she had 2 s. of mine, she would give it up—but she did not give it me, and I charged a policeman with her—I had seen the 2 s. safe within two minutes before I was with her—I was perfectly sober—no one could have taken them but her—I was in no company but hers.
Prisoner. This man wanted to take very indecent liberties with me, and a young person who was with me said, "Don't have anything to do with that man." I never asked him for any gin; I went home to a gentleman's residence in Gos well-street, and he gave me 4 s;. I then went to have a cap of coffee; a female there came out with me, and the prosecutor asked me to go and have a drop of gin at a place where he said he had been on the Sunday morning.
HARRIET HAYWARD . I searched the prisoner at the station—I found a shilling and a halfpenny in her stocking, and 1 s. 4 d. in her hand—she said she would have given the old man his 2 s. if it had not been for that b—r of an inspector.
Prisoner. I said I would sooner give him 2 s. than I would be locked up till Monday—you know that was it. Witness. I have spoken nothing but the truth—she was dreadfully drunk, and the language she made use of to the inspector, and every body else was dreadful.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Three Months.

1923. WILLIAM STOKES was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Frederick Harris.
, MARIA HARRIS. I am the wife of Frederick Harris; we live in Lisson Grove. On the evening of the 6th of June I was in our parlour, and the window was open—my niece was walking in front of the houses—I told her to throw a handkerchief down the area, which she did—it was a yellow silk one—it was left there, as I forgot to take it up—I did not see it again till I was called to the station next morning—this now produced is it.
GEORGE CHARSIEY . I live in North-wharf-road, Paddington. On the morning of the 7th of June I was going along Lisson-grove—I passed the house where Mrs. Harris lodges—I saw the prisoner and three others, who were standing outside the area—I did not say anything to them then, but
I saw them a second time, and asked what they were doing—the prisoner answered that Thompson, who was with him, had dropped his handkerchief and cap down the area—I looked, and saw these two handkerchiefs down the area, this yellow one and this red one—I said to the prisoner "Well, if they belong to you I will see you harmless, you may go down and get them"—he crept through one of the broken rails, went down, and brought them up; but immediately he got into the area he took up this yellow handkerchief, and put it into his own cap, and the cap that was down the area he gave up to Thompson—he then came out, and walked with the other three about forty yards—he then started off running, and so did the other—my suspicion were roused, and I pursued, and caught the prisoner in John-street, New-road—I asked him how it was he ran away—he made no answer, but went on—I followed him to York-street—I then told him he had been doing something wrong, and to give me up the property—he gave me this yellow handkerchief—I said, "Where is the other!" and he gave it me—I said, "I believe this is what you call a diver, is it not?"—he said it was—it has a piece of list tied to it, and some pins at the other end, to serve as hooks.
JOHN HEFFERMAN ( police-constable D 99.) I took the prisoner, and asked how he became possessed of these handkerchiefs—he said the yellow one belonged to Thompson, and the other was his own.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw the handkerchief down the area; Thompson said it was his, and if I would go and get it, he would give me 6 d. for myself; I got down, and he walked away; I came up, and ran after him.
GUILTY . 18.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1924]
1924. JAMES FORD was indicted for embezzlement.
CHARLES SMITH . I am a baker, and live at Chiswick—the prisoner was my servant. On the 16th of May I sent him to Strand-on-the-Green, to Mr. Jeffries and Mr. Heman with bills—he was to receive the money from them—he returned, and worked that week—on the 23rd I sent him out with bread, and to deliver bills again—he returned at half-past six o'clock—next morning I met him, and asked him where he was going, and he said he was going to send a man to do his work—he had not then paid me either of these sums.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you ask when he returned on the 16th, if he had received the money? A. He did not receive these sums till the 23rd, when he went out with his bread—he returned, and set his sponge, but did not book his bread—I did not see him again till the Thursday when he was given into custody—a person named Burt offered me the money that same afternoon, and I said I would settle it before the Magistrate, and he would not allow it—I was on friendly terms with the prisoner—I gave him notice on Saturday, the 21st, to quit me on the Saturday following.
COURT. Q. This offer of the money by Burt was after the prisoner was in custody? A. Yes.
ABRAHAM JEFFRIES . I live with my father, at Strand-on-the-Green, near Kew-bridge. My father deals with the prosecutor for bread—on the 16th of May the prisoner brought a bill which was not paid—on the 23rd he brought another, and I paid him 14 s., all in silver, for the two bills.
HANNAH HEMMINS . I am the wife of John Hemmins, he deals with the prosecutor for bread—the prisoner came to me with a bill on the 16th of May. and on the 23rd I paid him 8 s
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1925]
1925. CHARLES PRICE and SAMUEL PRICE were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 peck of oats, value 1 s;. and 2 quarts of split beans, value 3 d;. the goods of William Ralph Cartwright, the master of Samuel Price.
THOMAS HITCHCOCK . I am coachman to William Ralph Cartwright; his stable is at No. 17, North-road, near Hereford-street, Park-lane—the prisoner Samuel Price has been helping me for three seasons in the stable, and I have seen the other prisoner who called to see him—I saw the policeman have some oats and beans at Marlborough-street, and they are now here—I had some oats and beans at my master's stable on the 6th of June, but I cannot say I should know them again—I cannot say whether they were of the sort of those now produced, nor whether these came out of my stable—I had oats in the bin in the stable, but I should not like to say they were of this sort, because there are two or three more who have the same provision—I cannot say whether these are anything like them or not, nor whether these are the same sort of oats or not—there were some oats in the bin in the stable, that we used to feed the horses, but I cannot say that I missed any—we had a few beans up in the loft over the stable—they were split beans—I cannot say whether they were the same sort as those that are here—mine were bruised, and these are braised, but I cannot say whether they are the same sort—I have been with Mr. Cartwright turned of sixteen years, and have been in the habit of feeding my horses with oats and beans all that time—I can tell one sort from another, but there are two or three about me that use the same—I look at the oats and beans that come in, and feed the horses when I am at home—I have no opinion at all as to whose property this is—I never said I had—I was examined at Marlborough-street—I did not say that I believed this to be my master's property—what I said was taken down in writing, and read over to me—this is my signature to this deposition.
Q. It is here stated, "The oats and beans now produced, about a peck together, I believe to be my master's property," is that true? A. No—I said I could not say—I went to bed about half-past ten o'clock, and heard no more till about five in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PEENDERGAST. Q. Your master does not take any notice of this prosecution? A. No—he wished me to appear at Marlborough-street—I said I could say nothing, I was in bed asleep.
JOHN SPONG ( police-constable C 139.) I was in the North-road, near Hereford-street, on the 6th of June about one o'clock in the morning—I saw the prisoner Charles Price come out of the stable No. 17—he had this bag—he was carrying it towards Park-lane—I stopped him and asked him what he had got—he said, "Nothing much; it is all right"—I asked him where he got it from—he said, if I would come he would show me—I went with him—he took me to No. 17 stable, knocked at the door, and called "sam"—Samuel Price then opened the door—Rogers came up and asked
Samuel Price whose corn it was—Samuel Price said, "It is mine—the prisoners were then taken to the station—I took the bag—this is it—I contained these oats and beans.
Cross-examined. Q. There are a great number of stables there, are there not? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Is there more than one No. 17? A. No.
THOMAS STEWART ROGERS ( police-sergeant C 56.) About one o'clock in the morning, on the 6th of June, I saw Charles Price walking with Spong towards No. 17 stable-door—he knocked at the door, and called " Sam" —the prisoner Samuel Price then opened the door—I asked Samuel, in Charles's presence, whose corn it was—he said it was his—I asked where it came from—he said, "From here, and I am going to send it to my horse at Paddington, and it is all right"—I asked him if the stables were his—he said no—I took Charles into custody, and at the time Samuel snatched the corn out of Charles's hand, and took it into the stable.
THOMAS ENGLISH ( police-constable C 37.)I was at the station, and had Charles to take to Marlborough-street office—as we were going he said to me, "The best thing I can do is to speak the truth; it is no me to tell any d—d lies about it"—I said, "I know nothing about it, you must make what defence you think proper."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1926]
1926. HENRY JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of June, 1 purse, value 1 s., 6 half-crowns, and 1 sixpence; the property of Rachael Wilson, from her person.
RACHAEL WILSON . I am a widow, and live in Little Compton-street On the 10th of June I was in Lincoln's Inn-fields alone, about nine o'clock in the evening—it was on the same side that Turnstile is—I perceived two persons behind me—I had not proceeded far before I missed a weight from my pocket—I then turned and saw two persons so near me as almost to touch me, and the prisoner was next to me—I saw my purse in his hand, and saw him pass it to the other man who was with him—I went up to the prisoner, and told him he had robbed me—the instant I said that, the other man ran away—the prisoner said he had not robbed me—a mob collected round us, and the officer came up—I gave him in charge—I had in my purse six half-crowns and a sixpence—it was a brown silk purse—I am certain I had it safe when I turned into Lincoln's Inn-fields—I know by the weight of it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was not this later than nine o'clock in the evening? A. It might be a little later—the prisoner did not offer to run away—the mob gathered round—I had not noticed those persons behind me till I got into the square—there were other person coming towards us, but not going the other way—the prisoner had my purse in his hand—it was not dark, it was twilight—I do not know how they got at it—my pocket was not cut—I actually saw my purse in the prisoner's hand.
ADOLPHUS LONSDALE ( police-constable F 110.) I was on duty, and saw the prisoner, who was detained by the prosecutrix and a gentleman—the prisoner said he had not taken the purse.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1927]
1927. LEONORA CROSBY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 bonnet, value 6 s;. 5 caps, value 1 s. 6 d;. 1 towel, value 6 d;. and 2 collars, value 6 d.; the goods of Charles Evans; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MARY ANN EVANS . I am the wife of Charles Evans; we live at Chelsea. On Wednesday morning, the 1st of June, the prisoner came to see one of my lodgers—I saw her going up stairs, and asked who she was going to—she said, "Mrs. Donohue"—I said she was not at home—she went up stairs, and returned in two or three minutes, then went away, and returned in twenty minutes or half an hour—she then went up stairs and stopped four or five minutes, then came down and went away—when she came first to our house she had something in her apron, but she appeared to go away the second time with a larger bundle than she brought in—it was then between eleven and twelve o'clock—I went up between two and three, to my bedroom, which is adjoining the lodger's room that the prisoner went to—I missed a bonnet, a towel, five caps, and two collars, which had been hanging outside my bed-room door—I had seen them safe between ten and eleven the same morning—these produced are them—this bonnet is a tuscan bonnet, but it has been cleaned since it was taken from my house—I know it is mine—this is the lutestring that I lined it with—these are my collars and caps—they are my own make, and I can swear to them. Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What particular thing can you swear to? A. This is a French cap, and these others—I made every one of them myself, except this marked one—here is the ribbon that went over this cap, and I have a piece of the same kind of ribbon here—I know this bonnet more by the lining, but I know the bonnet is the same—there is only my husband and me, and one lodger up stairs, in our house.
SARAH RIEORDEN . I live in George-street, Chelsea, and keep a straw-bonnet shop. On the 1st of June the prisoner brought this bonnet to me—I had not seen her before—it was lined with this silk that is here—she asked me to clean the bonnet and turn it for her by the following Saturday, and if she did not call on the Saturday I was to send it her—she gave her name as Mrs. Crosby, 28, New-road—I had to take it to pieces—I was cleaning bonnets at the time she came, and I did this one immediately—I afterwards saw the policeman, and he told me not to go on with it—I took it to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it was the prisoner? A. Became she was very particular in trying on a shape—this was a very particular bonnet—it was short at the ears—it was quite a fright—they are not worn small now—the prisoner was with me about ten minutes—I am sure it was her—I knew her by her dress and her face.
JAMES BRADLEY ( police-constable B 134.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 1st June—I found these caps in her possession—I kept them—she was discharged, and then taken again.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you apprehend her? A. In Lower Sloane-street—she lives in the New-road—I know her, and know her husband—he has tried to compromise this case—the prisoner said these things were her own, and stuck to it, till I took them from her at Queen-square
WILLIAM BROWN ( police-sergeant B 9.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Clerk en well Session-house ( read)—I was at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY Aged 28—. confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1928]
1928. ELIZA ANN CRAWLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of May, 2 sheets, value 4 s., and 1 pillow, value 3 s., the goods of Benjamin Levy.
MARY LEVY . I am the wife of Benjamin Levy; we live in Albion-place, Clerkenwell. The prisoner took a furnished apartment of mine? there was a man with her whom she represented as her husband, but he afterwards said he was not—that man has been acquitted—as well as I remember, the prisoner was at my house on the night of the 29th of May but she did not sleep there every night—when I let my room to them these articles were in it—the prisoner left on the 5th of June, and I missed a pair of sheets that day—I had not seen them from the time I let the room, which was on the 30th of April—I did not see the prisoner again till she was in custody, but she had called at my house in my absence.
Prisoner. You gave me the pillow about a fortnight after I was in the room. Witness. I believe it was in the room when you took it.
MICHAEL PARKER MALONEY . I am in the service of Mr. Rawlins, a pawnbroker. I produce these two sheets, which were pawned by the prisoner, I believe, in the name of Ann Smith, on the 30th of May—I gave her this duplicate—( looking at one),
BENJAMIN MECHAM . I am in the service of a pawnbroker. I have a pillow, pawned on the 30th of May, I believe by the prisoner, in the name of Ann Smith—I gave her this duplicate for it.

ANDREW TWEEDALB ( police-constable C 222.) The prisoner was given to me by the prosecutor—she said something about being forgiven—I took four duplicates from the prisoner—these two belong to these things.

[trialAccount t18420613-1929]
1929. JANE ELLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 half-sovereign, value 10s., the money of John Brady, from his person.
JOHN BRADY . I live in George-yard, Lombard-street, and am a boot-maker. I was in the City-road about half-past eleven o'clock at night, on the 6th of June, walking home—I had been supping with a friend—the prisoner came up and laid hold of my arm, and asked how I did— she said she was very drunk, and would I see her home—I said, "No," and persuaded her to go home—she still asked me to go with her—I said I had no money, and could not—she then put her arm round my waist, and in fact all over me, and appeared in a druken state—I then found I had lost a half-sovereign out of my waistcoat pocket—she had not hold of me then—I had had my fingers in my waistcoat pocket three minutes before, and felt it safe—I charged her with robbing me—she said "what? let us go and have something to drink"—I still refused, and said, "Here is a policeman coming towards us, if you will not give me back the half-sovereign I will give you in charge"—I was quite sober, and the prisoner appeared more sober then—I gave her in charge—I do not know what became of my half-sovereign.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where had you been supping? A. In London-wall, and this was in the City-road—this did not take five
minutes in all—I told her I had no money, but that was to get rid of her—I had a sixpence and some halfpence in another pocket—I could not 20 away from her at first, and when she let me go I had lost my half-sovereign—I did not meet any other woman that night.
JOHN HARVEY . I am a policeman. I was on duty—the prosecutor gave the prisoner into my charge for stealing a half-sovereign from his waistcoat pocket—she said she knew nothing of it—she was searched at the station, and no half-sovereign was found—she had been drinking, but was not drunk—the prosecutor was quite sober.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she not tell you she had 2 s.? A. Yes, and that was true.
NOT GUILTY .

1930. HENRY WARNER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 saddle, value 1 l. 15 s., the goods of David Strachan .
JOHN LINES . I am a porter to Mr. Owen, of New Bond-street—he keeps a shawl warehouse. On the 6th of June, about a quarter-past twelve o'clock, I saw the prisoner and two more men at the corner of Blenheim-street, Bond-street—the prisoner went and sat on the stone opposite our shop door, and one of the others sat on Mr. Phillips's step—the third man went past Mr. Strachan't shop, and took a saddle that was outside Mr. Strachan's door, tied with a string—he gave it to the prisoner, who ran off with it—I gave information, and we followed him to Oxford-street—he then put it on his head, went up to Holles'-street, threw it on in omnibus, and then ran up Holies-street.
THOMAS ROSSITER . I am in the employ of Mr. David Strachan, a saddler, in New Bond-street. On the 6th of June I received information, and missed a saddle—I followed the prisoner to Oxford-street—I saw him with the saddle on his head—he threw it on an omnibus—I called to the omaibus to stop, and took the saddle—the prisoner ran up Holies-street, and was taken.
JOHN SPIERS ( police-constable D 142.) I took the prisoner in charge. Prisoner's Defence. A man asked me to carry it to the omnibus, which I did.

1931. CATHERINE CARTY was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 9 yards of calico, value 2 s., the goods of Adam Bird.
WILLIAM HARRISON . I am shopman to Adam Bird, a linen-draper, in High-street, Whitechape1. On Friday evening, the 13th of May, about nine o'clock, I was inside the shop, and saw the prisoner take a piece of calico, which was hanging on a rail inside the door—I went up, and asked what she wanted—she said, "A piece of calico"—I said I thought she had a piece—she said she had not—I took her inside, and this piece of calico fell from under her shawl—it is my master's.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. HOW do you know it is Mr. Bird's calico? A. Because I saw her take it from the rail, and it
had a ticket on it—I saw the ticket on it at the time it fell from her—I swear I saw her take it.
CORNELIUS FOAY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—I saw another piece of calico sticking out of her breast—I said, "You appear bulky, what have you got about you?"—she said, " Nothing,"
Cross-examined. Q. Did she appear the worse for liquor? A. No—she said her mother lived at Poplar, but did not say where—she said she herself lived anywhere, and sometimes slept at one place, sometimes it another.
GUILTY . Aged 12.— Confined Three Months.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner.)

1932. JAMES STEWART was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 copper, value 7 s., the goods of John Abbott, being fixed to a building,—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
JOHN ABBOTT . I keep the Harrow public-house, at Poplar. I missed my copper—I believe this one now produced to be it—I have no marks on it—it exactly fits the brickwork—I gave the prisoner no authority to take if.
SUSANNA ABBOTT . The copper was fixed at our house—I did not authorize the prisoner to remove it, or mend it—I saw it safe last on Tuesday, the 31st of May.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not admit before the Magistrate that you might have given me leave to take it? A. No—I cannot recollect that I ever did—you have several times done small jobs for me—yon have sometimes taken tins when I told you.
CHARLES AGAR ( police-constable K 211.) About ten o'clock at night on the 1st of June, I stopped the prisoner with this copper on his shoulder—he said he bought it for 1 s. 6 d., of a lad—I said he must go to the station—he then said it was a job, Mrs. Abbott knew he had got it, and had spoken to him about two months ago.
Prisoner*s Defence. She told me three times within the last six or seven months, that she wished the copper to be done; I said I meant to take it away that night, and I understood her to say, "Very well."
GUILTY . Aged 32. —Recommended to mercy. — Confined Nine
Months.

1934. GEORGE LANGLY was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, at St. Pancras, 9 forks, value 7 l. 5 s.; and 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 15 s; the goods of Edward Lindsey, in his dwelling-house; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH HEWITT . I am servant to Edward Lindsey, who lives at No. 14, Mornington-crescent, Hampstead-road—it is his dwelling-house, and is in the parish of St. Pancras. On Saturday evening, the 4th of June, at a quarter before six o'clock, there was a ring at the door—I opened it, and the prisoner appeared—he said he came for a bonnet to be cleaned—I asked him inside, went up, and asked my mistress—she said he had made a mistake—I went down again, and the prisoner was on the mat, where I had left him—I said he had made a mistake—he said he was sorry he had given me the trouble—I opened the door, and he went off—the plate-basket was about four yards from where he stood—there were a dozen forks in it, and the sugar-tongs, when I left it—when he was gone I looked at it again—nine forks and the tongs were gone—they were worth about 8 l —no one could have taken them but him—I have not seen them since.
Prisoner. Q. There were other servants in the house? A. There are two; but the coachman was in the stable, and the cook in the kitchen—I am positive you are the person.
JOHN JAMBS ALLEN ( police-constable E 150.) I took the prisoner—I told him what it was for—he said he had never been there at all—on the road to the police-court he said, "If I am sworn to, do you think I shall be transported?"—I said I did not know—he said, if I would not say any thing about what I knew of him, he would go to the Marine Society. Prisoner's Defence. I was not there at all.
THOMAS LANE ( police-constable E 52.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY.* Aged 14.— Transported for Ten Years.—Convict Ship.

1935. GEORGE COOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 2 moulds, value. 5 l., the goods of Daniel Merritt, his master.
DANIEL MERRITT . I am a modeller, living in Foley-street—the prisoner was in my employ. On the 6th of April I told him I had missed a great quantity of moulds—he said it was very strange—I discharged him—I went to his lodgings, and found these two moulds in the water-closet—they are mine—no other person in the house could have taken them. ANNE, BLAKE. I found these in the water-closet—I told the prisoner's mother he had been robbing his master—he denied it, and made his escape when the officer arrived at the house—I had seen these two moulds lying on the fire-place in the room where he lived.
CHARLES STANTON . I live in Harper-street, Fitzroy-square. I saw the prisoner coming out of Roberts'-mews—he said, "Oh, Charley, I expect to get nailed "—I said, "What for?"—he said, "I have been taking some moulds of my master."

[trialAccount t18420613-1936]
1936. ANN HALPIN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of June 1/2 an ounce weight of tobacco, value 1 d;. 1 knife, value 1 d;. 1 purse, value 6 d;. 16 shillings, 20 sixpences, 6 pence, and 4 halfpence; the property of Samuel Bartrick, from his person.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL BARTRICK . I am a labourer, living in Tothill-street. Between eight and nine o'clock in the evening of Saturday, the 11th of June, I was at the Brill public-house, Brewer's-street, Somers-town—I had a purse in my right-hand trowsers' pocket, containing 16 s., and 10 s. loose in the same pocket, all in silver—I had some halfpence, a knife, and some tobacco in my waistcoat-pocket—I had had the knife twelve months—I should know it well—the prisoner asked me for something to drink—I gave her a drink out of my pot—we then went to the Bee-hive, and I gave her part of two pints—I went to a room in Middlesex-street with her—in going the said she had no light in the room—I gave her a halfpenny, and she bought a candle—I then went to a brothel with her in another street—I did not take off my clothes—I went to sleep—I was awoke by the woman of the house in about an hour, and found my right-band trowsers' pocket was turned out, and nothing in it—all was gone, and my knife, tobacco, and halfpence—I went back to Middlesex-street, where I had been before, and saw the prisoner lying on the floor—she appeared very tipsy—I staid there that night, and the next morning I saw her in the passage, and charged her with having robbed me—she said she had not—she then went into a privy in the back yard—she tried to shut the door, but I kept it open, and saw her take my purse from her left breast, unpin it, and take the silver out—I rushed in, and she put the silver down her back—I do not know what she did with the purse—she came into the room again, and staid some time, then went to the privy again—I followed, and saw her hand down the privy—she came out wiping her hand—she went to a coffee-shop—I kept her in sight till I got a policeman—he found my knife on her—this is it.
Prisoner. It was another young woman that he slept with all night
Witness. The other woman was in the room, but I did not sleep with her.
JOSEPH YOUNG ( police-constable S 152.) I was called in on Sunday morning, the 12th of June—I took the prisoner and another woman—the prisoner was searched by a woman, who gave me, in her presence, 2 s. and 3/4 d.—the other woman had the knife—she said the prisoner gave it her, with 7 s. and some tobacco—the prisoner said, "You ought to have had 11 s."—some copper money and tobacco was produced.
Prisoner's Defence. Next morning he came, and said, "You have robbed me;" I said, "How could I, when I have not been in the room with you?" the other woman took the empty room, and I was to pay half the rent, as we had come out of the infirmary the night before; she was out with this man when I came home, and I sat down in the passage.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1937]
1937. ANN MARSHALL was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of June, 8 handkerchiefs, value 3 s. 4 d., the goods of George Briscombe.
WILLIAM BRISCOMBE . I live with my father, George Briscombe. On the 13th of June the prisoner came to his shop—I lost these eight handkerchiefs produced, which have my father's marks on them, and are his.
ELIZABETH HARMAW . I am a searcher at the station. I searched the prisoner on the 13th, and found six handkerchiefs, in one piece, on her.
Prisoner. I met a man selling these; he asked half-a-crown for them; I bought them of him for 2 s., to sell again.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1938]
1938. JAMES KAIN and JOHN FARRELL were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 9 ivory balls, value 1 l. 7 s. 6 d., the goods of Robert Edward Docking.
ROBERT EDWARD DOCKING . I am the landlord of the Coach and Horses, in Bunhill-row. On the 6th of June the prisoners were drinking in the tap-room—they afterwards went up stairs to play at bagatelle—I have lost nine ivory balls, worth 1 l. 7 s. 6 d.—they have not been found.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Is this a public room? A. Yes—the prisoners went into the room about four o'clock, and went away about half-past five—I was sitting in the bar all the evening, and not a soul went up stairs after them, and none but them—I missed the balls at eight o'clock.
JOHN REIS . I and three fellow-workmen were in the room—I remember the prisoners playing with these balls at bagatelle—Farrell was the last person who was playing—Kain came about half-an-hour after the other was there—I left them at half-past five o'clock—Farrell was amazing himself with the balls, and Kain looking on—no persons were left in the room but the two prisoners—my fellow-workmen went with me—I saw eight white balls there, and one red one.
JAMES GROOM . I am pot-boy at this house. Farrell was the last person I saw playing in this room, and Kain was looking on—four of De la Rue's men were in the room—they went, and the two prisoners only were left—I saw them go away after the other men were gone—I cannot swear whether any other persons came after they left.
JAMES BRANNAN . I am a police-sergeant I took the prisoners—they denied it.
KAIN— GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Nine Months.
FARRELL*— GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years. (There was another indictment against the prisoners.)
