[trialAccount t18420613-1940]
1940. THOMAS KIRBY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of April, 12 handkerchiefs, value 1 l. 7 s.; 18 yards of net, value 8 s. 4 d.; 180 yards of lace, value 5 l.; 360 yards of quilting, value 1 l. 1 s. 6 d.; and 12 pairs of cuffs, value 5 s. 6 d.; the goods of Richard Groncock and others.
MESSRS. RYLAND and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM OSBORNE . I am in the service of Richard Groncock—he has two partners—they are warehousemen, in Bow Churchyard. We have customers of the name of Pearce and Woodward, at Taunton—on Saturday the 30th of April, a person came from their firm to purchase goods—I sold some goods, consisting of two cards, of one sort of blonde lace, twenty-four cards of another sort, three pieces of thread lace not placed on cards, and twelve handkerchiefs—after I had sold them I gave them to Hill to be entered.
BENJAMIN HILL . I am apprentice at Messrs. Groncock's and others. On the 30th of April some goods were purchased by Pearce and Woodward, and handed to me by Osborn—I put them into a basket, and took them to the entering-room—Mr. Hibberd entered them.
JAMES HIBBERD . I am assistant at the prosecutor's. On the 30th of April, Hill brought me some goods in a basket—I called them over to the clerk, and saw them entered in the book—they were then put into paper, and delivered to John Hill.
JOHN HILL . I am in the service of Messrs. Groncock. I received the parcel—I put it into brown paper, and directed it to "Pearce and Woodward, Taunton," and delivered it to Charles Garland, to take it to Sharland's, in Bishopsgate-street, to go to Taunton.
CHARLES GARLAND . I am a carman. I received a brown paper parcel to go to Pearce and Woodward, at Taunton—I placed it, with other parcels, in my cart—I went with it to go to Bishopsgate-street—I had occasion to call at a house in Leadenhall-street, for eight or ten minutes—when I came out I missed this parcel.
GEORGE TREW . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner on the 5th of May—some policemen remained on the prisoner's premises till the 7th—on that morning I examined the premises, and found these two cards of lace and blonde in a little box behind the counter—I took them to Mr. Osborne, at Messrs. Groncock's.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. At what time did you take the prisoner? A. About nine o'clock in the morning—he was in custody when I found these things.
WILLIAM OSBORNE re-examined. These are some of the articles which were put into Messrs. Pearce and Woodward's parcel.
(James Veitch, wholesale haberdasher, Noble-street, Cheapside; Hussey Chapman, Manchester warehouseman, Hart-street, Wood-street; William Matthews, linen-draper, Bishop's Stortford; James Butcher, haberdasher, Greenwich; Richard Banton, Wood-street, Cheapside; and—Morley, Manchester warehouseman, Wood-street, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 43.
[trialAccount t18420613-1941]
1941. THOMAS KIRBY was again indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 1st of April, of an evil-disposed person, 361bs. weight of worsted, value 3 l.; 360 sheets of wadding, value 2 l. 16 s.; 576 yards of wire ribbon, value 5 s. 4 d.; 860 yards of stay-binding, value 8 s. 3 d.; 596 pieces of tape, value 34 l.; 34 pairs of gloves, value 2 l. 8 s.; and 25 gross of buttons, value 2 l. 10 s.; the goods of William Hutchinson; well knowing them to have been stolen.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Abraham George.
MESSRS. RYLAND and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK PIGGOTT . I am in the service of Leaf and Company, of Old Change, London. William Hutchinson, of Reading, is a customer of my master's—I received an order for goods on the 30th of March, to be sent to Mr. Hutchinson—I selected a part of the goods, three dozen balls of worsted, thirty dozen of wadding, and twenty or thirty gross of ribbon wire, some tape, stay-binding, and some gloves—I took them to the order-room, to be entered in the journal—Mordant was engaged in the order-room—it was his duty to enter them.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you venture to swear positively to these things? A. Certainly not—Leaf and Co. are in a large way of business—they have bought stocks in cases of bankruptcy.
WILLIAM MORANT . I am in the service of Leaf and Co.—I remember an order for goods from Mr. Hutchinson, of Reading—some goods were brought me by Piggott—I called them over to the clerk of the books, and delivered them to Swamell the packer.
EDWIN FREDERICK WINSTON . I am in the service of Leaf and Co. I was at that time clerk of the books—I remember entering this order—I saw no more of the goods after they had been sold and entered.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you recollect anything of the order, or do you only find there are entries in the book made by yourself? A. Yes.
HENRY SWAMELL . I am packer in this establishment. I received from Morant a parcel of goods for Hutchinson—I put it into a wrapper, and directed it for "William Hutchinson, Reading"—it was delivered to Virgin, the carman, to be taken to Gerard's Hall.
WILLIAM VIRGIN . I am carman to Leaf and Co. I received a packet addressed to Hutchinson, at Reading, from Swamell, on the 30th of March—I took it to the office at Gerrard's Hall, and left it there.
JONAS CHBNY . I am porter at the wagon-office at Gerrard's Hall. On the 30th of March I received a package addressed to "William Hutchinson, Reading"—I weighed it on the 31st of March, saw it loaded on the wagon, and helped to bind it—the wagon started for Reading about three o'clock in the afternoon—the package was in it then.
THOMAS SHEPHERD . I am wagoner for Reading. On the 31st of March I saw a truss safe in the wagon to go to Reading—I cannot read—it was placed on the corner behind, and was fastened with a rope—I met somebody near Cranford-bridge, I asked for a light, and then missed the package, which was safe when I went out of Brentford.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How many parcels were in the wagon? A. I cannot say.
GEORGE TREW ( police-constable H 125.) I went to the prisoner's premises on the 5th of May, at half-past nine o'clock, and took him—I searched the premises in his presence, and found thirty dozen of wadding under the counter—I asked how he got it—he said he bought it of a man who had lately gone out of business—I asked who the man was—he said I was no Magistrate, he should not answer that question—some of the policemen remained on the premises for nine days—I was thereon the 10th of May—I continued searching, and found some wire-ribbon, some gloves, and some other things—I have had them in my possession ever since.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you not take possessipn of the house itself? A. Yes, by the direction of Mr. Lewis, the inspector, the shop was shut up—the prisoner's wife was there on the 5th—I do not
know whether she was on the 10th—there were two men there at night, and two in the day—I did not take the key of the house—I do not believe the key was taken.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( police-constable R 202.) I was at the search on the 10th—I found six bags of worsted-balls, thirty-six roll of stay-binding, and fifty-eight packets of tape.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you sleep in the house? A. No—I cannot say that the key was taken.
FREDERICK PIGGOTT re-examined. This tape docs not tally in quantity with what I put up—I put up between fifty and seventy dozen—between fifty and sixty are found—they are the same Nos. and description—the wadding is the same in quality and description and number—there were large and small put up for Hutchinson—these bills of worsted are of the same make, and the same sort of goods, and one of the bags is numbered with a "3," which is the same writing as we usually have in our stock, but they come ready written—these balls are about the same quantity.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. With all that, you do not swear to them? A. I do not—we send out enormous packages of these goods.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
(There were four other indictments against the prisoner.)

[trialAccount t18420613-1945]
1945. CORNELIUS SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 4 1/2 lbs. weight of lead, value 8 d., the goods of Edward Sayers, in a certain port of entry and discharge.
JOHN LEITH . I am chief mate of the barque James Cruikshanks. in the West India Docks. The prisoner came on board on the afternoon of the 14th of June, between four and five o'clock—I was on the quay—I saw him walk from the larboard side on the deck towards the starboard—I followed, and as I went to the gangway, he met me, and went over the side again—I heard from those on board that he had something with him—he ran round the course of the sheds, and heaved something into the shed, then came and wished me to search him—I went to the place, and saw this lead picked up—it was the apron of a gun—it came from the star boardside of the deck—it is the property of Captain Edward Sayers.
GEORGE GORDON . I was on board, and saw the prisoner take something off the gun, and gave information to the carpenter, who told the mate—I saw the apron was gone from the gun, and I had seen the prisoner kneeling on the gun—this is the lead—it is used for the apron of the gun.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming there; I passed by the ship; a man opposite spoke to me, and about ten minutes afterwards the policeman came and said, "Halloo, come back;" I stood a moment, and then went; he overhauled me.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Two Months.

1946. HANNAH EDMUNDS and MARY NEWLAND were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 2 pewter pots, value 3 s., the goods of Peter Richard James.
PETER RICHARD JAMES . I am a victualler, and live in Charles-street, Berkley-square—these two pots are mine.
CHARLES DAVIS ( police-constable C 47.) About half-past two o'clock in the morning of the 14th of June I was on duty in Hayes-mews, Berkley-square—I saw Newland take these two quart pots from the chill of a stable window—she ran round from the Mews in the direction of Berkley-square—I took her—Edmunds was in Charles-street, not near enough. to see Newland—I asked Newland to give me the pots that she had just stolen from the window—she gave them to me—Edmunds was there in company with her—they directly ran away—Edmunds was not there when Newland took them.
Newland's Defence. I was out with Edmunds to look after a servant; we saw the pots, and thought we would take care of them till the morning, and the publicans would give us a few halfpence.
EDMUNDS— NOT GUILTY .
NEWLAND— GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18420613-1948]
1948. MATILDA CONNOLLY was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 guard-chain, value 16 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 4 s.; the goods of William Sprake, from his person.
WILLIAM SPRAKE . I am a pot-boy, and live in George-street. St. Giles. On the 2nd of June, about half-past five o'clock in the afternoon, I called at Bainbridge-street to see a friend—I saw the prisoner there—I asked her if George was at home, meaning the person she lived with—she said no—we had something to drink—I then went out by myself, for two boon and a half or three hours—I then returned there, as I wished to see George—I saw the prisoner again—she asked if my watch was safe—I said it was—she said it would be better for me to give it up to her, to give to the landlady to take care of—I said I was quite capable of taking care of it myself—she would insist on taking it, and took it out of my pocket, took the guard off my neck, and the silk handkerchief out of my cap, and went out—I had had a little to drink, but I knew what I was about, and was capable of taking care of myself—she returned to the room in two or three minutes, and I asked if she had left my watch with the landlady—she said, "Yes," and I was satisfied—she then went out—I asked if she was going for George—she said, "Yes," but, instead of that, she fastened me in the room, and left word not to let me out—I afterwards gave her into custody—my watch and handkerchief have not been found.
BRIDGET PRENDERGAST . I was staying with a person in the next room at this house—the prisoner showed me a handkerchief the same evening—I heard her tell the prosecutor that she would take the watch round to Mrs. Sparrow, the landlady—the prisoner was drunk, and I called out, "Don't take the watch to Mrs. Sparrow"—she and the prosecutor had been together, and drinking—they passed as brother and sister—I told her she had better leave the watch with Ann—that was the person I was staying with.
JOSEPH SMALLEY ( police-constable K 175.) I took the prisoner on the 3rd of June—I told her it was for stealing a watch in St. Giles's—she said she had not been in St. Giles's for a fortnight.
Prisoner's Defence. I have not seen it.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Four Months.

ALFRED UNDERWOOD . I am a butcher, and live in William-street, Westminster. About a quarter past eleven o'clock, on the evening of the 11th of June, I was in my shop—I had a leg of mutton there—I saw the prisoner take it off the rail, and run off—I ran and overtook him—he dropped it on the road—a woman picked it up.
Prisoner. He said he would not know his mutton hut he heard the book drop. Witness. No—I said I was satisfied it was mine, but was more confirmed by hearing the hook drop.

1950. EBENEZER WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of April, 3 decanters, value 5 s., the goods of John Curtis.
JOHN CURTIS . The prisoner has been paying his addresses to my daughter for some time. In consequence of what she told me, and having tome duplicates put into my hand, I looked over my stock, and missed some decanters—these are them.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Is your daughter here? A. I believe she is—she has not resided with me for several days—I have not spoken to her since last Friday.
LOUISA CURTIS . The prisoner has been paying his addresses to me for tome months—I received a letter from him, a week ago, containing some duplicates, which I produced to my father—I had authorized the prisoner to take these decanters, and I knew they were out of the house—I was so agitated when I was before the Magistrate, I did not know what I said—I have some recollection of his taking the decanters"
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1951]
1951. AMBROSE BALL was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of May, 1 sack, value 2 s., the goods of John Trulock and another, his masters; and SAMUEL WRIGHT HILL , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS TRULOCK . I am in partnership with my brother John; we are corn-merchants and factors; at Albion-wharf, Old Ford. Ball was our carman—we had no other regular carman—by chance we might send out another—I have seen a corn-sack produced—it is our property, I am certain of it—it is one of some I bought in December—Ball had no authority to deliver it to Hill or to any person whatever—he had no business with this sack on the 27th of May—he might have taken sacks of this description in his business—he had to deliver beans that day, which he did, and they might be in sacks of this kind—he had no right to take anything but beans that day.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How many sacks had you of this description? A. thousand.
MATTHEW YOUNG . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Mile-end-road. About seven o'clock in the morning, on the 26th or 27th of May, I was standing at Canham's door, which is opposite to where the prisoner Hill lives—he is a corn-chandler—I saw a wagon come up driven by Ball, Mr. Trulock's carter—it stopped opposite Hill's door, and a man came out
of Hill's, but I could not say who it was—Ball took a sack off his wagon and put it on the shaft, and the man who came out of Hill's took the sack off the shaft.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. This was done openly in the motning? A. Yes—I had seen Hill before, but I do not know that it was him.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Were you on the opposite side? A. Yes—the road is wide, but not so wide but that I could see Mr. Trulock's name on the wagon—it was about a fortnight or three weeks after, before I gave any information—I have never bad any dealing with Mr. Trulock—I swear that Ball was there—I did not go to him, nor speak to him.
THOMAS EDWARD CANHAM . I am a corn-dealer, and live in Mile-end-road. I was in company with Young on the morning of the 26th or 27th of May, about half-past seven o'clock, at my door—I saw Mr. Trnlock's wagon come up to Hill's door—Hill came out and took a full sack of something of the carman—there were other sacks in the wagon—I mentioned it some days afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You carry on the same business as Hill does? A. Yes, and have for four years or four years and a half—I was talking to Mr. Young about a sample of beans.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was this done immediately? A. Thi sack was given down as soon as the wagon stopped—I did not mention this, because Mr. Hill was a neighbour and in the same line of business.
JAMES PHILLIPS ( police-constable K 20.) I received charge of Bali at Mr. Trulock's wharf, on Friday evening last—Mr. Trulock said to him, "You have been robbing me"—he said, "I don't know what you mean, I know nothing at all about it"—I then went with Mr. John Trulock to Hill's—Hill was not at home, but when he came in, Mr. Trulock said to him, "You have got some property in your house belonging to me"—Hill said, "If this sack is yours you can have it"—the sack was then three-parts full of peas—Hill said he could account for where he got the peas—the sack was lying on the floor, without anything to bide it—the top was turned down, I could just see the T on it.
Cross-examined by Mr. PAYNE. Q. Was it not just inside the door, against the counter? A. Yes—any person going by could see it—I did not see any ticket on the peas, but they were standing openly.
JOHN TRULOCK . I am in partnership with the prosecutor. Mr. Knight is a customer of ours—when we send him beans they are generally in his own sacks—on this occasion we sent him some in his own sacks—Hill was not a customer of ours—no man has a right to take anything of ours off our premises, without he has a note to show of it, written by our clerk—I had no regular carman but Ball—I occasionally send a man out with a single horse, but Ball had four horses.
MR. GREEN. I am clerk at Lambeth-street, Office. I took the depositions in this case—this is the Magistrate's signature to this deposition.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Are you quite sure this was taken in the presence of the Magistrate? A. No, it was said in a room adjoining the office—it was then read to the prisoner in the presence of the Magistrate—( read)—the prisoner Ball says, "All I have got to say is I never delivered a sack of corn to this man in my life, or any other sack; the man must be mistaken in the man that drove the wagon at the time, if it was
so; Hill says, "I never had any such sack from Mr. Trulock; I had other sacks, and this one came from one of my customers in Limehouse-field, in February last; about five weeks ago I took a sack of barley-field, of my shop, and I emptied these pass in to this sack of Mr. Trulock's; this sack never was dropped at my door; I wish God may strike me down dead if ever it was; it is very common for corn-dealers to have other people's sacks."
(The prisoner Hill received a good character.)
BALL— GUILTY . Aged 33.— Judgment Respited.
HILL— GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1952]

SARAH ELIZABETH DEANE . On Friday, the 10th of June, about one o'clock in the day, the prisoner came to my house to inquire for lodgings—I am sure it was her—my watch hung on the mantel-piece—the prisoner requested some water, and I desired her to follow me to get it—after she was gone I missed the watch—this now produced is it.
HENRY MALLAM . I am in the service of a pawnbroker. On the 10th of Jane, this watch was pawned for 8 s., I believe by the prisoner, about five o'clock.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the watch, nor this witness; I never was in the pawnbroker's shop; I do not know where it is; I was at my aunt's from ten o'clock in the morning, till six in the evening, GUILTY .** Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-1953]
1953. ANN PICKETT and ROSE HAMILTON were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 1 purse, value 1 s.; 1 ring, value 8 s.; 4 sovereigns, and 5 shillings; the property of John Cook, from his person.
JOHN COOK . I live in Somers-place, Chelsea. On the 14th of June I was going home, and two females spoke to me—I then turned out of the Fulham-road, to go home, and one of the females walked by the side of me, and pressed against me, making propositions that I rejected—the other girl staid at the top of the turning, and said something—the one who was by my side said, "My sister calls me"—she went up to her, and they both went away—I then missed my purse, containing four sovereigns and five shillings—this is my purse, and my ring, which I much value.
Cross-examined by ME. CHARNOCK. Q. Where had you been pasting the evening? A. With a friend in Cook's-ground, Chelsea—I was sober—I do not know who the females were—one was a tall girl, and the other short—the short one followed me down, and pressed against me.
EDWARD DEVALL ( police-constable V 48.) I was on duty near Somerset-place, and saw the two prisoners pass me, running—in a few minutes the prosecutor came up, and said he had been robbed of a purse and money—in the course of the same night I went to the prisoners' room, and found them in bed—I saw this purse found on the bed in the room by Kimber.
HENRY KIMBER ( police-constable V 20.) I went to where the prisoners were—I told them they were charged on suspicion of stealing a purse, with four sovereigns and some silver, in the Fulham-road, Little
Chelsea—they denied having been there at all—I then saw Pickett hand the purse on the bed to the other side to which she had been sleeping.
PICKETT— GUILTY . Aged 20.
HAMILTON— GUILTY . Aged 21 Confined Three Months.

EDWARD KENNEDY . I was at work there—I went to dinner, and left my shovel there—it was gone when I returned—this is it.
THOMAS BROWN . About one o'clock that day the prisoner came and offered me this pick-axe and shovel—I bought them of him—be came again on the 14th, and from what I had heard I sent for a policeman,

1957. MARY PURDY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of June, 2 lb. 14 oz. weight of mutton, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Bottrill.
JOHN GULLARD . I am servant to Mr. Bottrill, butcher, of Rochester-row, Westminster. On the 17th of June, I saw the prisoner outside our shop—she took the mutton from the board, and my master took it away from her, when she had got about thirty yards—I saw him take it from
under her cloak—she said she was very sorry, and did not know how she came to do it.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had she bought any thing? A. Yes, one piece—I am sure she did not buy this piece—my mistress is not here, nor my master—the prisoner had paid my mistress for one piece, which came to sixpence—that piece was in her basket—I have been a witness in the Old Court before—I got more than 1 l. 4 s. by that—I did not say that I liked such jobs as these, to my knowledge—I cannot swear that I did not—the prisoner's daughter and sons were joking with me over the way in a public-house, and I said I did not know whether there was a God or not, as I bad never seen him—I said that, because I was asked the question—I was asked a great many questions by the whole of them—they had been trying to make me tipsy—I have known the prisoner to come to my master as a customer for four years—the lives in the neighbourhood—I saw my master yesterday rooming—he said he did not wish to prosecute the prisoner—he said yesterday that he did not want to have any thing to do with it.
WILLIAM CORNISH ( police-constable B 35.) I took the prisoner—she said she was very sorry for what she had done, and hoped the prosecutor would forgive her.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she not say she did not mean to steal it? A. She said she did not intend to steal any thing—I found 1 s. 6 d. on her.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18420613-1962]
1962. JOHN THURSTON was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying John Mullins.
EPHRAIM GWALTER . I am a baker, and live at Plaistow. On the 30th of May, I was outside the Coach and Horses there, and saw the deceased John Mullins, who I knew by sight, standing sparring through the window at the prisoner—he was not within reach—they were both in liquor—the deceased called the prisoner a b— thing, and said he was no use either for fighting or for work—the landlord came out, and ordered Mullins away—he refused to go until he had seen the prisoner—the landlord sent the prisoner out—the prisoner came out and said something to Mullins, who immediately stripped to his skin—the prisoner pulled off his jacket, and they sparred at each other—a man named Smith tried to make it up between them—at last they got to fighting in the road—they each had a second—the deceased struck the first blow—the prisoner struck him again underneath the left ear, and he fell—that was the only blow—in falling he struck himself at the back of the head, and was taken into the public-house yard insensible—he was carried to his brother's house on a man's back—I afterwards saw him dead.
Cross-examined by MR. PBENDERGAST. Q. It was entirely provoked by the deceased? A. It was, quite.
THOMAS FLOWER . I saw the quarrel in the public-house—the deceased began it—he provoked the prisoner very much indeed to fight, and used very bad language—he said he was no man at all—he was nothing but a thing, he could neither fight nor work, and he aggravated him very much—the deceased went out and stripped, and told the prisoner to come out—he said, "I do not want any thing to say to you"—he threw the window up, and said if he did not come out, he would pull him out by his snout —he asked him again, "Are you coming out?"—he said, "I do not want any thing to do with you"—he said, "If you do not come out I will fetch you out"—he looked over my left shoulder, and tried to reach hold of him—he threw both the windows open, and sparred at him—a boy pulled
them down—he threw them up again to catch hold of him to pull him out to fight—I did not go out of the house, and did not see the fight.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you known the prisoner? A. From a child—I never knew him to be quarrelsome.
CATHERINE MULLINS . The deceased was my husband—he left home at five o'clock the morning this happened—he was quite well then—I saw him next at half-past eight or nine at night, at his brother's—he was not insensible then—I saw a cut on his head—it was bleeding—he got his speech, and came to his senses on the Thursday following, but lost them again on Friday morning—he died last Wednesday week—his name was John—Mr. Beale the surgeon saw him.
WILLIAM WELLER . I am a watchman, at Plaistow. I was at the Coach and Horses there on the 3rd of May, and saw the prisoner and Mullins in the tap-room—Mullins told the prisoner to come out, and he would fight him—I afterwards saw them fighting in the road—I saw the prisoner strike one blow, and the deceased fall.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see whether the deceased struck too? A. No—I cannot tell—I think he was provoking the prisoner for about a quarter of an hour in various ways, using very had language to him—he stopped in the house some time after the deceased went out, and I saw the deceased push up the window, still provoking the prisoner—I have known the prisoner from a child, and never saw him in any row or trouble before—he is no fighter.
JOHN EVANS BEALE . I am a surgeon, and live at Plaistow. I saw the deceased first on Monday, the 6th of June—he had then been ill a week—he had symptoms of effusion of blood, or fluid on the brain, producing convulsive spasms and fits—he was sensible, but only at times—when I first saw him, I told him to put out his tongue, which he did—he appeared sensible then, but that morning he had two or three fits—he died on the 8th—I made a post mortem examination—on removing the skull cap, I found lying on the left hemisphere of the brain, from one to two ounces of coagulated blood, between the pia mater, and dura mater, which accounted for his death—he had a small wound at the back of his head, it was nearly well—there was a bit of plaster on it, which I took off—I did not apply anything further—there was not much concussion, and no external swelling—there were evidently several other blows on the back of his head—I apprehend that concussion was the cause of the extravasation of blood—I consider a blow or fall was the cause of it—I do not know that a blow under the left ear would produce it without a fall, but accompanied with a fall it would—such a blow and fall as has been described would be sufficient to account for what I saw, and the man being partially intoxicated.
Cross-examined. Q. You could not trace the extravasation to the wound at the back of the head? A. It led me to know that he had had a severe blow on his head—there was no fracture—the blow was between the hemispheres of the brain—the blood was all in one hemisphere—extravasation may occur without violence—it is more likely to occur during intoxication—I do not think he could have recovered under any circumstances—the extravasation had most likely existed for some days, but I cannot state positively how long.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Strongly recommended to mercy. — Confined One Week.

[trialAccount t18420613-1970]
1970. WILLIAM WEBB was indicted for stealing, on the l 7th of May, 1 pair of shoes, value 5 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; 2 watchguards, value 6 d.; 2 shillings, and 2 pence; the property of William Merritt.
WILLIAM MERRITT . I live at Deptford, the prisoner lodged in the same house for a few days, and slept in the same room with me. On Whit Tuesday morning I missed the things and money stated, from my pocket in the bed-room—they were my own—I have no father—the prisoner was the first that went out that morning—he knew I had some money, for he had seen me give change with it—this watch-chain now produced is mine—I know it by a join in the middle—I kept it in my pocket in the bed-room.
Prisoner. He bought that chain on Whit Sunday for a halfpenny, and a thrush-bird for 4 d.; he gave me the chain to look at; while I was looking at it the parlour bell rang, and he went away, leaving the chain with me. Witness. I did not, for I had it in my pocket next morning.
ROBERT HORNSBY . I am a constable at Seen, near Newbury, in Berkshire. On the 25th of May I apprehended the prisoner at Newbury, on another charge—in searching him I found in his pocket, among other things, this chain—I said, "Halloo! what do you call this?"—he said, "Oh, it is nothing but an old chain that I found on the road"—he said nothing about Merritt having given it to him.
Prisoner's Defence. I forgot that at the time. Three men lodged in the third floor besides me. I got up in the morning, and the landlady let me out. I had no bundle, or anything in my hand.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-1971]
1971. WILLIAM WEBB was again indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 2 chaise-springs, value 1 l. 15 s.; the goods of Thomas Gower, his master.
THOMAS GOWER . I am a coachmaker, at Greenwich. The prisoner was in my employ for about a week—I had a pair of chaise springs in my shop—the prisoner said he knew a person at Woolwich, where he had been at work, that wanted a pair of springs, and he repeatedly asked me, in the course of the week, if I would dispose of them to his friend—I said they were disposed of to another person, which they were—but as the persons did not come for them, and thinking that the sale of these would enable me to make a new pair, on Whit Monday, the 16th of May, I trusted the prisoner with them to take to Woolwich and show his friend—he said he did not know the man's name—the prisoner had repeatedly asked, in the coarse of the week, to buy them himself, which I refused, and also refused his taking them to Woolwich, till the Monday morning—they were worth 35 s.—I have a copy of the account which I gave him with the springs—I did not tell him what to do if the man did not pay for them—he took them from me, in the full confidence that the man would take them at that price—it was to be a ready money transaction, of course.
Prisoner. I worked for Mr. Gower nine days and three-quarters, but could get no wages, and understanding these springs were for sale, I asked him if he would sell me them; he said a person had been about them; I asked him for money; he gave me 4 d. and 6 d. at a time, in the course of the week; on Saturday night he gave me half-a-crown, and said, if I would call in about an hour, he would give me some more; hearing that he had deceived so many of his men, I said, if he would give me another half-crown I would make shift till Monday; when I went on Monday he said, "I think you shall take these springs, and bring me back a few shillings, as I am very short this morning." Witness. I deny the whole of it—I had paid him his wages, all but a trifle—he only worked for me four days and a quarter—he was to have 18 s. a-week—he received 6 s. 4 d. in the course of the week, and 5 s. on Saturday night—when he came to work on Monday morning I gave him the springs to take to his friend at Woolwich—he said he should be home between nine and ten o'clock, and I never saw him till he was apprehended—I gave information to the police, and they put it in the "Hue and Cry."
ROBERT HORNSBY . I am a constable of Speen. On the 25th of May
I went to Reading after the prisoner, seeing his description in the"✗ and Cry"—I said, "I want you for a robbery at Mr. Gower's, at Greenwich, for a pair of coach-springs"—he said, "Oh, he can't hurt me for that, I bought the springs; in fact, he gave them to me to sell, and I sold them to a man on the road, in a horse and cart, but I don't know the man, nor where he lives."
Prisoner's Defence, Mr. Gower agreed to give me 27 s. a-week, and I only received 9 s. 6 d.; a man who lived with him heard us talk about my buying the springs, and that man told me he could not get his money; he said, “Mind, he is very deep, if you can't get your money buy the springs of him." I was up all Friday night, not being able to get money for a lodging; when I went on Monday morning, he said, "Halloo, young fellow, I thought I had lost you;" I said, "I thought you had no money for me;" he said, "Oh, I can always squeeze out some for you;" and afterwards he said he had none, and gave me 8 d. The Magistrate said he believed my statement was true, as he had been there before, trying to cheat his men, who had summoned him for their wages, and put him in the court for it. He told me he had a boy coming to work for him; and when the boy came, he said, "Oh, I am not going to work for him; they tell me I shall never get any money." He had no money to give me on the Monday morning, and he said if I had a friend to buy the springs, I should have them. I met a man on the road, and he said, "Those springs would look well under my cart;" I said, "You can have them," and I sold them. I was going home to get work where I served my time. You could buy such springs for 30 s. anywhere; 1 l. 15 s. is due to me.
THOMAS GOWER re-examined. All I can make out due to him 1 s. 5 d.—he told me himself that he only worked four days and a quarter—I employed several men out of charity last winter, on condition that if I could not pay them all their wages, they would give me time, and work till the spring—they then wanted me to raise their wages, which I refused, and they summoned me—some I have paid, and some not—I suppose that is what the prisoner alludes to—I agreed to give him 18 s. a-week when I could put him on work—I kept him a fortnight or three weeks at bay before I put him on—I gave him the springs to dispose of for me—he said he was sure the party would buy them, and I put the lowest price I could on them—he was not sent with them to hawk about—he said the person he was going to sell them to was a potato-merchant, in Pole-street, Woolwich, which is three miles and a half from ray house—I took him by the hand, under the impression, from the respectable shops he had worked in, that he was a good workman, and I said I would be a friend to him, and make it a constant place—I gave him 8 d. on the Friday, which was all the change I had in my pocket—he prejudiced the mind of the boy who came to work, because he wanted the place for a nephew of his own, and told me he had written to Bristol for him—I certainly never sold the prisoner the springs—he got them from me under the representation that there was a customer for them at Woolwich, and I authorized him to sell them to that customer—he asked me for money on the Monday morning, but I had no change—I had only a 5 l. draft in my pocket—there was no one present at the conversation between the prisoner and myself about the springs.
Prisoner. He put the springs on my shoulder; there was a pair of shackles on them; he said, "Take them off, they will be worth 5 s. to
me;" I got the wrench, and took off. Witness. I took them off myself—they would not be wanted with the springs, and they are always an additional charge.
GUILTY.* Aged 25.
[trialAccount t18420613-1972]
1972. FREDERICK THORNTON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 6 sovereigns and 10 shillings, the monies of Joseph Cole, his master.
JOSEPH COLE . I am a stationer, and live in London-street, Greenwich—the prisoner was in my employment upwards of two years—I had him from his parents. On Saturday afternoon, the 14th of May, I gave him six sovereigns and ten shillings, to go to town and purchase some Sunday newspapers at the different news-offices—he generally brought them by the railroad—he did not return—next Monday he was brought to my house by my son—I asked what he had done with the money—he said, "I have none"—he had 12 s. a-week—he always behaved well up to this time.
DANIEL MONYAHAN ( police-constable R 184.) I took the prisoner into custody in London-street, Greenwich, and brought him to the station—I found on him a silver watch, a gold ring, a sovereign, five shillings, a fourpenny-piece, and 7 d. in coppers.
MR. COLE re-examined. These do not quite account for the money—I understand he got some of his clothes out of pawn—he was bailed, and has surrendered to take his trial.
Prisoner. I am extremely sorry for what I have done, hut I had no felonious intent; I intended to make up the money in the course of the week, and pay my master. Witness. I understand that he belonged to a 6 l. money-club; they pay so much a-week or month, and have a draw. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1973]
1973. JOHN WRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of May, 561bs. weight of hay, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of William Morris, his master.
RICHARD MORRIS . I am the son of William Morris, of Lee, in Kent—the prisoner was in our service between two and three months, at 15 s. a week. On the 31st of May he went with a wagon to town—I saw the wagon go out of the yard with a quantity of hay in it, which he had no right to have—in consequence of suspicion, I followed him, and saw him deliver the hay at the Rose, just by New Cross-turnpike—he remained there some time, and then drove away, leaving the hay behind him—I have since examined that hay, and it is of the same quality as the hay on our premises—it was a truss—I followed and overtook him after he left the Rose, and asked what he had done with the hay he had in his wagon—he said he had left it at the Rose to bait his horses on his return from London—I asked what quantity he had left—he said a small parcel, a quantity that he could put under his arm—I asked if he had not left any more—he said no—I said, "I am sure you are telling me a story, you left more"—he denied it again—I said, "I am convinced that you left a truss, for I saw you deliver it"—he denied it—I had seen him put it out into a rack which they feed the horses out of, and a bundle of hay besides
—I gave the hay in charge of a policeman—the small bundle was quite sufficient for the horses, with the corn he had, independent of the truss—they could not have eaten the truss in the time they would be gone—they left our yard at four in the afternoon, and would return about twelve—they had three nose-bags full of corn, besides a bushel of corn, the bundle of hay, and the truss.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. He had the charge of three horses? A. Yes—I was in the yard when he started—he saw me there—he went away, about a hundred yards, and I called him back to put a quarter of oats into the wagon to leave at Deptford—I saw the oats put into the wagon, and then saw the truss of hay—it was distinctly to be perceived—I was on the landing of the granary, and the wagon was immediately underneath, but I firmly believe that he did not know that I saw it—when he got to the Rose, he got up into the wagon, and I suppose ordered the man to bring the rack round to the wagon—I was not near enough to hear him—I remained at some distance—I saw the hay put into the rack—the rack was then moved to the side of the house—it was not put under cover—nothing passed between the prisoner and any one else—I waited till the wagon drove off—there was no occasion for his stopping at the Rose on his return—I never knew that he stopped there—when I came up to the Rose, a policeman was there, and witnessed the whole of it—I cannot swear to the hay, but I can swear it was taken out of our yard.
COURT. Q. Have you compared it with the hay on your father's premises? A. Yes, next morning—I know it came out of one of the stacks in the stack-yard—we were cutting hay from that stack that same afternoon.
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable R 49.) On the 31st of May I received information, and watched the prisoner—I saw him at the Rose-he stopped the wagon, and called the ostler, who brought the rack—the prisoner got up into the wagon, and put down a small bundle of bay, and a truss—he then got down, untwisted the two hay-bands, and the horses eat some of the truss—he went inside with the ostler, came out in about ten minutes, and went on—the bundle of hay was removed by the ostler to the side of the house, and put into another crib—I took the prisoner into custody next morning—I asked if he knew what I wanted him for—he said he did not—I said it was for stealing a truss of hay from his master's premises—he said he did not, what hay he had taken, he meant to bait his horses with, when he came back from London—I said, "Did not you see me?"—he said, "No"—I said, "I saw you deliver a truss of hay to the ostler, besides a small bundle"—he said he did not—the weight of the hay I took possession of, including two halters, with which we tied it up instead of the two bands, was 541bs.—the three horses had only fed on it ten minutes.
Cross-examined. Q. When did the ostler take the bundle? A. When he called for the crib, the bundle and the truss were put out—the small bundle was removed by the under ostler, and the truss was put before the horses—the small bundle was put into a rack by the side of the house—where no horses were feeding—none of it was removed inside—I returned to the public-house in two minutes—I only went off the bridge to see that he had no hay in his wagon, and when I returned I found the prosecutor
demanding his hay—the ostler had removed the rack to the side of the house—I did not ask the ostler any questions—I apprehended the under ostler next day—the upper ostler was there, but he took no part in the proceeding—horses may eat a good deal of hay in a quarter of an hour.
RICHARD MORRIS re-examined. The wagon was going to Barclay's brewbouse for grains—it is six or seven miles from my house—we did not allow the men to take any hay at all—I should say three large horses might eat half a truss on their return from London—there was 10lbs. or 12lbs. of hay in the bundle, but they had considerably more than they could have eaten in their nose-bags, and corn besides, had they stopped out till eight or nine next morning—we had been robbed previous to this, and ever since then have refused the men having bay—it is unnecessary—a carter would sooner take corn than hay for his horses—I do not think I had spoken to the prisoner personally about it, as he was only two or three months in our service—my father hired him—he came from Mr. Egerton's service, with whom he lived five or six years; even if we did allow hay, this would be an unreasonable quantity—the horses had been in the stable baiting, three or four hours before they started—they would return between twelve and one in the night—when I first saw the hay in the wagon, I said nothing about it, because I was determined to see whether he made a property of it or not.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18420613-1974]
1974. JOHN THORNTON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 2 d., the goods of a man whose name is unknown, from his person.
RICHARD HILL ( police-constable T 118.) On the 17th of May, between eight and nine o'clock, I was on duty in Greenwich fair, and saw the prisoner attempt different persons' pockets—he went up to a gentleman behind Richardson's show, put his left hand into the gentleman's pocket, lift it, put his right hand in, drew the handkerchief out, and put it under his waistcoat—I took hold of him, and took the handkerchief from under his waistcoat—I went, and asked the gentleman if he had lost his handkerchief—he put his hand into his pocket, and said be had—I told him to come to the station with me—he was coming, but there was a great rush, and I lost him—he did not come to the station.
Prisoner. As I was coming through the crowd I took the handkerchief off my neck; it is my own; he said he took it from my jacket pocket, and now he says from under my waistcoat. Witness. I took it from under his waistcoat.
HENRY FOWLER ( police-constable E 111.) I saw the prisoner in Greenwich fair—he took hold of a gentleman's pocket, put his hand in, and took out this handkerchief—Hill laid hold of him, and took the handkerchief from him—I had noticed him before, following several gentlemen, apparently with that object—he took hold of two or three gentlemen's coats.
Prisoner. I was not five minutes in the fair. Witness. I saw him two hours before that.
Prisoner's Defence. It is my own handkerchief which I had on my neck; there is no mark on it.
RICHARD HILL re-examined. Q. Was it in the middle of the crowd this happened? A. No, just on the outside, by Richardson's show—I was
behind him, looking over his shoulder at the time he put his hand into the pocket—he said at the time it was a yellow handkerchief—I found nothing on him but an old purse—I believe he had a handkerchief on his neck—I will not be certain—I spoke to the person directly in the prisoner's presence—he had no money whatever—he had not the handkerchief in his possession an instant before I took him—as soon as he put it under his waistcoat I took him—he had no time to examine whether it was marked or not—it is not marked—it was produced before the Magistrate—the inquiry was made there whether it had a mark, and he said it was a yellow handkerchief of his own.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420613-1975]
1975. JAMES PROCTER was indicted for feloniously assaulting William Willcox, on the 2nd of June, and stabbing, cutting, and wounding him in and upon the left shoulder, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
WILLIAM WILLCOX . I am a waterman, and work on the water, at Putney. I live in Coltman's buildings, Greenwich. On Thursday, the 26th of June, I was in the tap-room of the Red Lion—the prisoner was in the room—I threw some ale out of a glass towards the fire—it was not intentionally done—I think a little of it touched the prisoner—I had seen him before that—I had no quarrel with him—I did not intend to throw it over him—he ran towards me, I turned my back to him, and perceived a knife cut me in the back—my brother said he had a knife in his hand—I did not see it taken from him—I was wounded with it—it has got better now—I did not go to the hospital—the injury was not sufficient.
Prisoner. He threw the beer in my face; I was intoxicated at the time. Witness. I believe some persons had been chaffing him before I came in—I should be very sorry to hurt him.
GEORGE CAMPBELL . I saw Willcox throw about a table spoonful of ale out of his glass—I think some of it went on the prisoner's cheek—he instantly rushed between the tables, and began to hit him with his hand—we thought he was playing—nobody thought he had a knife in his hand till his brother said, "My God, he has got a knife in his hand"—Curtis instantly seized him, and took the knife out of his fist as he was going to make a jab at him—he said to me, "I have done it; I will go any where with you; I have done it, and must suffer the laws of my country for it"—he was handed over to the police.
HENRY KEEBLE . I am a surgeon. I examined the prosecutor, and found two wounds on one shoulder, and another on the loin—they were about an inch deep—I think the knife must have gone obliquely, which would diminish the depth—neither of them were severe, nor attended with any serious consequences—it was in a muscular part of the loin, and not followed by any inflammation—the knife produced would make such a wound.
GEORGE GOOD ( police-constable R 2.) I apprehended the prisoner at the Red Lion—he appeared to have been drinking, and said he knew he had done it, and must suffer the laws of his country—I asked why he did it—he said he did not know that the prosecutor had done any thing to him to cause him to do so—a man named Curtis, I believe, gave me the knife produced—the prisoner said the man had never done him any hurt, and
he did not know why he did it—he is a Greenwich pensioner, and bears a very good character at the hospital.
Prisoner's Defence. My head is very bad; I have been under a surgeon at times; and when in liquor I lose myself entirely; I know nothing of taking the knife out of my pocket at all; it was like a dream to me; when I saw the deed was done it struck me with terror instantly. GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 62. —Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Confined One Year.

1976. JOHN GOLLICHER was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 4lbs. weight of leaden pipe, value 6 d., the goods of Sarah Shepherd, and fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, stating it not to be fixed; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES LAST ( police-constable R 76.) On the 20th of May, between five and six o'clock in the morning, I was on duty in London-street, Greenwich. I saw the prisoner in Shepherd's-court—I followed him into the back of the premises—he got into a privy, and there I found a piece, of lead by the side of him—I asked how he came to be wet about the head and face—he said, "I don't know any thing about it"—I took the lead, and compared it with some near a doorway of Shepherd's premises, and am certain it had formed part of it—I had seen that pipe safe within half-an-hour before.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me break it? A. No, I saw you go from the spot it was taken from—you begged of me hard to let you go, and said you would never do so any more.
ANTHONY SHEPHERD . I am the son of Sarah Shepherd, a widow, and the landlady of the premises in question; it is her freehold—it is in Church-passage, Greenwich, about a street and a half from where the prisoner was apprehended—the officer called on me—I went round the premises, and found that a portion of the pipe had been broken off—I cannot tell whether the prisoner worked on the premises, as the house is in the occupation of a man named Doughty, an umbrella-maker.
GEORGE WILSON . I am a policeman. I produce a piece of pipe from the tank, where it was cut off—I have compared it with the other—it appears to me to be part of the same.
JOSEPH LAST re-examined. It was fixed to the tank, which was at the side of the wall in the passage, outside the house—it was fixed to the building.
GEORGE WILSON . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from the Deputy Clerk of the Peace at Maidstone—(read)—I know the prisoner—I was present at his trial—he is the man described in the certificate.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.

—she said a gentleman had just given them to her to carry—she refused to go back to the shop, and I gave her into custody.

A. Between two and three yards—I am sure it was not above ten yards—when I first saw you moving away, you was about two yards from the shop—I went out directly.
JOHN STEVENS ( police-constable R 55.) The prisoner was given into custody fifteen or twenty yards from the shop—she had these trowsers under her arm, exposed, so that any one could see them—she said they were given her by a gentleman—she appeared to be in liquor, but pretended to be so more than she actually was.
Prisoner's Defence. A man gave me the trowsers; he told me to get myself a pint of beer, and come up the street; I cannot exactly tell the words, for I had been drinking.

1983. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 2 sovereigns, the monies of Thomas Page, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS PAGE . I am a pensioner, living in New-row, Deptford. On Wednesday, the 1st of June, I went to the Royal Charlotte—I had something to drink, and asked the prisoner to partake of a pot of porter—he drank with me—I had about two sovereigns and two shillings in my pocket, when I gave a sovereign to my wife I saw the prisoner wink to another man, and then I told him to leave me—he struck me, and I lost my senses.
JOHN HENRY EDWARDS . I was in the Two Brewers, on Wednesday evening, the 1st of June—I saw the prosecutor come in with a female and a little child—he called for a quart of ale—the prisoner came in—the prosecutor said, "Get out of my sight"—he gave him a glass of rum, and then the prisoner sat down by the prosecutor, and began to feel his pocket, and said, "You have got plenty of baccay, " I then saw him draw out something which looked like a piece of rag—the prosecutor gave a sovereign to pay for the pot of ale, and then the prisoner took a glass of ale, and dropped it, and broke the glass—the woman said, "You have broken the glass"—he said it was all through that d—d old rogue, and I said, "You have robbed the man," the prisoner up with his hand, and struck the prosecutor in the eye, and then I said, "You rascal, you have robbed him, and now you ill-use him"—the prisoner said, "I will go and pay for the glass," and then he went away, and a policeman was sent for.
Prisoner. Q. Why did not you take me when you saw me take the money? A. I did not know it was money.
KEZIAH DYER . I was at the public-house, and saw the prisoner take and break a glass, then he hit the prosecutor, and took from his pocket a kind of bag, and put it into his own—be hit me, and went out of the house.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not tell persons that you were so drunk you were not capable of seeing any thing? A. No.
JOSEPH GREEN ( police-constable R 107.) I told the prisoner I took him for robbing Page of two sovereigns—he said he could always get over such a little job as that.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . ( police-constable R 15.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Ten Years.

1987. ROBERT COULDERY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 1 purse, value 1 d.; 3 shillings, 1 sixpence, 4 pence, and 5 half-pence; the property of Samuel Hall, from his person.
SAMUEL HALL . I am a Greenwich pensioner. I was in the Infirmary and got leave to go out for a few hours, on the 19th of May—I went to the Red Lion—I had a pint of beer—I paid one shilling, and had sixpence and four penny-pieces in change—I put my beer down in the parlour, and went into the tap-room to light my pipe—I had then 3 s. 6 d. in silver, four penny-pieces, and 2 1/2 d., in a leather purse, in my waistcoat pocket—probably the string might be hanging out, I cannot say—I was not three minutes gone into the tap—I then returned and drank my beer up—I went out, and did not walk above three yards before I missed my purse—I do not know who took it—I am nearly blind.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you certain you had the purse when you went in? A. Yes—I cannot swear it might not have dropped out of my pocket, but I was not taking any thing out of my pocket—I saw no person near me—I went back to the Infirmary, and on the Sunday following a policeman and Abraham Young came to me.
ABRAHAM YOUNG . I am a dustman. I was at the Red Lion that day, and saw the prisoner take the money out of the prosecutor's waistcoat pocket—it was in a leather bag—I did not say any thing to him—there were two or three more persons with him—I do not know when I first gave information of this—he put it into his pocket—I knew two or three chaps that were there—I heard that one of them had been in Maidstone Goal—I did not see him give any of the money to any person there.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-1988]
1988. JOHN KERRISON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 1 pocket-book, value 1 s., the goods of a certain person whose name is unknown.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I was on duty in Greenwich fair, about four o'clock, on the 17th of May, and saw the prisoner in company with a boy about eleven years of age—I observed the prisoner cast his eye towards a gentleman's pocket, which drew my attention—I then saw him take the boy, who turned out to be his brother, round to the
gentleman's pocket—the boy turned, and said to the prisoner, "There is nothing there"—they then went on in the fair, from pocket to pocket, of nearly twenty gentlemen, and in some instances the prisoner was not satisfied with the boy trying, but he tried after him—they then went towards the College—there was a mob, and I saw the prisoner take the boy by his two shoulders, turn him round, and tell him to go on—the boy then put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, and took out a crust of bread—he looked up in his brother's face, and chucked it up—the little boy then turned to a gentleman, took a pocket-book out of his pocket, and drew it half out—he then dropped it, and ran off—I took the prisoner—I saw the pocket-book half way out of the pocket, in the boy's hand.
Cross-examined by MR. LOCKE. Q. How long have yon been in the police? A. Twelve years and six months—I am well known—I had been in the fair nearly the whole day—I had seen the prisoner for two hours—I was close to him and heard his words—he could see me—he did not know me—I was in plain clothes—I had not blackened my face—I did not know him—he always took the boy by the shoulder and told him to go on—I was so near that I touched him at times—I had a blouse on and a publican's apron—I have seen hundreds and thousands of thieves in aprons—I was close to the gentleman who had the pocket-book—they put their hands into twenty people's pockets—in some the persons moved away or looked at them—I heard them say several times, "Nothing"—I said they tried twenty pockets, but if I should say thirty I should speak under the mark—there were plenty of civilians saw them as well at myself—I did not call on any of them for aid—I believe a brother-officer took the prisoner's brother—I could not find the gentleman who had the book, because the prisoner made off and the gentleman moved away—I made no attempt to speak to him—the book went back into his pocket, and then I ran after them—it was a dark-coloured book—it appeared to me to be a black one—I could not see whether it had a clasp to it—it was six or eight inches long—I looked over his shoulder, and my hand was on his shoulder all the time—it was between five and six o'clock in the evening, and quite light—I do not know whether any one told the gentleman—I certainly was very much out of temper with the prisoner, to see him induce a child to do this—he said, "What do you mean to lay hold of me?"—I said, "I know you are a vagabond and a thief"—he might at once have denied that he had done anything wrong, but I cannot recollect everything he said, or that I said to him.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-sergeant R 122.) I was on duty there, in plain clothes—I saw the prisoner with a little boy—I saw the little boy take a crust of bread from a pocket, and then go to the gentleman, take his pocket-book half-way out, and when he saw he was perceived he dropped it and ran off—we ran after them, thinking the gentleman would follow us—we did not see the gentleman's face.
Cross-examined. Q. What induced you to suppose he would follow you? A. Seeing us make a rush after the prisoner—the gentleman might not have heard or felt anything—there was a great crowd—it was in the street leading to the College—a man was selling knives and other things there were more than a hundred persons all close by the gentleman—the prisoner was taken before the Magistrate, and admitted to bail—we brought another policeman before the Magistrate—he knew the prisoner's brother, but he did not know him.
(Francis Rowles; Thomas Melburn, a coachman; Charles Coring, a waiter at the Spurr-inn, in the Borough; and Jeffry Hammington, a french-polisher; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.

1994. JOHN CALLOWAY was indicted for stealing, on the 25th"th May, 50lbs. weight of rope, value 5 s., the goods of John Wilson.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Thomas Brocklebank and others.
JOSEPH JOHN LEWIS . I am a Thames-police inspector. [trialAccount t18420613-1995]
1995. JOHN EGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of June, 1 half-crown, the monies of Henry Summerlin, from his person.
HENRY SUMMERLIN . I am a labourer; I came from Bedfordshire. I met the prisoner at the Crown and Nelson Arms, at Woolwich—he began to talk about soldiering, and wanted me to be a soldier—I said I was not tall enough—I at last agreed to be a soldier—I went with him to the Prince of Wales, then to another place, which he said was a guardhouse,—I was to sleep there if I did not pay for another place, and I gave bin 3s. 6 d. sooner than remain in the guard-house all night—I thought I m enlisted for a soldier—he told me I had, and said I must pay the half-crown and the 1 s., or else go to the guard-house all night, I might do which I liked—I said I would pay him rather than go there—he told me if I did not he should put me into the guard-house, to stop there till twelve o'clock the next day, and have half a pound of bread, and one print of water—I should not have paid him the money if it had not been fort* threat of going to the guard-house—I thought he would have taken next there—when I enlisted, the prisoner gave me 1$., and I gave him half-a-crown and the shilling back again, in order to keep from the guard-house
—I have been to inquire at the barracks since, and they said I would not do, I was too short.
Prisoner. In about ten minutes after I had given him the shilling I asked him to give it back again, if he was sorry for what he had done—he said, "Will you enlist me for a soldier?"—I said, "Yes," and I gave him a shilling—he never gave me the half-crown nor shilling back again—if he pave me the half-crown, why did he not report me to the police that night? Witness. He told me, unless I gave him half-a-crown and a shilling, he would take me into a wood, where there was a guard-house, and let me lie there till twelve o'clock next day, and then he should bring me half-a-pound of bread and a pint of water—I do not know when I was to come out, I am sure—he did not take the money from me—I gave it to him—I told him I would rather pay the 3 s. 6 d. than sleep in the guard-house—he had a man with him who he said was his father.
ROBERT REEVE . I am waiter at the Prince of Wales, at Woolwich—I saw the prisoner and prosecutor—the prosecutor wanted to stay at my house till nine o'clock—the prisoner told him he should go then to the barracks—he dragged him to the bar—he made a disturbance—I put him out, and told him to go to the barracks, if the man want a soldier.
Prisoner. Q. Did you hear him refuse to come to the barracks with me? A. Yes—I told you to go and get the policeman.
CHARLES HIERONS ( police-constable R 228.) I saw the prosecutor and the prisoner on Sunday night, about a quarter to nine o clock, near the rope-yard rails, in a road which leads to a wood, or to the barracks—the prosecutor was following the prisoner, and trying to get something from him, but I did not notice it—on the next day he went to the barracks, and the commanding officer sent the prisoner to the station.
Prisoner. I am in the 73rd Regiment; it is my duty to enlist any man—I never received the half-crown or shilling of him; he refused going to the barracks with me; there was an old man with me, but I did not say he was my father; he gave me a drop of beer; I took it, and said, "Good health, father;" the prosecutor said he could not go till nine o'clock the next day. I said if I waited for him I should get put into the guard-house; that was what I said.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18420613-1996]
1996. THOMAS WATERS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of February, 2 beds, value 1 l. 6 s.; 2 bolsters, value 5 s.; 2 pillows, value 3 s.; 1 table, value 6 s.; 4 chairs, value 6 s.; 1 carpet, value 7 s. 6 d.; 1 blanket, value 6 s.; 1 counterpane, value 6 s. 6 d.; 1 clock, value 5 s.; 2 candlesticks, value 2 s. 6 d.; 3 saucepans, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 tea-pot, value 1 s. 6 d. 1 wash handstand, value 9 s.; 3 saucers, value 1 s.; 1 tender, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of tongs, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 poker, value 1 s.; 1 tea tray, value 3 s.; 1 teakettle, value 1 s. 6 d.; 3 cups, value 1 s.; and 6 pictures, value 9 s.; the goods of Harriet Holder.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
HARRIET HOLDER . I have known the prisoner twelve months—I first became acquainted with him at Woolwich—he spoke to me first as I was coming from a friend's house at Woolwich, he asked me where I was going, and walked along with me—I gave him my address before I left him that afternoon, and he promited to write to me—I was then in the service of
Mrs. Ewbank, of Black heath-terrace—I am now in the service of a lady in Black heath-park—about a fortnight after my first seeing the prisoner I met him again as I was out on an errand—he told me he had lost my address, which was the reason he had not written to me before—I met him repeatedly afterwards by his solicitation—he made proposals of marriage to me, in consequence of which I purchased some property of two persons named Moore and Jennings—the prisoner went with me to Moore's on the 10th of February, and I purchased a bed, bolster and pillows, blanket, table-cloth, carpet, counterpane, six knives and forks, and six silver teaspoons, which came to 3 l. 4 s.—the prisoner proposed taking the things to the room of a friend of his named Jennings, there to remain till we were married—we were to live there when married—he was to take the room when Jennings left it—I saw the things taken to Jennings' house, and put into Jennings' custody—the prisoner was with me—I also purchased a bed, bedstead, a clock, half a dozen pictures, four chairs, three saucepans, and a tea-kettle of Jennings for 3 l.—I paid 1 l. down on account that evening, and he was to come over to Blackheath for the rest—on the Sunday evening following, the 14th of February, he and Jennings came together—the prisoner sent a note in to me—I went out, and he said he had come for the remainder of the money for the furniture—I gave him 2 l. to pay Jennings for the furniture—I saw the prisoner again a week or nine days after, and have often met him since—about six weeks ago he said he had a furlough, that he was going into the country on account of some property that was left him by his aunt, and asked me to lend him 2 l. to go down—I said I could not lend him 2 l., I would lend him 1 l. 10 s., which I did—I did not see him afterwards till I saw him at the police-court—my suspicions were excited, and I went to Jennings last Monday fortnight to inquire about the things I had deposited there, and they were all gone—I never gave the prisoner any authority to part with any of the things—I have since seen the table at the police-court at Woolwich.
Prisoner. Q. By the virtue of your solemn oath did not you say as we were walking on Blackheath, that it would be an admirable thing if Jennings would take back the furniture again, our marriage being postponed? A. No—you never told me that Jennings had taken back the things and restored you the money—I never gave you permission to restore the things to Jennings and receive the money—I never went to the room after purchasing the things until I went and found they were gone—I left them under your care—you said you would take care of them for me—I did not know whether Jennings had left the room—we were to have the room when Jennings left it.
PATRICK JENNINGS . I am a soldier in the same regiment as the prisoner. I now live at Deal—at the time in question I lived in Woolwich. Some goods were brought to my room by the prisoner and prosecutrix, and placed in my custody to take care of—there was a bed, bolster, pillows, carpet, clock, and a good many things—I think the prosecutrix had the knives and forks in her own possession—she did not leave them with me—she purchased 3 l. worth of things from me, and paid me 1 l. on account that same evening—she said she would give me the remaining 2 l. in two days—I afterwards went with the prisoner to where she was in service to get the remainder of the money—he spoke to her privately—I saw her hand him some money, but I cannot tell what quantity—the prisoner afterwards told me that he would give me the 2 l. in the morning—he did not do so—in the morning he told me he would give it me in the evening
—I never got it—he came to me the following evening, and said the marriage was put off for six months, and it was her request that I should return the sovereign to him for her, which I did, and kept my things—I considered the contract at an end, and sold my own things next day—the things the prosecutrix had brought were taken away by the prisoner the day before I left—he said he thought it better to pawn them for six months for safe custody, the marriage being put off, for he had no friend to leave them with, and they would be safer in pawn than anywhere else—believing that, I gave them up to him—I saw him take away all the articles that had been brought there by the prosecutrix and himself—I have not seen them since—I saw the prisoner after he was taken, and he requested me to ask the woman I lodged with to go to the prosecutrix and offer her 2 l. to settle it, and not to prosecute.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not tell the prosecutrix at the time you sold her the furniture that you were to leave the room on the Tuesday? A. Not that I know of—I did leave the room the following week—I went with you to the pawnbroker's the week before, when you removed the things.
JOHN MOORE . I am a pawnbroker, at Woolwich. The prosecutrix purchased the articles she has enumerated of me some time since—I believe the prisoner was with her at the time—my young man sold the articles—some of them were afterwards brought back by Jennings, and the prisoner, and I took back of the prisoner the bed, carpet, counterpane, and blanket in exchange for other articles—I cannot exactly recollect for what, but believe it was the watch now produced—I sold this watch to the prisoner, I believe, but I have had so many transactions I will not swear that this is the watch—I knew the things again when they were returned—I did not express any surprise at their being returned so recently—he did not offer to pledge them, but sold them, and I gave him other articles in exchange.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant.) I took the prisoner into custody at Deal, on the 5th of June—he said he knew nothing about it—on our passage up in the steam-boat, he said he had had the 1 l. back from Jennings—I found this watch, three rings, a gold seal, and chain, a knife, four duplicates, and 1 l. 7 s. on him—the duplicates do not relate to these articles.
HARRIET HOLDER re-examined. The prisoner never told me that he intended to pawn or sell the articles—he never asked my leave to do so, nor ever told me that he had done so—I was quite in ignorance of it—I discovered it by going to Woolwich, where I bought the things—he did not come forward on the day appointed for the wedding, and I went to where Jennings lived, and found the things were gone—I traced a washing-stand to a pawnbroker's in Artillery-place which the prisoner had sold, and I went to Moore's—the things were disposed of on the 19th of February—I had purchased them on the 10th—he told me in February that he should put off the marriage for six months—he did not tell me so until after the things were bought—it was on the 12th of May that he borrowed the 30 s. of me—I knew nothing of the things being disposed of then—he never told me he had disposed of them—he said he would go into the country to get some property, and on the 1st of June the wedding was to take place—I supposed that the things were all safe in the prisoner's care at Jennings'—I did not know that Jennings was gone.
Prisoner. Q. Why did not Jennings at the time he sold you the furniture, tell you he was going? A. He did not say how soon he was
going, and I did not know that he was gone—he told me he was going, and that was the reason the things were cheaper—I thought if he did go the things were to remain in the room—we were to take the room on the 1st of June, the day we were to be married—the marriage was first appointed for February, I cannot say what day—it was before the 19th, and afterwards you said it was put off till the 1st of June.
Prisoner. She told me on the heath that if we kept the things in the room, we should of course have to pay house-rent during that time, and that was the reason she authorised me to sell them. Witness. I did not authorise him to sell them—we were to pay the rent of the room from the time we were married—we were to take the room of Jennings when he left—I did not know the prisoner was going—I do not know where he went when he said he was going on a furlough—I believe he remained with his company at Woolwich till they were called on to go to Deal.
JAMES PARRY re-examined. He went to Deal with his company a few days after.
Prisoner's Defence. There is property of mine in the hands of the police amounting to upwards of 12 l. which they refuse to give up, consequently I am left totally undefended; I am only charged with stealing property to the amount of 6 l., and out of that 3 l. worth of the things are recovered; the marriage was not put off solely on my account, it was her own desire; it was a mutual thing; she considered after the room was furnished that she had better remain in her service still; I was not aware of going to Deal until three days before I went.
HARRIET HOLDER re-examined. It was at his suggestion that the marriage was put off, and he told the Magistrate at Woolwich that he never intended to marry me—he told me he had put the banns up at Plumpstead church—I am twenty-three years of age.
JOHN MOORE re-examined. The things belonging to the prisoner are not worth more than 6 l.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined One Year.
