1997. MARY SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 1 thimble, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of William Mason.
HARRIET MASON . I am the wife of William Mason. On the 14th of June the prisoner came between four and five o'clock, and when she was gone I missed the thimble—no one had been there but her—I can swear to this thimble by the letters.
BENJAMIN HASLEDINE . I am a pawnbroker. I bought this thimble of the prisoner for 2 d.
Prisoner's Defence. I was fetching water, and kicked against this thimble.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Ten Days.
[trialAccount t18420613-1998]
1998. HANNAH OWEN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of June, 1 sovereign, the monies of Robert Cummings, from the person of Elizabeth Cummings.
ELIZABETH CUMMINGS . I am the daughter of Robert Cummings—I am eleven years old—we live at Deptford. On the 13th of June my mother gave me a sovereign, and a purse, to go to Mr. Sharp, the pawnbroker—he refused to take it, and said it was light—I put it into the purse, and had it in my hand—I was on the pawnbroker's steps, the prisoner tapped me on my
shoulder, and said she knew my mother very well indeed, and she had got some very pretty beads to give me—she took hold of my hand, and walked as far as Mr. Gardiner's—she then asked me to show her the sovereign—I showed it her in the purse—she had a bit of blue paper in her hand, and said she would wrap the sovereign up in it—she put it down my bosom—she then walked a little way, and left me—I returned home, and found there was nothing in the blue paper—there was nothing in the purse—it was a long purse, and had two little rings.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You never saw the prisoner before? A. No—it was about eight o'clock in the morning—I was about ten minutes in her company—I did not meet any body else—she went into a little shop and bought a farthing's worth of gooseberries—I know the shop, but I did not go in—I stood at the door—I saw her again about half-past ten the same night, at her own house—I was taken there by my mother and the policeman—I described her to my mother, and told her how she was dressed, when she was at the pawnbroker's.
COURT. Q. Are you sure she is the woman? A. Yes—she was not dressed the same in the evening, but I knew her.
MARTHA PARROT . I go to school with Elizabeth Cummings—I was near the pawnbroker's that day, and saw the prisoner speak to her—I am sure she is the woman.
Cross-examined. Q. How was she dressed? A. She had a dark lilac gown, and a white straw bonnet with black ribbon—she was not above a minute in my view—I saw her go to Mr. Roberts—she was in custody when I saw her again, and was dressed differently.
ELIZABETH ANN SCALES . I was at the pawnbroker's, and saw Elizabeth Cummings there—I saw a woman touch her on the shoulder when she was at the steps—the woman had a white straw bonnet on trimmed with black ribbons—she had this dress on—( looking at one.)
WILLIAM HENRY FISH . I am a shoemaker. I know the prisoner—I saw her go out from her own house that day, about nine o'clock—she then had this dark dress on—not the one the witness has seen.
ISABELLA CUMMINGS . I am the mother of Elizabeth Cummings—I gave her a sovereign—she came home and gave me information—I made inquiries, and went to the prisoner's house—I asked her if she knew my little girl—she said she never saw her before—I said, "You saw her this morning, between eight and nine o'clock, and took a sovereign from her" she exclaimed, "Good God! I have never seen the child."
CHARLES STEER ( police-constable R 198.) I took the prisoner—I said she must go to the station-house—she said she would not, but I took her—she said, "Good God! how did they find me out? how did they know I lived here?"—the prisoner's husband gave me these two dresses.
MR. DOANE called
SARAH PERRY . I live in High-street, Deptford. I know the prisoner—I saw her a little after nine o'clock that morning—she had a dark flowered gown on, with a light flower, and a Tuscan bonnet trimmed with black. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Two Months.

2002. MATILDA BATSFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 1 table-cloth, value 5 s.; also, on the 19th of May, 1 table cloth, value 6 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 4 s.; the goods of Ann Davis, her mistress; to both of which she pleaded

[trialAccount t18420613-2003]
2003. JAMES SANGER was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 1 bag, value 3 d.; and 150lbs. weight of sugar, value4 l. 15 s.; the goods of Thomas Pillow, in a barge on the navigable River Thames.
HENRY JACKSON . I live in Elizabeth-place, Bermondsey. I was on duty, as a watchman, at Stanton's-wharf, Tooley-street, on the morning of the 24th of May, and noticed two persons in a small old dredging-boat, alongside a barge, between thirty and forty feet from the wharf—I saw the boat shove off from the barge with a bag of sugar in her—I said nothing to them, but when they got out of sight of me, I went on board the barge, and alarmed the people, who then complained of losing something—I cannot speak to the prisoner being one of the persons in the boat.
Cross-examined by MR. LOCK. Q. How do you know it was a bag of sugar that they had in the boat? A. I know there were bags of sugar on board the barge, and this bag appeared like them—I did not see the sugar, only the bag—I was standing right opposite the barge—I do not think the people in the boat could see me, on account of the tide being lofty, and a brig was lying between us.
WILLIAM JOHN ARNOLD . I am a lighter man, in the employ of Mr. Thomas Pillow. On the 23rd of May some sugar of his was put on board a barge called the Grace, to be taken to the West India Docks—Jackson alarmed me on the rooming of the 24th, and I missed a bag of sugar from the starboard-quarter of the barge—I went ashore, and ran up along the water-side to Battle-bridge plying-place, and when I got down to the stairhead, I saw the prisoner shoving off in his boat—I went to Beale's wharf, and watched the prisoner to Hayes' wharf-tier—he made fast to the tier, and began to bale the water out of his boat—I borrowed a ship's-boat, went off to him, and all at once he shoved off his boat, and got up the side of a schooner next to him—I got up after him, collared him, brought him down, and found the bag of sugar in his boat, covered over with an old coat—I told him he must come with me—he begged and prayed of me to let him go—I said, "If you had got away with the bag of sugar, I should have had to pay for it"—he said if he had known I should have had to pay for it, he would not have taken it—it was the one we had lost from the barge—it is marked "S. and J. H."—the tally was on it, and I have one like it with me belonging to another bag—the bag itself was marked "A"—they were all marked alike.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not this what he said? "If I had known you would have to pay for it, I would not have taken it into my boat? " A. No, he said nothing about his boat—he said, "If I had known you would have to pay for it, I would not have taken it"—I am quite sure he did not say, "into my boat"—I said nothing to him before I came up to him—I saw him make his boat fast to a schooner about twenty-five or thirty feet from Hayes' wharf—that is about 800 or 400 yards from where our barge was moored—we cannot see Hayes' wharf from the barge—there is generally a tier of vessels all the way down—I did not shout out to him when I first saw him—I said nothing to him before I got hold of him—there were ninety bags of sugar on board the barge—I loaded and tallied them all myself, and have since counted them—I met a man coming up the plying-place at Battle-bridge, who I expect had just been put ashore—I did not say anything to him—he was not coming up the steps—he had come up the steps from the stair-head, and was coming up the plying-place, which is a passage—I cannot say exactly how he was dressed, for I was in a hurry—he had a South-western on, I believe—when I got down, the prisoner was shoving his boat off—he was about eight or ten feet away from the stairs—I did not shout out to him then—I walked round to Battle-bridge, which was about fifty yards, for a boat—I was not a minute gone, and when I came up to the prisoner his boat was made fast to the schooner—the prisoner had a dredging-net for coals in his boat—it was not above seven or eight minutes from the time Jackson first spoke to me, to my finding the prisoner alongside the schooner—I had about 150 yards to go—I know when I went down into the cabin, it was twenty minutes past three, and it was about four when we were at the Police-office.
JOHN JUDGE . I am an inspector of the Thames-police. I heard a cry of, "Police" on the river, about five minutes before four in the morning—I noticed a dredging-boat come down the river—I found the prisoner in that boat, with Arnold, and a person named Bailey—the prisoner was given into custody, charged with stealing the bag of sugar—I asked him how he came by that bag in the boat—he said he was coming down the river, and by London-bridge a man op shore called him, and said, "I
want to go down as far as Pickle Herring-stairs," that he rowed him till he came to this barge loaded with sugar—that the man said, "You had better go alongside this barge, I want to get a sample from the barge"—that the boat went alongside, and the man held on by the bonnet of the barge, lifted up the tarpaulin, and rolled the bag of sugar from the barge into the boat—that he then said, "Row me to Battle-bridge," which he did, and he saw no more of him—I found the same marks on the bag of sugar as are on the tally produced by Arnold.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he say that the man told him he would be back again in half an hour? A. No—he said he put the man on shore at Battle-bridge.
W. J. ARNOLD re-examined. I am positive this is one of the bags that was on board the barge.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-2004]
2004. RICHARD M'KNIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of May, at St. Saviour's, Southwark, in the dwelling-house of William Thomas Robinson, 1 coat, value 1 l. 5 s.; 2 pairs of trowsers, value 1 l;. 15 s.; 1 pair of braces, value 1 s. 6 d.; 3 waistcoats, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 yard of satin, value 15 s.; 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 seal, value 10 l.; 1 watch-key, value 5 s.; 1 knife, value 3 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of scissors, value 3 s. 6 d.; 3 razors, value 5 s.; 5 handkerchiefs, value 155.; and 4 pairs of stockings, value 8 s.; his goods:—8 spoons, value 4 l.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 1 l.; 1 ring, value 1 l.; 1 brooch, value 1 l.; 2 shawls, value 2 l.; 5 handkerchiefs, value 1 l.; 1 sheet, value 5 s.; 1 sovereign, 2 half-sovereign and 16 s.; the property of Phoebe Robinson.
MATTHEW M'GUIRE . I am a police-constable of Liverpool, and live in Hewit's-buildings, Great Homer-street, Liverpool.—On the 12th of May I apprehended the prisoner in Albion-street, Liverpool—I charged him with robbing his lodging in London—he said I might search, I should find nothing there—I desired him to get up and dress himself—I found a pair of stockings in his shoes, a piece of chamois leather in his pocket, and a glazier's diamond; also this silver watch under his head in the bed—he was in bed—I then went to his mother's, searched his box, and found a pair of braces, which I showed to Mr. Robinson, and he identified them—the prisoner was not there when I found them—he was sleeping about 200 or 300 yards from his mother's house—I have known his mother two or three years—I called the prisoner's attention to the braces being found there—he said they were his own, and afterwards, on the railway, he said Mr. Robinson could not swear to them; did I think anything could be done to him?—I cautioned him not to say anything to me, for whatever he did say would be produced in evidence against him.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Where did you find the braces? A. In a box which his mother stated to me to be his—these stockings appear to have been worn—they are very common stockings—he said the diamond belonged to his trade.
WILLIAM THOMAS ROBINSON . I am in the employ of Messrs. Barnes ironmongers, and live in Orange-street, Gravel-lane, Southwark—I rent the whole house—it is in the parish of St. Saviour's. The prisoner came to lodge with me on the 16th of April, and occupied a bed-room on the top floor—on the 8th of May I and my mother went out, leaving the prisoner at home alone—previous to going out I had asked him if he intended
to go out—he said he did not know that he was, and we went—my mother returned home first—when I came home the prisoner was gone, and I missed my watch from the top drawer of a chest of drawers, some teaspoons from a drawer which was left locked, some table-spoons, a mournin? ring, a brooch, a red silk shawl, a white silk shawl, some neck-handkerchiefs, three pocket-handkerchiefs, a linen sheet, some stockings, one sovereign, two half-sovereigns, and 16 s. odd—part of the things belonged to my mother, Phoebe Robinson—I also missed some waistcoats and a pair of kerseymere trowsers from the drawers, and another pair from a box in the room, with a brown Taglioni coat, a pair of blue braces, a knife, a pair of scissors, and some razors from a drawer in the room the prisoner occupied—the drawer which contained the plate was opened by a knife, which I found by it—the blade was broken in two—I have part of it—the officer found the other part—the property I lost amounts altogether to about 20 l. or 25 l.—I went down to Liverpool, and saw the prisoner in custody there—I recognized this piece of leather as the piece which had contained the spoons—I found this other piece of leather, and this paper, which had contained the spoons, on the prisoner's bed, after he was gone—I found the house safe when I came home.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you able to identify these stockings? A. Yes—I have seen them in my drawer before—I know them by this little blue mark on the top—I had noticed that on them previously—they are my mother's—they have only been washed once—I do not know that this is a common mark—it was on them when they were bought—this watch is not mine—I lost a watch, seal, and key—when I first saw this piece of leather at Liverpool it appeared then to have the impressions of spoons having been in it—it was used to wrap the spoons in—I know it by the shape—it is not exactly a square—I told the officer before I saw it that there was a piece of leather which contained the spoons—I have seen it for some yean, and had it in my hand—there is no particular mark by which I know it—I have no mark on these braces—I know them by the size, and the exact position in which the buckles are—they are exactly the; length, I wore them with the pair of trowsers to which they were attached when taken away—I can swear to them—this knife I found in my room—it had no business there—it belongs to my mother—this piece of paper, which I found in the prisoner's room, was taken out of the drawer where I had left it locked up, on the first-floor—the spoons were in that paper when it was lost, and were also enclosed in these pieces of leather—I do not claim this diamond—I left my house a little after six o'clock, and returned at a quarter to ten—the prisoner was then gone, and all the things—I gave information immediately—the prisoner was not indebted to me for rent or anything—he paid weekly—he had paid on the Saturday, and this occurred the next day—I went up into his room, and found the few things in his room gone—he had no watch of his own, and scarcely any clothes, merely a bundle—I have not found any of my property but this now produced.
PHOEBE ROBINSON . These stockings are mine—I left the house on the 8th of May, about ten minutes before six o'clock—my son was last in the house—I missed two shawls, four handkerchiefs, and a variety of other things, which I have not found—I can swear to this mark on the stockings—it is wove in—I have not a doubt of them—I went to my drawer for them, and they were gone—they have only been washed once.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you buy them? A. By the Town. hall—I bought them out of a bundle—I do not know whether the others were marked so—I only noticed those I purchased—I never bought a pair with that mark before—I have never mended them.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Fifteen Years.

2005. JAMES LLOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June 1 breast-pin, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of John Nolan, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN NOLAN . I am a mason, and live at Lambeth. About fire minutes before one o'clock, on the morning of the 6th of June, I was coming out of a public-house—the prisoner was in the house—as I passed through the persons who were collected there with him, the prisoner raised his left arm, and with his left hand pulled the pin out of my scarf—it was a large gold pin, with turquoise stones, worth about 2 l. 10 s.—I saw him pass something with his left hand to a female who stood on his right—she did not go away—I do not know what became of her—I went to the door, called a policeman, and about a second or so after I charged him with the robbery, the people of the house opened the door, and allowed the prisoner and three or four more to go away—I followed the prisoner, and gave him in charge—his companions went away without waiting to see what was to be done to him.
Prisoner. The landlord of the house was standing by at the time be accused me of taking the pin out of his bosom; I said to the landlord, "Did you see me do it?" he said," It is very false"—I never put my hand near him at all. Witness. He is the man, I am quite certain.
LAWRENCE DACY ( police-constable L 107.) I took the prisoner to the station, searched him, and found half-a-crown, a shilling, a sixpence, and three halfpence on him.
JURY. Q. Was the prisoner at all intoxicated? A. I could not perceive it exactly; he was under the influence of liquor, but was not drunk—the prosecutor was not in liquor; there was, no appearance of his baring been drinking.
JAMES BROOK . I am a policeman. I produce the certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the person described in the certificate. GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18420613-2006]
2006. MICHAEL JOSEPH LYONS and HENRY JONES were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of. Joseph Johnson, about the hour of four in the night of the 14th of May, at Bermondsey, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 gown, value 3 s.; 1 curtain, value 1 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 2 s. 6 d.; 2 scarfs, value 3 s; 1 night-gown, value 18 d.; 1 shawl, value 7 s; 1 1/2 yard of calico, value 1 s.; 1 bottle, value 6 d.; 4 medals, value 3 d.; 6 pieces of copper coin, value 2 d.; 1 half-crown, and 2 pence; his property.
LOUISA JOHNSON . I am the wife of Joseph Johnson, who is a linen-draper's shopman; our house is No. 5, Riley-street, in the parish of Bermondsey, I believe. On Wednesday morning last, between three and four by my clock, I was disturbed by the fall of a box in my room—I got up a little alter four, and found my room in confusion—most of the things were in the passage outside the door—the passage was strewed with trifling things.—the street-door
was open—it had been safe at ten at night—I missed a mahogany box off the table, but found it in the room—its contents were gone—I missed a dark shawl, which hung up behind the room-door, and half-a-crown and two pence off the mantel-piece—the things altogether are worth 10 s. or 12 s.—I have recovered them all.
Lyons. Q. Did you see see anybody in your house when you awoke? A. No—none of the persons in the house were up when I arose, and no person in the house, I believe, would wrong me of anything—I have got four lodgers—I am sure they did not take the bundle out and leave it any where—there was nobody awake in the house—all their doors were locked, and I awoke them and disturbed them.
WILLIAM NOAKES ( police-constable M 104.) I tried the doors in Riley-street, to see if they were safe that night—No. 5 was safe—about a quarter past three o'clock I saw the two prisoners together within about a hundred yards from No. 5, going in a direction from the house—I was near enough to hear them—I could not hear what they said, but I beard they were speaking—I saw them together again, about a quarter before four, by Bermondsey New-town, about two hundred yards off—I had missed them from about a quarter-past three till then—I apprehended Lyons about two hundred yards further off, in Great George-street—Jones made his escape—he walked at first, and then ran towards the railroad—I called out, and the night-sergeant followed him—on taking Lyons to the station I found this shawl wrapped round his loins, buttoned inside his trowsers; the piece of calico in his hat; this bottle and calico in his right-hand coat-pocket; these two silk scarfs, and this handkerchief, in the front part of his trowsers, concealed, and this gown in the seat of his smallclothes.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What time was it that you pushed the door to ascertain that it was safe? "A. Somewhere about two o'clock, I cannot say to half an hour—I cannot say whether it was a quarter before ox after—I saw the prisoners about a quarter-past three, and again about a quarter before four, or not quite so much.
Lyons. Q. Was I sober or tipsy? A. You appeared to have been drinking, but you were sober—in going to the station you told me you would show me what you had got—a pint of brandy was found on you in a bladder—I did not know you had anything more till I came to search you—when I took you to the station I did not know you had got anything belonging to the people in Riley-street—I did not tell you, going to the station, that you had these things—I said you had some things.
WILLIAM CASY . I am a police-sergeant. At the instance of Oakes I pursued Jones—I followed him about a quarter of a mile—he ran the whole way—I saw William Smith also in pursuit of him—I called him to assist—he joined in the pursuit.
WILLIAM SMITH . I am a policeman. I ran after Jones about thirty yards—he found I was close to him, and sat down on a step—I took him to the station, searched him, and found this gown wrapped round his loins, inside his trowsers; I also found on him a bed-curtain and this handkerchief in his left-hand jacket-pocket, half-a-crown and two penny-pieces and a few topper coins, a mould, and some other things belonging to the little boy, which were emptied out of the box.
MRS. JOHNSON re-examined. These things are all ray property, and what I lost from a small glass box—they were taken out of my bed-room while I was asleep—I do not lock my door of a night, on account of being deaf, I am so afraid of fire.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did you go to bed that night? A. About a quarter-past ten o'clock—my lodgers have keys to get in, but there was none out after I went to bed—I had not been into the lodgers' rooms just before I went to bed—I had shut the room-door when I went to bed—the street-door was locked.
Lyons's Defence, I had been at Foots ray, and coming home at night I went into a public-house, and met this young man. I stopped there till about half-past two, or very near three o'clock. I then left, and came towards home; Jones came part of the way with me, I being in liquor; when we came within about My yards of this place, Jones went behind a heap of rubbish for a certain purpose, he called me, and said," Look, here is a bundle." I went, he opened it, and it contained these things; he said, "It is a wrong time to walk through the streets with these, we had better put them out of sight some where; "so we put them up in this way; he said, "You had better come home to my place and sleep, as you are in liquor;" another thing was brought against us at the station; the sergeant found a blunderbuss under the rail-road, but a man came and owned it; now if I had picked that up, I should have been blamed for that also.
LYONS— GUILTY . Aged 22.
JONES- GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for ten years

2008. EDWARD BOXALL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of May, 22lbs. weight of lead, value 3 s.; the goods of Joseph Frederick Humphreys, being fixed to a certain building; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOSEPH FREDERICK HUMPHREYS . On the 6th of May, about two o'clock in the afternoon, I missed some of this lead—I had seen it safe about twelve the day before—the prisoner lived in a house adjoining the back of my premises—some lead was shown to me the same evening, which I believe to be mine—I was present when it was compared with what remained—it fitted exactly—it was cut off in little pieces.
SAMUEL WRIGHT ( police-constable P 172.) About half-past ten o'clock, on the 6th of May, I stopped the prisoner at Peckham, and took out of his breeches' pocket these two pieces of lead, and these other pieces from his jacket pocket—he was about 300 yards from the prosecutor's premises-I took him to the prosecutor's, and matched the lead the next morning to the roof of the skittle-ground—the remains of it were still fixed—it exactly fitted to what remained—I compared the prisoner's shoes with some foot-marks which I found in the prosecutor's garden—they corresponded exactly—they were about ten yards from the premises—part of the tips of the shoes were off and they were a good deal cocked on one side.
Prisoner. You said at Union-hall they did aot fit. Witness. I did not.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going home, about half-past nine o'clock it night; I saw a lump lying along-side the wall; I took it up and went to sell it, but could not; I had had no victuals all day; as for stealing, I did not.

2012. ADOLPHUS FREDERICK TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, 27 tassels, value 5 s.; 288 buttons, value 1 s. 6 d.; 11 caps, value 19 s.; 6 yards of printed cotton, value 2 s. 6 d.; 54 yards of ribbon, value 5 s.; and 3/4 of a yard of velvet, value 1 s.; the goods of William Muller and another, his masters.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM MULLER I am in partnership with Mr. Simpson—we are Japan hat and cap manufacturers, in Farnham-place, South wark—we have between seventy and eighty persons in our employ. The prisoner was foreman of the cap department, and had been so about seven months—he kept a shop of his own in the London-road—he dealt with us for Japan hats, but never for caps, or buttons, or velvet—on Sunday, the 15th of May, in consequence of suspicion, I marked a quantity of tassels, ribbons, and chene velvet, and packets of buttons, in the gross packets—I afterwards missed some packets of buttons, and twenty-four inches of two pieces of this velvet—here is a pattern of it—it is a cotton velvet, not very valuable—this piece was found at the prisoner's house, and this is the piece it was cut from—the prisoner was taken into custody on the 27th of May—I accompanied the officer to search his house, and found these three gross packets of buttons—I can swear to two of them, from having the mark I put on them for detection—the other packet I believe to be ours, from its being a Kind of button not generally used in caps, but we bought it as a job—we found
nine tassels, the whole of which belongs to us—there is a pencil mark a the button of each, which is covered by the silk (they were marked as Sunday, the 15th) and some ribbons, one of the ribbons has not ur mart on it—the only reason I have to suppose it is ours, is that we bought what we call four pieces, and we never used a whole piece—we used a portions of a piece, and one piece is gone—we never sell ribbons, except made up; nor tassels, but as forming part of a cap; nor valve, except made up—we found some caps—they are not marked, but from the particular quality of the cloth, and their appearance, I have no doubt they an ours—I believe all these caps have had our stamp taken. off then—it was a paper stamp stuck on with gum—when we took them, one of them had our stamp it, but before we left his premises his wife concealed it—I saw it, and called the attention of the inspector to it, but it was taken away—the stuff of these caps is particular—it was made by brother for shipment, but it was not ready when his other goods were, and we bought it of him.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. When the prisoner was first engaged I suppose you made some inquiries? A. We understood he had been living with Mr. Wilson, with our foreman, Mr. Hunt, had been living—we were satisfied with Mr. Hunt's representation, we had that confidence in him and we knew the prisoner was in business on opinion of his abilities—I do not know that he has the character of a first rate hand—the time he devoted to our services at first was from nine o'clock in the morning till one—we afterwards came to an arrangement for him to give us his time from nine till five o'clock, for which we paid him 30 s. a week—we proposed that he should give up his wholesale trade, a proposed he should give up his business entirely—I never did so—Mr. Simpon might have done so.
Q. Did you not propose that he should give up his business entirely, and devote the whole of his time to your business, and bind himself in as agreement not to enter into any other person's employ for six months? No—we proposed he should give up his wholesale business, and enter into an agreement of 50 l., not to enter not any other persons employ, but I do not think by my partner—the prisoner rather agreed to the proposal that he should give up his wholesale business, but the thing was never brought to a conclusion—it was only talked of—O swear he did not refuse to did it—the proposal was made two or three months ago—he carried on the same business as us—he sold the same sorts of hats and caps—I knew where he lived—it was within half a mile of or shop—I went to his shop once before—I think it was before he was in our employ—to the best of my recollection, I never was in his shop again, till we went to search his place—I think about thirty or forty men work on run premises, they are only hat makers—we employ a good many women—the prisoner employed some of the same women as employ a good many women—the prisoner employed some of the same women as we did—they worked at home whether for us or him—among them is in person named Mrs. Carpenter—since I gave the prisoner into custody, I have had some conversation with her—i selected the nine tassels, I found at the prisoner's house from about two
dozen caps—a person employed by the prisoner brought them In—they were it two bags—they were on the caps—I did not claim any of the caps—they were made by Mrs. Carpenter—I hare had conversation with her about caps—at the time I found these caps she was at work for us—two young girls in Mrs. Carpenter's employ brought home these two dozen caps—I do not know their names, or where they lived—they have not been in my employ, that I am aware of—I never saw them before—I had this conversation with Mrs. Carpenter on the Monday morning after—she told me the had made these Gaps for the prisoner, and a great many others—I do not think she told me she made three dozen at the tame time—I will not swear she did not—she said she had some caps to make for us at the same time, and she had put on three of our tassels in mistake for the prisoner's—she did not say nine—she is still in our service.
Q. Having ascertained this, why have you indicted him for stealing sine, when she had put three on in mistake? A. I had not ascertained that it the time of his commitment—I have preferred this indictment within the last three or four days—he was in the habit of cutting out at my premises—that was his principal work—I never knew of his bringing bit own materials to our shop to cut out—I have since understood that be has given his own materials to the workwomen who worked there—he has never bought any materials of us for cap-making—Mr. Sinpson is in the habit of selling in our shop sometime, and Hunt like wise, and they are all—Hunt keeps the day-book—it is his duty to enter in the books the goods he sells in the course of the day—Hunt has been in our service about fourteen months, and is so still-I have had no dispots with Hunt lately—I thought it necessary to call on him for an explanation of his conduct—he had the management of my petty cash up to about March—I have relieved him from that part "of his duty—I swear to the best of my belief about these caps—I saw these five the first time I went to the prisoner's shop—I looked at them, and said I believed they were ours—I left them behind me, because I did not know I had a right to take them—I took what I could swear to—I saw this piece of ribbon the first time I went—I examined it, and left it behind—there were some labels taken—they are about 1 s. 6 d. a thousand—I never borrowed any labels from the prisoner to my knowledge—Mr. Simpson is on a journey—he was not at home when I went to the prisoner's house—I bought this even velvet at Capper's wholesale warehouse, in Old change—they supply persons in our line—I have never seen any of this velvet it the trade, but what we have—it may be about three months since I bought it—these buttons are commonly used it that business—I went on a Friday to the prisoner's house—I found these things behind the counter, mixed up indiscriminately with other goods—Mrs. Taylor was in the shop—no one else—we found one cap with one of our tickets in—I handed it to the inspector, and said, "I will swear to that"—he laid it down, and we looked all about the shop afterwards, and could not find it—there was only a little boy there—Mrs. Taylor was seen to touch the parcel—I did not see her touch the cap.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did the prisoner's attorney come to you to subpcena some persons? A. Yes—I called the parties before him—I have brought all my books in obedience to his directions—these two gross of buttons have the mark I put on them on Sunday—I never sold them or
handed them to any one to make up—they are taken out fa one a two dozens as they are wanted—there were no other tassels besides these nine—his were a great deal worse.
ANN BALLARD . I am the wife of William Ballard, who was formerlys policeman, and live in Chapel-place. On Saturday the 21st of May, in co. sequence of an arrangement, I went to the prisoner's shop in the London road—I bought a cap there, and said I wished to have a baby's'-l said I wished to make a present to a little boy of a cap, to go out of doors, and wanted a very pretty one made"—the prisoner asked if I should like one of cheni velvet—he showed me a bit of ribbon of the colour and I ordered him to make one by Wednesday—I deposited 1 s. 6 d —be said he had not got one by him, but would have one made for me—on Wed nesday the 23rd I went to the shop and received this cap—( producing it)—I paid 3 s.—I did not see him the day I got the cap—I went the next day, and saw him, and said the cap was too large—I wanted a smaller one—he said he would make me one, and would take this back if it did not suit me—I said the child was small, I would keep it, and he might make me another of the same colour—he said he had not sufficient velvet—he bought but a small piece in the market, and if he had to buy more the cap might not be so cheap.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. You did not get the second cap? A No—I went to the shop because I was told to do so by my husband—I was by myself when he bid me—I did not see any one else-I had no communication with the prosecutor about this cap—I did not give the measure—I told the child's age—I had taken this cap home and tried it on—I did not return it because I thought it was cheap, and I could give it to a child—there was no other reason for my wanting another—my husband told me not to take it back—I live in an ordinary sized house-we keep baths.
JANE TOYNE . I live in Lemon-street, Gravel-lane, and am in the employ of Muller and Simpson. On Monday, the 23rd of May, I saw the prisoner at Mr. Muller's warehouse—he wished me to make a cap of the chain velvet—he intended purchasing a yard from Mr. Muller's—on the Wednesday he gave me the velvet—I made it up, and sent it to his house—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. How long have you worked for Muller and Simpson? A. About seventeen weeks—I work at home-I never saw the prisoner till I went to work for them—he was employed there cutting out—I do not know whether he brought his own materials from his shop and cut them out there—I once had a silk velvet cap of him at Mr. Muller's—he gave me the materials at Mr. Muller's warehouse to make it—he must have brought them from home—I once fetched a piece of cloth from the prisoner's to make a cap for the prosecutors—I never fetched any thing else—no one was present when he told me he should want me to make up a cap—I made no secret of it—I made it at my own house—I sent it to his own shop—I did not show it to any one.
EDWIN HUNT . I am warehouseman to Muller and Simpson. It is my business to make the entries in the day-book—if the prisoner had made any purchase of any velvet or any materials to be made up at our shop, it would be my duty to enter it in the book—he made no purchase of any velvet of me—Mr. Muller occasionally makes entries, and Mr.
Simpson—I never knew my master sell any velvet unprepared, nor any tassels or buttons.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A Some two or three years—I knew him in Mr. Wilson's service—I was also in Mr. Wilson's service—he has always borne the character of a respectable man, and been a first-rate man in his business—he was in Mr. Wilson's service when I went, and I left him there—he had the management of their cap business—he at that time carried on business for himself in the London-road—he worked on Muiler and Simpson's premises—I never recollect his bringing his own materials to make up—I remember some braid being fetched from his premises, which was made up for Muiler and Simpson, and we had some old hat-boxes of the prisoner—I have sold the prisoner an article we call" threshers," what the country farmer boys wear—I do not recollect any thing else—there was a cap ordered of me—I ordered it of Simpson and Muiler, and the person did not call for it—I could not afford to lose it, and the prisoner took it of me—I was examined at the police-office—I swear I never sold, the prisoner any thing else—he never supplied the prosecutors with any buttons—I know that he employed some of the same workwomen as the prosecutors did—I swear positively I have never seen him giving them work to do for himself on the prosecutors' premises—there is a boy named William Trimm in the prosecutors' employ—he might look out hats for the prisoner, not caps and velvet—I think the prosecutors bought the chain velvet at Manchester—I do not recollect whether they deal with any one in the Old Change—I do not know the value of the velvet—it is my duty to enter articles which I sell of the prosecutors—Messrs. Muiler and Simpson sometimes make entries of the articles they sell—the prosecutors are not in the habit of selling. materials to their workpeople—we have never done so—I told the prosecutors of the cap the prisoner ordered, on the day of his commitment—I told Mrs. Harris, the forewoman, I wanted a cap of a certain size, and she got it made for me—I paid the prosecutors for it—the prisoner made some allusion to a cap, and I said the only cap I sold him was the one I had entered—I entered it at the time I bought it, before I had it away from the premises, after I had ordered it—I left Mr. Wilson's employ about eighteen months ago—I had been in his service three years—I collected orders for him, and occasionally received cash—I left him suddenly—I was out of employ six months before I got to the prosecutors'—Mr. Gibbs, Mr. Wilson's partner, gave me a character to the prosecuton—I swear I referred to them for a character—I had a dispute with Mr. Wilson before I left, about money matters—he was my partner at that time—I swear that Mr. Gibbs was not partner with me, but he was with Mr. Wilson—I was in business in Castle-street, Southwark, as a hat-manufacturer, and Mr. Wilson was my partner there—"Edwin Hunt" was over the door—Mr. Wilson's name did not appear—his partnership with Mr. Gibbs was not made public, and mine with Mr. Wilson was not made public—I was in partnership with Wilson—our term was seven years—the business did not answer—Mr. Wilson got the money—there were deeds and an agreement—I left Mr. Wilson because he charged me with misappropriating his money—I am not aware of the amount—it might be 200 l.—I left him the next day—I had been two years in his employ, receiving no salary—I was his partner and his servant—I have not taken any proceedings against Mr. Wilson to enforce my partnership agreement—I at one
time kept the prosecutors' petty cash—I have discontinued keeping it for six weeks or two months—a young man keeps it now—I have not had any dispute with the prosecutors about the petty cash—there have been errors, but no misappropriation—I have had no dispute with them about those errors—they were satisfied with my explanation—there was a dispute about 8l. which I was short in my account—I kept the petty cash for months after that—I have 2 l. a week—I have never been charged with being drunk since I have been there—I have not got drunk on the premises.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you ever charged with dishonesty by the prosecutors? A No—I did not run away when Mr. Wilson charged me with misappropriating his monies.
ELEANOR JARVIS . I made caps for the prosecutors—I receive the materials for doing so before I make them—I made part of these caps which were found at the prisoner's, for the prosecutors—I made part of this cap—I got the materials from the warehouse, and for these three also. Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. How long have you worked for the prosecutors? A. About seventeen weeks—all that time the prisoner was there as foreman—I have made two caps for him at two different times—they were delivered at his premises—he delivered the materials to me to make them at the prosecutors' warehouse—a girl made the other part of these caps—I know I made them for the prosecutors by the work that is in them—the inside work is mine—I know it by the sewing of the lining—I have worked for other people besides—I have not made caps of this description for other people—they are of a different shape—I swear there is some of my work in this cap—the inside work of the top is mine, covered by the lining—it is called stitching in the top—I cannot see it-whether the work of other persons is like this or not I cannot tell.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is this the form of cap you received to make A Yes, my work in this cap is under the lining—here is my work-I put the peak on this cap—the work on it is such as to enable me to say it is mine—I made two caps for the prisoner on two occasions, and be gave me the materials at the prosecutor's warehouse, but they were quite different to these—I have a book in which the prisoner writes the goods which I am to be paid for making up—part of this book is in Mr. Taylor's hand writing—I find in it the making up of two of the caps I made for the prisoner on the 4th of April, and the two others, which I made on the 9th of May—the prisoner put "paid" to the book—none of them were made for the prosecutors—they were paid for at the prosecutors' warehouse by Mr. Mullins or Mr. Simpson.
JOHN MASON . I am police-inspector. On Friday, the 27th of May, I received charge of the prisoner on his leaving Mr. Mullins—I went and searched his shop in the London-road—I found there these ribbons, velvet slips, and parcels of buttons, in grosses—they had the marks on them at the time—also these tassels for caps, with the marks in pencil under them, and the four caps which have been identified by Jarvis—among the caps there was one with the prosecutors' label on—I put it in a paper parcel among some other things that he identified, at the corner of the shelf—I saw Mrs. Taylor put her hand on the parcel—I said, "Don't move the parcel "—some time after it was taken away altogether, and found at last under some things on the counter, but the cap with the label was gone.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. What time did you go? A. About half-past fife in the afternoon—the shop was open—I found the ribbons and buttons on a shelf, and the caps in different bags—the drawers were unlocked—two of these caps were in the window—I stated my business—Mrs. Taylor was agitated—she remained in the shop—I searched all her apartments—there were about forty dozen caps in the shop.'
NOT GUILTY .

MYRA JUDGE . I paid the prisoner 8 d. on the 22nd of May for his master.
MARGARET CHOURNE . I paid the prisoner 2 d on the 20th of May, and 7 d. on he 21st.

Prisoner's Defence. On Saturday night my master only paid me 4 s. in part of 8 s.—I took this because I wanted to get some things, instead of getting them on Monday—I thought I should have had the money. Witness. I passed my word for 12 s. for a pair of trowsers—he was to pay me 1 s. a-week—it passed eleven weeks, and then he paid me 6 s.—my wife gave him 2 s. more than was coming to him.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Month.

2015. JOHN MITCHELL was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of May, 81bs. weight of leather, value 20 s., the goods of John Waude, his master.
JOHN WAUDE . I am a coach-builder, living in Kent-road. The prisoner was in my service some years as coach-maker. About the 31st of May, I missed a quantity of leather—I saw this leather in Mr. Judkins' shop—It is the cutting of my skins—to the best of my belief it is mine—on Monday, the 13th of May, I had information from one of my men, and spoke to the prisoner about the leather—he said it was the first time he had committed any offence, and hoped I would forgive him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. These are cuttings? A. Yes, to make elbows, and other parts of seats—they are worth 15 s.—I merely institute this, for the sake of example to my men—I always had the highest opinion of the prisoner—he had an opportunity of taking more valuable property
JAMES HOLMES I am in the service of Mr. Judkins. I bought this leather of the prisoner at 1s. a 1b.—there is about 31b.—it is the Full value.
MICHAEL TOBY . I am a coach-trimmer. I have examined this leather, it belongs to the prosecutor—I cut it myself—I can swear to this piece—( looking at it.)
Cross-examined. Q. Was it you gave information about the prisoner A It was—I never stole any leather, or took any to make shoes of—I made a shoulder-strap of a piece of leather—this piece is of no value but to a coach-maker—my master does not permit us to take any, but it is a thing that is generally done—I have seen both of my shop-mates do it,
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.

2019. DENNIS COLEMAN and WILLIAM HOLDER were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 2 bushels of oats and chaff, value 4 s, the goods of Thomas Brocklebank and another, the master of Coleman.
JOHN EDMONDS HUBBARD . I am in the employ of Thomas Brocklebank, and another, timber-merchants, at Rotherhithe—Coleraan was in their service.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) On the 13th of May I was on duty at Millpond-bridge, between one and two o'clock in the day—I saw Mr. Brocklebank's timber-carriage drive up—Coleman, the driver, went and spoke to Holder the ostler, at the public-house—I then saw Holder go and take a bag three parts full of chaff and corn, from the timber-carriage—Coleman had just left him, and gone to the horses' heads—Holder took the bag about fifteen yards from the carriage, into a shed where he keeps his horses and corn—I waited till he came out, and then I asked him if that property belonged to him—he said yes—I said I did not believe it, and he must go with me to the station—I got him back to the carriage, and then he said Coleman was going to leave it till he called for it—he said he was going to put it into some cribs—I did not hear what Coleman said—there were four nose-bags of corn for the horses.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSQN. Q. Is there any statement about the nose-bags in your deposition A. I did not state that—the depositions were read, and we were all asked if we had anything to add—I did not lay one thing or another—there is a shed in a passage by the side of the public-house at Millpond-bridge, where Holder keeps his things as an ostler.
GEORGE POTTER ( police-constable M 39.) I was on duty on the 13th of May, near Millpond-bridge, and I saw the carriage stop at the Queen Charlotte—I saw Coleman go and speak to the ostler—the ostler then removed some horse-cloths off the sack, took the sack in his arms and went to the rear of the public-house, towards a place where he keeps corn—I followed him down—I then went back and apprehended Coleman, the carter—I said I wanted him to go with me?—he said, "For God's sake don't take us down for that, it is only a little corn and chaff"—I saw some nose-bags—they were all three-parts full.
JOHN EDMONDS HUBBARD re-examined. From what I have heard from one of our carmen, the quantity of food Coleman had altogether, was about sufficient to provide his horses for the journey he had to go, pro vided they had sufficient before they set out, but they ought to have emptied their nose-bags at Paddington—they were coming back.
Coleman's Defence. I went with the team—the man who was accustomed to go told me he had been detained at Paddington an hour or an hour and a half, and if I had been the nose-bags would have been emptied, but I did not stay a quarter of an hour; when I got back to the Queen Charlotte I said to the ostler, "Empty this stuff into the crib, and feed the horses"—I thought I would keep the nose-bags full.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-2020]
2020. GEORGE BUSH was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of June, 2 half-crowns, 2 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of John Smith.
JOHN SMITH . I live in Little Canterbury-place, Lambeth. On the 3rd of June the prisoner came to my house with a cask of wine and some furniture—he charged 20 s. for the carriage—we had a dispute about its being more than it ought to be—I paid him the 20 s., and said I should give him a
note to say that it was too much—he said he would take the things back—he threw the money on the table—I wrote the memorandum in his book, and during that time he took up the 20 s. and two half crowns, two shillings, and a sixpence besides, which laid on the table—there was no pretence for saying that was part of his moneys—he only demanded 20 s.—I was finishing a letter—there were letters on the table—I said to my servant, "Is the man gone?"—she looked through the window and said, "He is gone"—I am sure the two half-crowns and two shilling, and sixpence had been on the table—the money I gave the prisoner was a half-sovereign, a five-shilling-piece, and two half-crowns—that was a my left hand, and the other money on my right hand.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Why did not you stop him A. I had a number of papers on the table when he came in, I was writing for the evening post—as soon as he was gone I gathered up the papers, looked for the money, and it was gone—I missed my money about a minute and a half after he was gone—I sent my servant after him—there was no other man with him when he received the money—there was during part of the time—he refused to leave the goods unless I signed the book and paid him the 20 s.—I did not say I would not pay him more than 17 s. 3 d.—I said nothing about 17 s. 3 d.—he threw the money on the table himself, and said he would take back the goods—I afterwards sent my sister-in-law to Pick ford's—I did not desire her to say that she had no doubt that a mistake had occurred, by my having had a part of the money at the time in my hand, which I unthinkingly handed to the prisooer—I never desired her to say any such thing—I went to Pickford's the next morning—I bad an interview with the prisoner there—I did not ask whether 20 s. was the charge he was directed to make—I afterwards complained that 20 s. was more than he ought to have charged, but not in the first instance—I will not swear that I did at all.
COURT. Q. You must know whether you found that the 20 s. was a wrong charge? A. It was a wrong charge decidedly, which I intend to put them in the Court for—it is an overcharge—I do not mean to say it was a wrong charge on his part—I did not say to the prisoner, when I went to Pickford's the next morning, that unless he gave me the 7 s. 6 d. I would give him into custody—I said nothing of the kind—I said, "You took 7 s. 6 d. yesterday that was not your own"—he said, "I did no such thing"—I said, "Then I shall give you into custody"—f sent my sister-in law for a policeman, and the moment I did so some of the clerks there did their best to get him out of the place—they sent him to another part of the office, and I followed him—the prisoner told me he had only received the right money; so did the clerks—he did not say he would refund nothing—I swear he never said so—I do not recollect his saying he would not give me any thing back—I believe he did not.
ELIZABETH SMITH . I remember the prisoner coming with this parcel from Pickford's—I went down to open the door—he gave me a paper, and delivered the parcels—I gave it to my brother—they disputed about the price—he said he would pay him, but he would not sign the book—be wrote a note, and wished the prisoner to sign it—he said he had nothing to do with his notes, he would sign his own book—my brother said be would not sign it, he would pay him the money—he said, "I can't take the money, unless you sign my book"—he threw down the money that was given him—my brother said, "I will sign your book;" and while he
was writing, the prisoner cleared the table of all the money—there was 27 s. 6 d. on the table, I am sure of that, 17 s. 6 d. in silver and 10 s. in gold—it was a short distance apart—I cannot say how much was in one lot, and how much in the other—I was not at the table—no one was near the table but my brother.
Cross-examinedQ. Did you go that night to Mr. Pickford's? A. I did—I saw two gentlemen there—I said I wished to see the prisoner, in order that the mistake might be rectified—one of the gentlemen said the prisoner would not be on the wharf till the following morning, and if I would call in the morning the matter should be inquired into—when I went away I said, "I suppose we must trust to this man's honesty, as the money was not marked"—the gentleman did not say, "No, if we are satisfied he has received more than he ought, we will take care that the matter shall be rectified"—he said no such words—I went the next morning, accompanied by my brother, to the wharf—I again saw the persons I had seen over-night; I also saw the prisoner—he was not there at first; they fetched him—my brother did not threaten him that unless he gave him the 7 s 6 d. be would give him in charge—he said, "What money did you take away last night?"—he said," 1 l."—my brother said, "No more?"—he said, "No"—my brother turned to me and said, "Fetch a policeman"—I went and got one. MR. CLARKSON called
DAVID WOODCOCK . I am clerk to Messrs. Pickfords. I remember Elisabeth Smith coming to the wharf on the evening in question—she told me her brother had paid 27 s. instead of 20 s. by mistake, and she wished to see the prisoner, in order that she might arrange it with him—I said he was not there then, and told her at what time he would be there——she said," I suppose I must depend on the man"; honesty and recollection for the purpose of repaying the money"—I said, "No; if we are satisfied he has received more than he ought, I will take care the matter shall beset right"—I did not in any way endeavour to secrete the prisoner, or get him out of the way—the firm would not sanction any thing of the kind—he has been three years and a half in their employ, and is a man of honesty and merit, and has given satisfaction in every way.
NOT GUILTY .

2021. JAMES HAYES and EDWARD SCOTT were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 1 loaf of bread, value 4 d.; 1lb. weight of butter, value 1 s.; and 1/2 lb. weight of bacon, value 4 d.; the goods of William John Ward.
WILLIAM MARTIN . I am a lamp-lighter, and live in Spring-grove, Lambeth. At half-past three o'clock in the morning of the 2nd of June I saw the two prisoners at the end of Devonshire-road, Larkhall-lane, lurking about with two others—presently I saw Scott get over a fence, close by Mr. Ward's house—I gave information to the police—they went round one way, and I the other—the policeman got there first—I saw him take Scott, and I took Hayes, who had a bundle with him—he threw it down, I picked it up, and gave it to the policeman—Hayes was taken a quarter of a mile from where he threw down the bundle—I chased him, and lost sight of him about half a minute, in turning round a wall—there was no one else there, except the other two—I saw Scott throw a parcel over Mr. Field's garden.
Scott. Q. How far might I be from the house, when you saw me get over the fence, as you say? A. It might be about 150 yards-there were four of you together when at the end of Devonshire-road, when I put out my last lamp—you did not speak to me—I am sure I saw you throw a parcel over Mr. Field's garden—there are houses on both sides of the way.
ROBERT RATCLIFF . I am a policeman. From information I received I saw Hayes getting over a fence close by Mr. Ward's house, with this' bundle in his hand—there were four of them together—I saw Scott close under the fence—he had the bundle, which he took from his pocket, and flung away into the garden—Martin picked up the bundle, and gave it to me—it contained bacon, bread, and butter.
Hayes. Q. Was I not in the middle of the road? A. You were after wards, but I saw you get over the fence.
Scott. Q. When you took me, where was I standing? A. About 100 or 120 yards from the place—I collared you—I did not say, "If you take any thing out of your pocket I will knock the teeth down your throat," nothing of the kind—you were close to Mr. Field's market-garden when I took you.
MARY ANN PATTEN . I am servant to Mr. William John Ward. There was a loaf of bread, some butter, and some bacon in the safe in the yard the last thing at night, and the next morning they were gone—the brad and other tiling produced are like my master's.
Scott. Q. Are you positive that the bread and bacon belongs to your master? A. It resembles it—the safe was unlocked—the key was in the key-hole—all that was in the safe was taken out.
Hayes's Defence. I picked up the bundle as I was coming towards the house; I was not near the wall; I did not know whose or what it was; I was going up to two young men, to ask if it was theirs, but seeing Scott taken, I was frightened, ran away, and chucked the bundle away.
Scott's Defence, Is it possible for a man 400 yards off to identify another one? I am positive it is 400 yards from where Ratcliff says he saw me; I have lived in Clapham nearly six years; he said, "If you offer to take any thing from your pockets I will knock the teeth down your throat;" I never threw any thing from my pockets; there was no place for me to throw any thing away; there are houses on each side
ROBERT RATCLIFF re-examined. It was not 120 yards from where I saw him.
(Hayes received a good character.
HAYES - GUILTY . Aged 17.
SCOTT* - GUILTY Aged 21.
Confined Three Months.

Nothing—I saw Thurston's husband soon after I took her, and she was discharged because they could not find Whiteman—I made search after him, and gave information—I met him in Lower Deptford-road—his brother and I bad a few words—he came and struck me in the face—I did not make it up—he came afterwards, and put some beer into a pot I was drinking out of—I did not drink it—I put it down—his brother and I had some beer—the prisoner came up and abused me again, and I gave him in charge—I had known his brother seven or eight years—I have asked his brother where he lived—Thurston was abusing my wife, and I gave charge of her—I should have charged her before if I bad found her.
HANNAH WALTER . I am the prosecutor's daughter. RICHARD CLOSE and FREDERICK MADDOX were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of May, 1 copper boiler, value 5 l. and I copper arm of a still, value 1 l.; the goods of John Marks; and CHARLES BURNEY , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
ALFRED PHILLIPS . I am a carman, and live in Long-lane, Bennondsey. In consequence of directions from Mr. John Marks, I went to a person named Jones, in Rosemary-lane, and got a copper boiler—I put it into my stable—it was safe there about eleven o'clock on Thursday night. the 26th of May, and it was missed at ten minutes past seven' on the Friday morning—I know Maddox—Close keeps a horse and cart in our yard—I have since seen the arm of a boiler, and, to the best of my belief, it is part of the boiler we lost that night—it is the property of John Marks. o
JOHN WARD . On the morning of the 27th of May I was in the backroom at Mr. Burney's, talking to the child—this copper was put into the shop, but I cannot say by whom—Burney was not at home—I went and put the copper into the back-place—I then went out, and saw Close in a cart, driving off—there were several other carts in the street—Burney came in afterwards at the back-door.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. The policeman caught you with the copper on you? A. I was taking it to the back-place, to show Mrs. Burney, as there was no one in the place but her—I am a labourer, and corn or coal-porter—I never was in prison.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you work for Mr. isaacs? A. Yes—he is a marine-store dealer—I went to the Cherry Tree that morning, and saw Burney there, drinking with a policeman who was off duty—I told him I wanted him to come and load a cart of rags for me—he said he would.
GEORGE POTTER ( police-constable M 39) A few minutes after eight o'clock, on the morning of the 27th of May, I was going by Mr. Burney's door, promiscuously, and saw a chocolate-coloured cart—there was no one in it—I went into the shop—Burney met me, and I said, "What is up now?"—he said, "Nothing much"—I said, "What is that; is it all right?"—he said, "Ob, I will make it all right with you; no one saw it come in but you"—I said, "What is it?"—I went to the back part of the shop, and saw it was part of a copper still—I said, "You can't make it right with me; you must go to the station, and make it right at the station"—he said, "Well, if you mean that, you had better stop; the man will be back in half-an-hour; I have not paid him; he is coming back for the money"—I said, "Very well, I will stop"—I stopped five or ten minutes above half an hour—I then said it was useless waiting any longer, and I took Burney to the station—in the afternoon I took Ward, who was carrying the copper through the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. DO you live near Burney? A. Yes, within about 100 yards—I did not notice any person present but Barney's wife, when I had this conversation with him—I believe this it part of a still.
THOMAS WEST ( police-conskible M 249.) On the meming of the 27th, about twenty minutes past six o'clock, I was on duty, about half-a-mile from Phillips's—I met Maddox and Close going towards Burney's house, in a direction from Phillips's—and about fifteen-yards behind them I saw a chocolate-coloured cart, and a person was driving it who frequents the Kent-road—I should know him if I saw him.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you know the yard that Phillips speaks of? A. Yes—I have seen three or four carts about there—I know Maddox—I had not seen Close before.
THOMAS BRYANT . I am a general dealer. I lent Close a chocolate-coloured cart, on the 27th of May, to go to Epsom—he was to return it the next day—I found it again on Saturday morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you go to Epsom yourself? A. Yes—I saw Close in my cart, on this side of Ewell, which is fourteen miles from Cornhill—and I saw him at Epsom—he had his own horse in my cart—his horse is a slow one.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420613-2031]
2031. MARY JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of June, 1 razor, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Benjamin Fisher.
BENJAMIN FISHER . I am a hair-dresser, and live at Rotherhithe. The prisoner came to my shop, on the morning of the 15th of June, about a quarter before seven o'clock—when she was gone I missed this rasor.
WILLIAM MOBB ( police-constable M 273.) I was in the Jolly Waterman on that morning, about seven o'clock—the prisoner came in, walked
to the end of the counter, took something from her pocket, and put it under the beer-engine.
THOMAS WILEY ( police-constable M 25.) I took the prisoner—I looked under the engine, and found this razor.
Prisoner's Defence, I do not remember having it.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Three Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JULY 4 TH, 1842.
